---

## PART C — TEXTUAL AND CONTEXT QUESTIONS

### C1. Mark 7:19 "Thus He declared all foods clean" **DISPUTED** (text VERIFIED)

**1. The text.** The Greek editions on BibleHub read:

- SBLGNT 2010: ... ἐκπορεύεται; — καθαρίζων πάντα τὰ βρώματα. (question mark, then a dash)
- Westcott-Hort 1881: ... ἐκπορεύεται; καθαρίζων πάντα τὰ βρώματα.
- Nestle 1904 and Tischendorf 8th: ... ἐκπορεύεται, καθαρίζων πάντα τὰ βρώματα; (question mark after βρώματα)
- Byzantine (RP 2005), Greek Orthodox 1904, Stephanus TR 1550, Scrivener TR 1894: καθαρίζο**ν** (neuter)
- Codex Bezae (D): καθαρίζει (per Williams 2024)

Metzger, _Textual Commentary_: "The overwhelming weight of manuscript evidence supports the reading καθαρίζων." Scribes found it hard to construe and "attempted various corrections." Williams adds that the change ω → ο was common in pronunciation from the Roman period, so the neuter is likely secondary.

**2. Grammar.** καθαρίζων is a present active participle, nominative **masculine** singular. The nearest nominative masculine singular subject is the implied "he" of καὶ λέγει αὐτοῖς ("and he says to them," 7:18). If it goes with λέγει, the sense is "(he said this,) making all foods clean." This construal makes 7:19c the narrator's comment.

**3. Why the KJV reads "purging all meats."** The KJV translated the TR's **neuter** καθαρίζον. A neuter participle can stand in apposition to the whole clause "it goes out into the draught" (Williams, citing Smyth and the _Cambridge Grammar of Classical Greek_). The sense is then physiological: evacuation purges the food. Bengel (on TR): "not polluting, but purging, whilst the wholesome nutriment remains." Barnes and Gill read it the same way. Novatian (3rd century) apparently read it so ("purged in the privy by natural law"; Williams).

**4. Positions (named).**

- **Mark's editorial comment (Jesus as subject):**
  - Origen (_Comm. Matt._ 11.12), Gregory Thaumaturgus, Chrysostom (_Hom. Matt._ 51.4). Cited by Metzger and Williams.
  - F. Field (_Otium Norvicense_), the Speaker's Commentary, Weizsäcker (per Expositor's Greek Testament, A. B. Bruce, who favors this view); Vincent's _Word Studies_ ("not Christ's, but the Evangelist's").
  - Translations: ESV, NASB, NIV, CSB, NET, RSV/NRSV, BSB all print a parenthesis.
  - Paschen (1970) called 7:19c by near-consensus "an early gloss"; Kazen (2010) calls it "obviously a redactor's comment" (both quoted in Williams).
- **Within that camp, three readings of "all foods" (Williams's classification):**
  - (a) Mark rightly draws the end of the food laws: Räisänen, Borg, Loader, Svartvik, Joel Marcus (_Mark 1-8_, AB 27, 458: "explicit revocation of the OT kosher laws ascribed to Jesus by Mark"), Dunn (_Jesus Remembered_ 574), Meier (_A Marginal Jew_ 4).
  - (b) Mark wrongly draws that conclusion from Jesus' words.
  - (c) "All foods" means all _permitted_ foods, and the issue is hand-washing only. Michael Bird ("Did Jesus Declare All Foods Clean?", Substack) gives this as one option and states "Jesus here does not nullify the laws of unclean foods."
- **Other masculine construals:**
  - Ellicott takes the participle with the subject of "he saith" but reads it as Jesus' act ("in so saying, cleanseth all meats").
  - EGT reports that "most recent commentators" of its day took it loosely with ἀφεδρῶνα: evacuation purges.
- **Part of Jesus' own speech:** Logan Williams, "The Stomach Purifies All Foods: Jesus' Anatomical Argument in Mark 7.18-19," _NTS_ 70 (2024) 371-91 (SBL Achtemeier Award). He reads a nominative absolute: the person, specifically the stomach, purifies food from _ritual_ impurity, because Jewish sources treat excrement as incapable of impurity. Williams concludes the translation "Thus he declared all foods clean" "should be abandoned." He notes NA28/UBS5 punctuation puts the question mark after βρώματα.

**5. Parallel.** Matt 15:17 has εἰς ἀφεδρῶνα ἐκβάλλεται with no καθαρίζων clause. Matt 15:20 ends: τὸ δὲ ἀνίπτοις χερσὶν φαγεῖν οὐ κοινοῖ τὸν ἄνθρωπον "eating with unwashed hands does not defile a person" (VERIFIED).

**6. Bottom line for the devotional.** Quoting "(Thus He declared all foods clean)" from ESV/NASB/NIV/BSB is accurate to those translations and to the majority reading since Origen. Say it is **Mark's explanatory comment**, not Jesus' spoken words, and footnote that a recent minority reading (Williams 2024) disputes it. Do not claim the Greek "clearly" says "He declared."

### C2. 1 Corinthians 11:24 "broken for you" **VERIFIED: "broken" is not in the earliest manuscripts**

**Manuscript evidence** (UBS apparatus as posted on earlywritings.com; cross-checked with Meyer and Bengel's editors):

- **Omit (τὸ ὑπὲρ ὑμῶν alone):** 𝔓46 (c. AD 200), א* (Sinaiticus, first hand), A, B (Vaticanus), C*, 6, 33, 424c, 1739, vg^st, syr^pal, Origen, Cyprian, Athanasius (per Theodoret), Pelagius, Cyril, Fulgentius.
- **κλώμενον "broken":** א² (later corrector), C³, D², F, G, K, L, P, Ψ, 0150, 81, 88, 104, many minuscules, Byzantine majority, Lectionaries, it^b, d, e, f, g, syr^p, h, Gothic, Ambrosiaster, Basil, Chrysostom, Euthalius, Nestorius, Theodoret, John of Damascus.
- **θρυπτόμενον "broken in pieces":** D\* (Bezae, first hand), Theodoret.
- **διδόμενον "given"** (from Luke 22:19): several Old Latin, Vulgate (Clementine and Wordsworth-White), Sahidic and Bohairic Coptic, Ethiopic. Vulgate/Cyprian: _quod pro vobis tradetur_.

**Editions.** SBLGNT, Westcott-Hort, Nestle 1904, and Tischendorf omit κλώμενον. The Byzantine text, Greek Orthodox text, and both TR editions include it. Translations: BSB, ESV, NASB, NIV, CSB, NET, RSV/NRSV read "which is for you." KJV, NKJV, and WEB (Majority-text based) read "broken for you."

**Judgments.**

- Meyer: "κλώμενον is spurious." The word is a "mere supplement," though "very correct in point of interpretation."
- Expositor's Greek Testament: "an early gloss."
- Ellicott: "not supported by MS. evidence."
- Metzger's separate note on the added "Take, eat" ({A}) lists 𝔓46 א A B C\* D F G among the witnesses that lack it. That note covers "Take, eat," not κλώμενον.

**Plain answer.** A devotional **may not present "broken" as what Paul wrote in 1 Cor 11:24.** The earliest witnesses (𝔓46, א*, A, B, C*) lack it, and it is absent from all modern critical editions. Accurate options:

- Quote "This is my body, which is for you" (BSB/ESV/NIV).
- If you quote KJV/NKJV, label it and build no point on the word "broken."
- The _act_ of breaking is in the text: ἔκλασεν "he broke [it]" (1 Cor 11:24; Luke 22:19).

### C3. Genesis 3:15 and Jacob's name: the same "heel"? **VERIFIED same noun; interpretive link NOT FOUND in traditional sources**

- **Same noun: VERIFIED.** Gen 3:15 עָקֵב (N ms absolute) and Gen 25:26 בַּעֲקֵב (N ms construct) are both H6119. BDB lists both under "heel ... as object of attack." The LXX uses πτέρνα in both. The name יַעֲקֹב is explained from this noun in 25:26 (BDB).
- **Who draws the connection?**
  - The _REV Bible Commentary_ (Spirit & Truth Fellowship, an online non-academic commentary) on Gen 3:15: "A noteworthy subtheme ... is that the word 'heel' is part of the name of Jacob."
  - Popular articles: twopathways.org, "And You Shall Bruise His Jacob," which cites no scholar, rabbinic source, or church father; redeeminggod.com.
- **Where I checked and found no such link:** BibleHub's commentary sets on Gen 3:15, Gen 25:26, and Hos 12:3 (Ellicott, Barnes, Gill, Keil-Delitzsch, Pulpit, Cambridge, Matthew Henry, Poole, and others). Web searches for Waltke, Hamilton, Sailhamer, Wenham, and Alexander returned nothing. No patristic or rabbinic source surfaced. That is a limited search, not proof of absence.
- **Connections interpreters do draw from Jacob's heel-grasp:**
  - Hosea 12:3 (the prophet's own use).
  - Jer 9:4 (disputed pun, A7).
  - Rashi on Gen 25:26: Jacob held Esau "with justice" to hold him back, since Jacob was conceived first (Sefaria).
  - John 13:18 / Ps 41:9: the betrayer "lifted his heel" (πτέρναν); heel imagery, not a Jacob link.
- **Caution.** The roles do not map neatly. In Gen 3:15 the woman's seed _is struck on_ the heel. In Gen 25:26 Jacob _grasps_ his brother's heel.
- **Devotional guidance:** You may note that the same Hebrew word appears. Present any theological link as the writer's own reflection. Do **not** say "the rabbis / church fathers saw..."

### C4. Genesis 27: Jacob in Esau's garments **VERIFIED** (one DOWNGRADE on attribution)

**The text.**

- Gen 27:15: וַתִּקַּח רִבְקָה אֶת־בִּגְדֵי עֵשָׂו בְּנָהּ הַגָּדֹל הַחֲמֻדֹת אֲשֶׁר אִתָּהּ בַּבָּיִת וַתַּלְבֵּשׁ אֶת־יַעֲקֹב בְּנָהּ הַקָּטָן. "Rebekah took the garments (בִּגְדֵי, N mp construct) of Esau her older son, the choicest ones (הַחֲמֻדֹת), which were with her in the house, and clothed (וַתַּלְבֵּשׁ, Hiphil waw-consecutive imperfect 3fs) Jacob her younger son." LXX: τὴν στολὴν Ἠσαὺ ... τὴν καλήν "Esau's fine robe."
- Gen 27:16: she put the skins of the goat kids (עֹרֹת גְּדָיֵי הָעִזִּים) on his hands and the smooth part of his neck.
- Gen 27:27: וַיָּרַח אֶת־רֵיחַ בְּגָדָיו וַיְבָרְכֵהוּ וַיֹּאמֶר רְאֵה רֵיחַ בְּנִי כְּרֵיחַ שָׂדֶה אֲשֶׁר בֵּרְכוֹ יְהוָה "He smelled the smell of his garments and blessed him and said, 'See, the smell of my son is like the smell of a field that YHWH has blessed.'" LXX: ὠσφράνθη τὴν ὀσμὴν τῶν ἱματίων αὐτοῦ.

**Christian interpreters (exact citations, primary texts read).**

1. **Ambrose, _De Iacob et vita beata_ 2.2.9** (Latin, la.wikisource; FC 65). Ambrose reads the robe as the **Old Testament**. Rebekah, "a type of the Church," gave the younger son "the robe of the Old Testament, the prophetic and priestly robe, the royal robe of David ... and gave it to the Christian people." The goat kids point to Christ as food ("led as a sheep to the slaughter"). On the smell of the garments (27:27): _Et fortasse illud est, quia non operibus justificamur, sed fide; quoniam carnalis infirmitas operibus impedimento est, sed fidei claritas factorum obumbrat errorem, quae meretur veniam delictorum_ ("And perhaps this means that we are justified not by works but by faith, since the weakness of the flesh hinders works, but the brightness of faith, which merits pardon of sins, overshadows the error of deeds").
2. **Augustine, _City of God_ 16.37** (NPNF, newadvent.org). Jacob "put himself under his father's hands, having covered himself with kid-skins, **as if bearing the sins of others**." The guile is "a profound mystery of the truth." "The world like a field is filled with the odor of Christ's name." Here **Jacob typifies Christ** bearing others' sins. It is not a picture of the believer clothed in Christ.
3. **John Calvin, _Institutes_ 3.11.23** (Beveridge translation). "Ambrose appears to me to have most elegantly adverted to the blessing of Jacob as an illustration of this righteousness ... so we conceal ourselves under the precious purity of Christ, our first-born brother, that we may obtain an attestation of righteousness from the presence of God." Calvin then quotes Ambrose's sentence given above.
4. **John Calvin, _Commentary on Genesis_, 27:27.** "The allegory of Ambrose on this passage is not displeasing to me. Jacob, the younger brother, is blessed under the person of the elder; the garments which were borrowed from his brother breathe an odour grateful and pleasant to his father. In the same manner we are blessed, as Ambrose teaches, when, in the name of Christ, we enter the presence of our Heavenly Father: we receive from him the robe of righteousness, which, by its odour, procures his favor; in short, we are thus blessed when we are put in his place."

- **Luther:** his _Lectures on Genesis_ were not checked. UNVERIFIED; do not cite.

**DOWNGRADE — attribution.** "We hide under the purity of Christ, our firstborn brother" and "the robe of Christ's righteousness" are **Calvin's paraphrase** of Ambrose. They are not Ambrose's words. Ambrose's own allegory makes the robe the Old Testament Scriptures; his faith-not-works line concerns only the _smell_. Cite as "Calvin, following Ambrose."

**Jewish readings (Sefaria).**

- **Rashi on 27:15:** "THE COSTLY GARMENTS — the clean ones, as the Targum has it"; called "coveted" (חֲמֻדֹת) "because he had coveted them from Nimrod."
- **Rashi on 27:27:** "Surely there is no more offensive smell than that of washed goat-skins! But Scripture implicitly tells us that the perfume of the Garden of Eden entered the room with him."
- **Babylonian Talmud, Sanhedrin 37a** (R. Zeira): "Do not read 'his garments' [*begadav*]; rather, read: His traitors [*bogedav*], meaning that even traitors and sinners among the Jewish people have qualities 'as the smell of a field that the Lord has blessed.'"
- **Genesis Rabbah 65:16:** Rabban Shimon ben Gamliel says he never honored his father one hundredth as much as Esau honored his. The garments were kept with Rebekah because Esau did not trust his wives (Sefaria Midrash Rabbah, trans. Schreier).
- **Numbers Rabbah 4:8:** the garments God made for Adam were garments in which the firstborn performed service, passed down Adam → Seth → Methuselah → Noah → Shem → Abraham. **UNVERIFIED** whether this passage ties them explicitly to Gen 27:15; my reading did not confirm it.

### C5. Leviticus: date and authorship **DISPUTED** (positions stated)

**Traditional position.**

- The book presents itself as divine speech to Moses: Lev 1:1 וַיִּקְרָא אֶל־מֹשֶׁה וַיְדַבֵּר יְהוָה אֵלָיו מֵאֹהֶל מוֹעֵד "He called to Moses, and YHWH spoke to him from the Tent of Meeting" (VERIFIED). The "YHWH spoke to Moses" formula recurs (Lev 11:1).
- Jewish and Christian tradition held Mosaic authorship. Jesus attributes Torah commands to Moses in Mark 7:10: Μωϋσῆς γὰρ εἶπεν "For Moses said" (VERIFIED).
- Conservative commentators defend an early setting, e.g., Gordon Wenham, _The Book of Leviticus_ (NICOT, 1979), and Nobuyoshi Kiuchi, _Leviticus_ (Apollos, 2007). Both are cited in McIlvaine; their dating arguments were not read in this pass.

**Critical positions.**

- **Documentary assignment.** Leviticus belongs to the Priestly source (P), with chs. 17-26 set apart as the Holiness Code (H). BDB (1906) labels Lev 11 texts "(P)" and Lev 17-26 texts "(H)" (VERIFIED in the entries above).
- **Wellhausen** (_Prolegomena_, 1878): P reflects the post-exilic temple community, after 586 BC.
- **Yehezkel Kaufmann** (_The Religion of Israel_): P predates Deuteronomy's centralization and reflects pre-exilic Israel (TheTorah.com summary).
- **Jacob Milgrom** (Anchor Bible _Leviticus_, 3 vols.): P early, "probably in the milieu of Shiloh"; H about the time of Hezekiah; H supplements P.
- **Israel Knohl**, _The Sanctuary of Silence_ (Eisenbrauns): the Holiness School worked _after_ the Priestly Torah and edited it; H belongs to a late stage of the Pentateuch's redaction.
- **Avi Hurvitz**, "Evidence of Language in Dating the Priestly Code": P's technical vocabulary is classical, pre-exilic Hebrew. The linguistic dating method is itself debated (e.g., Robert Rezetko).

**Honest summary.** Tradition: Mosaic. Critical scholarship: priestly composition, dated anywhere from pre-exilic (Kaufmann, Milgrom, Hurvitz) to exilic or post-exilic (Wellhausen and his successors). The relative order of P and H is also disputed (Milgrom vs. Knohl). Many details above rest on secondary summaries (search results, TheTorah.com). Cite the scholars' names and positions, not page numbers.

### C6. Jerusalem Council requirements (Acts 15:20, 29) **VERIFIED text / DISPUTED rationale**

**What was required.**

- **Acts 15:20** (James): ἀπέχεσθαι τῶν ἀλισγημάτων τῶν εἰδώλων καὶ τῆς πορνείας καὶ [τοῦ] πνικτοῦ καὶ τοῦ αἵματος "to abstain from the pollutions of idols, sexual immorality, what is strangled, and blood."
- **Acts 15:29** (the letter): ἀπέχεσθαι εἰδωλοθύτων καὶ αἵματος καὶ πνικτῶν καὶ πορνείας, ἐξ ὧν διατηροῦντες ἑαυτοὺς εὖ πράξετε "abstain from things sacrificed to idols, blood, things strangled, and sexual immorality; if you keep yourselves from these, you will do well."
- Restated at Acts 21:25 (Wikipedia; not separately fetched).
- Rationale in the text, Acts 15:21: "Moses has been preached in every city from ancient times and is read in the synagogues every Sabbath."
- Circumcision was not required (15:19 "not trouble those Gentiles").

**Textual variants.**

- The article τοῦ before πνικτοῦ in 15:20 varies (NA27 brackets it; SBL and TR include it).
- Metzger rates πνικτῶν in 15:29 {B} and refers to "the omission" discussed at 15:20.
- The Western text (Codex Bezae) adds the negative Golden Rule: "whatever you would not have done to yourselves, do not do to another" (Wikipedia, "Council of Jerusalem," citing Metzger 1994). Search results report that it omits "strangled." Metzger's full note at 15:20 was not accessible: UNVERIFIED in detail.

**Main readings of why.**

1. **The laws for resident aliens in Lev 17-18.** The four items match rules binding the גֵּר "in your midst": idol sacrifice (17:8-9), blood (17:10-14), animals not properly drained (17:13-15), and forbidden unions (ch. 18). Richard Bauckham, "James and the Jerusalem Church," in _The Book of Acts in Its Palestinian Setting_ (BAFCS 4; Eerdmans, 1995) 415-80, keys this to the phrase "in the midst." Joseph Fitzmyer also proposes Lev 17-18 and rejects a Noahide basis (per Wikipedia).
2. **Noahide laws (Gen 9).** Some relate the decree to rabbinic Noahide commandments. Wikipedia: "modern scholars dispute the connection"; Fitzmyer rejects it.
3. **A practical concession for table fellowship** in mixed Jewish-Gentile churches. Dummelow: "The object of these prohibitions was to render social intercourse [possible]." Ellicott: "It was of the nature of a compromise." (Both snippets verified from BibleHub/bibliaplus listings; the full sentences were not read.)
4. **Ethical reading (Western text).** With "strangled" dropped and the Golden Rule added, the list becomes idolatry, fornication, and bloodshed.

**Link to the series.** Paul later handles food offered to idols as a matter of conscience and love (1 Cor 8; 10:25-29). Scholars debate how that fits the decree. I verified no named position here; state only that the question is debated.

### C7. Galatians 2:11-14 (Antioch) **VERIFIED text / DISPUTED specifics**

**What the text says.**

- 2:12: πρὸ τοῦ γὰρ ἐλθεῖν τινας ἀπὸ Ἰακώβου μετὰ τῶν ἐθνῶν **συνήσθιεν** "before certain men came from James, he **used to eat with** the Gentiles" (imperfect: habitual).
- ὅτε δὲ ἦλθον, **ὑπέστελλεν καὶ ἀφώριζεν ἑαυτόν**, φοβούμενος τοὺς ἐκ περιτομῆς "but when they came, he **began to draw back and separate** himself, fearing those of the circumcision" (imperfects).
- 2:13: the other Jews and Barnabas joined the "hypocrisy" (Wikipedia quotes the NIV).
- 2:14: οὐκ ὀρθοποδοῦσιν πρὸς τὴν ἀλήθειαν τοῦ εὐαγγελίου "they were not walking straight toward the truth of the gospel." Paul to Cephas "before all": Εἰ σὺ Ἰουδαῖος ὑπάρχων ἐθνικῶς καὶ οὐκ Ἰουδαϊκῶς ζῇς, πῶς τὰ ἔθνη **ἀναγκάζεις ἰουδαΐζειν**; "If you, a Jew, live like a Gentile and not like a Jew, how can you **compel** the Gentiles to Judaize?"

**What the text does not say.** It does not say what food was on the table.

**Scholarship (named; argument details UNVERIFIED unless noted).**

- James D. G. Dunn, "The Incident at Antioch (Gal. 2:11-18)," reprinted in _Jesus, Paul, and the Law_ (WJK, 1990). The chapter exists (verified); its argument was not read. Dunn elsewhere calls Peter a "bridge-man" between James and Paul (_The Canon Debate_, 2002, 577; verified via Wikipedia).
- E. P. Sanders, "Jewish Association with Gentiles and Galatians 2:11-14," in R. T. Fortna and B. R. Gaventa, eds., _The Conversation Continues_ (Abingdon, 1990) 170-88. Existence verified; argument not read.
- Common views, stated without attributing specifics: (a) Peter had been eating Gentile food, implied by ἐθνικῶς ζῇς; (b) the issue was the degree of Jewish observance at shared meals, or the association itself, rather than non-kosher food.

**Why it matters for Romans 14 (textual observations, VERIFIED).**

- **Antioch:** Peter's withdrawal _compelled_ (ἀναγκάζεις) Gentiles to adopt Jewish practice to keep fellowship. Paul treats this as a **gospel-truth** issue (2:14).
- **Romans 14:** no one is compelled. Each is "fully convinced in his own mind" (14:5). The strong must not despise, the weak must not judge (14:3). The strong limit their own practice for love's sake (14:15, 20-21). Food is not the kingdom (14:17).
- **The same Paul** says in both settings that food does not make one clean before God (Gal 2:14 context; Rom 14:14, 20). He resists food rules when they are imposed as a condition of belonging (Antioch). He protects scruples when they are held as conscience (Rome).

### C8. Hebrews 9:16-17: "will" or "covenant"? **DISPUTED**

**Translations (VERIFIED, BibleHub parallel).**

| Version | 9:16                                                                                                   | 9:17                                                                                                                         |
| ------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **BSB** | "In the case of a **will**, it is necessary to establish the death of the one who made it"             | "because a **will** does not take effect until the one who made it has died; it cannot be executed while he is still alive." |
| **KJV** | "For where a **testament** is, there must also of necessity be the death of the **testator**."         | "For a **testament** is of force after men are dead: otherwise it is of no strength at all while the testator liveth."       |
| **WEB** | "For where a **last will and testament** is, there must of necessity be the death of him who made it." | "For a **will** is in force where there has been death, for it is never in force while he who made it lives."                |

- Also "will": ESV, NIV, CSB, NET, RSV, NRSV, NLT.
- "Covenant": NASB (current, 1995, 1977), LSB, Berean Literal, Literal Standard Version, YLT ("covenant-victim").
- Heb 9:15 is "covenant" in all these versions.

**The debate** (named scholars from Jared Compton, "Once More: Diathēkē in Hebrews 9:16-17," Detroit Baptist Theological Seminary, 2025).

- **"Will" (testament):** Harold W. Attridge (_Hebrews_, Hermeneia 1989); F. F. Bruce (NICNT 1990); Craig R. Koester (Anchor Bible 2001); Thomas R. Schreiner (EBTC 2020); Douglas J. Moo (ZECNT 2024). Arguments: διαθήκη commonly meant a will in the Greco-Roman world; "inheritance" (9:15) and "the death of the one who made it" fit will language; the author makes a deliberate play on the double sense.
- **"Covenant":** John J. Hughes, "Hebrews IX 15ff. and Galatians III 15ff.: A Study in Covenant Practice and Procedure," _NovT_ 21 (1979) 27-96; Scott Hahn, "A Broken Covenant and the Curse-of-Death: A Study of Hebrews 9:15-22," _CBQ_ 66 (2004) 416-36, and _Kinship by Covenant_ (Yale, 2009); R. B. Jamieson, _Jesus' Death and Heavenly Offering in Hebrews_ (SNTSMS 172, 2019); Sigurd Grindheim (Pillar 2023); Robert J. Cara (Mentor 2024); Jared Compton. Arguments:
  1. διαθήκη means "covenant" in all its other uses in Hebrews.
  2. ἐπὶ νεκροῖς (literally "upon dead [bodies]," plural) is an odd way to describe a testator's death and fits covenant sacrifices.
  3. Wills in that period could take effect before death, which clashes with "never in force while he lives."
  4. The context (9:18-22) is the Sinai covenant ratified with blood (Exod 24).
  5. The "death" is the covenant-breaker's curse, borne substitutionarily.
- Thayer (lexicon, 19th century) lists Heb 9:16f under the "testament, will" sense.

**Devotional guidance.** The "inheritance through a death" line works with either reading: 9:15 explicitly joins death, redemption, and "the eternal inheritance." Do not claim "the Greek word means _will_ here" as settled. Say: "The Greek word can mean both covenant and will; many translations read _will_ in 9:16-17, while others argue the author still means covenant."

### C9. Simon of Cyrene **VERIFIED facts / tradition flagged**

**Verified from the texts.**

- **Mark 15:21:** καὶ ἀγγαρεύουσιν παράγοντά τινα Σίμωνα Κυρηναῖον ἐρχόμενον ἀπ᾽ ἀγροῦ, τὸν πατέρα Ἀλεξάνδρου καὶ Ῥούφου, ἵνα ἄρῃ τὸν σταυρὸν αὐτοῦ. "They **compel** (ἀγγαρεύουσιν, requisition) a passer-by, Simon a Cyrenian, coming in from the country, the father of Alexander and Rufus, to carry (ἄρῃ) his cross."
- **Matt 27:32:** Κυρηναῖον ὀνόματι Σίμωνα· τοῦτον ἠγγάρευσαν ἵνα ἄρῃ τὸν σταυρόν.
- **Luke 23:26:** ἐπιλαβόμενοι Σίμωνά τινα Κυρηναῖον ... ἐπέθηκαν αὐτῷ τὸν σταυρὸν φέρειν ὄπισθεν τοῦ Ἰησοῦ "seizing ... they laid the cross on him to carry behind Jesus."
- **John 19:17:** Jesus goes out βαστάζων ἑαυτῷ τὸν σταυρόν. Simon is not named.

**Cyrene.**

- A Greek city in Cyrenaica, in modern eastern Libya (Wikipedia, "Simon of Cyrene").
- Acts 2:10 lists "the parts of Libya around Cyrene" (τὰ μέρη τῆς Λιβύης τῆς κατὰ Κυρήνην) at Pentecost (VERIFIED).

**The Jewish community in Cyrene (primary sources VERIFIED).**

- Josephus, _Antiquities_ 14.7.2 (14.114-118), quoting Strabo: "There were four classes of men among those of Cyrene: that of citizens; that of husbandmen; the third of strangers; and the fourth of Jews."
- Josephus, _Against Apion_ 2.4 (2.44): Ptolemy son of Lagus, "desirous to secure the government of Cyrene, and the other cities of Libya ... sent a party of Jews to inhabit in them."
- Acts 6:9: "the synagogue called of the Freedmen, and of the **Cyrenians** (Κυρηναίων), and of the Alexandrians" in Jerusalem.
- Acts 11:20: men of Cyprus and **Cyrene** first preached to Greeks at Antioch.
- Acts 13:1: "Lucius of Cyrene" among the prophets and teachers at Antioch.
- The Wikipedia figure "100,000 Judean Jews settled [in Cyrene] during the reign of Ptolemy Soter" is **DOWNGRADED**. Josephus, as fetched, says only "a party of Jews." Do not print the number.

**Rufus and Romans 16:13.**

- Rom 16:13: ἀσπάσασθε Ῥοῦφον τὸν ἐκλεκτὸν ἐν κυρίῳ καὶ τὴν μητέρα αὐτοῦ καὶ ἐμοῦ "Greet Rufus, chosen in the Lord, and his mother, who has been a mother to me also" (VERIFIED).
- A link to Mark 15:21 is **possible but unproven.** Adela Yarbro Collins (_Mark_, Hermeneia 2007, 734, quoted on Wikipedia) says the sons "were known to the earliest audiences." Wikipedia notes Alexander and Rufus "were both common names."

**Archaeology.** A burial cave of Cyrenian Jews in the Kidron Valley, found 1941 (E. L. Sukenik), dated before AD 70. An ossuary there is inscribed "Alexander son of Simon." Published by N. Avigad, "A Depository of Inscribed Ossuaries in the Kidron Valley," _IEJ_ 12 (1962) 1-12. It "cannot ... be certain that this refers to the same person" (Wikipedia, citing Avigad 1962 and C. A. Evans, "Excavating Caiaphas, Pilate, and Simon of Cyrene," in _Jesus and Archaeology_, 2006). Identification unproven.

**Tradition, speculation, and heterodoxy (FLAG).**

- **Simon's ethnicity or skin color:** the Gospels do not say. Cyrene had Greek, Libyan, and Jewish residents (Josephus/Strabo). "Simon's name alone does not prove he was Jewish" (Wikipedia). Do not assert he was Black African, and do not assert he was Jewish, as fact.
- **Basilides** (per Irenaeus, _Against Heresies_ 1.24.4) taught that Simon was crucified in Jesus' place. This is a Gnostic, docetic claim.
- **Anne Catherine Emmerich** (19th-century visions): Simon was a pagan. Not historical evidence.
- **"Simeon called Niger" (Acts 13:1) = Simon of Cyrene:** a popular identification that the text does not make. No scholarly support was verified.
- **"Simon became a disciple":** inferred from Mark naming his sons. Not stated.

### C10. Genesis 33:10 and Peniel **VERIFIED**

**Wording.**

- Gen 33:10: כִּי עַל־כֵּן רָאִיתִי פָנֶיךָ כִּרְאֹת פְּנֵי אֱלֹהִים וַתִּרְצֵנִי "for therefore I have seen your face (N cp construct + 2ms) as one sees (Prep כְּ + Qal infinitive construct) the face of God (N cp construct + אֱלֹהִים), and you have received me favorably (Qal waw-consecutive imperfect 2ms + 1cs)."
- The verse begins with Jacob pressing Esau: "take my gift (מִנְחָתִי) from my hand." The next verse says "take my blessing (בִּרְכָתִי)" (see A9).
- BSB: "For indeed, I have seen your face, and it is like seeing the face of God, since you have received me favorably." KJV: "for therefore I have seen thy face, as though I had seen the face of God, and thou wast pleased with me."

**Link to Peniel (32:30).**

- **VERIFIED as a textual echo:** 32:30 רָאִיתִי אֱלֹהִים פָּנִים אֶל־פָּנִים "I have seen God face to face"; 33:10 רָאִיתִי ... פְּנֵי אֱלֹהִים "I have seen ... the face of God." Same verb (רָאָה, Qal perfect 1cs), same nouns.
- Commentators who draw the link:
  - Cambridge Bible for Schools (H. E. Ryle): "We can hardly doubt that this turn of compliment contains a side allusion to the name of the locality, Peniel. Cf. Genesis 32:30-31."
  - Pulpit Commentary reports Quarry: "as he had already seen the face of God and his life was preserved, so now he had seen the face of Esau, and the anticipated destruction had not been inflicted on him."
- Other readings:
  - Keil-Delitzsch: "In thy countenance I have been met with divine (heavenly) friendliness (cf. 1 Sam 29:9; 2 Sam 14:17)"; Jacob "must have discerned the work of God in the unexpected change in his brother's disposition."
  - Cambridge: also "an elaborate compliment" like 1 Sam 29:9 and 2 Sam 14:17, where David is compared to an angel of God.
  - Pulpit lists and rejects mere adulation (Tostatius) or calling Esau a god (Vatablus).
- **Setup in 32:20:** Jacob hoped to "cover his face with the gift ... see his face ... perhaps he will lift up my face."

**Jewish note on 33:4** (the embrace just before). The word וַיִּשָּׁקֵהוּ "and he kissed him" carries extraordinary dots in the Masoretic text (visible in BibleHub's Hebrew). Rashi, citing Sifre: "some explain the dotting as meaning that he did not kiss him with his whole heart, whereas R. Simeon the son of Johai said: Is it not well-known that Esau hated Jacob? But at that moment his pity was really aroused and he kissed him with his whole heart."

## PART D — ADDITIONAL CHECKS (11-14)

### D11. Genesis 32:32 and _gid hanasheh_ (the sciatic nerve) **VERIFIED** (one qualification)

**Hebrew (Gen 32:32 Eng = 32:33 Heb).**
עַל־כֵּן לֹא־יֹאכְלוּ בְנֵי־יִשְׂרָאֵל אֶת־גִּיד הַנָּשֶׁה אֲשֶׁר עַל־כַּף הַיָּרֵךְ עַד הַיּוֹם הַזֶּה כִּי נָגַע בְּכַף־יֶרֶךְ יַעֲקֹב בְּגִיד הַנָּשֶׁה
"Therefore to this day the sons of Israel do not eat the sinew of the _nasheh_ which is on the socket of the thigh, because he touched the socket of Jacob's thigh at the sinew of the _nasheh_."

| Word                                     | Data                                                                                                                                                                                                                       |
| ---------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| גִּיד gîḏ (geed), H1517                  | "sinew" (BDB: Job 10:11; Ezek 37:6, 8; Isa 48:4 "iron sinew")                                                                                                                                                              |
| הַנָּשֶׁה hannāšeh (hah-nah-SHEH), H5384 | BDB: "a vein (or nerve) in the thigh (**etymology dubious**) ... only גִּיד הַנָּשֶׁה Gen 32:33"; Arabic cognate = sciatic vein/nerve. Strong's links it to נָשָׁה "forget," "in the sense of failure." KJV "which shrank" |
| כַּף הַיָּרֵךְ                           | "socket/hollow of the thigh" (A11)                                                                                                                                                                                         |
| יֹאכְלוּ                                 | Qal imperfect 3mp "(they) do not eat"                                                                                                                                                                                      |
| LXX                                      | τὸ νεῦρον ὃ ἐνάρκησεν "the sinew that grew numb"                                                                                                                                                                           |

Chabad (Menachem Posner) glosses _gid hanasheh_ as "forgotten [sinew]." Strong's "forget" link and BDB's "dubious" etymology both appear above. Treat "forgotten" as a traditional explanation, not established etymology.

**Jewish law today.**

- **Mishnah Chullin 7:1** (William Davidson/Koren-Steinsaltz, Sefaria): the prohibition "applies both in Eretz Yisrael and outside ..., in the time of the Temple and not ..., with regard to non-sacred animals and ... sacrificial animals. And it applies to domesticated animals and to undomesticated animals, to the thigh of the right leg and to the thigh of the left leg. But it does not apply to a bird ... [which] has no spoon of the thigh. And the prohibition applies to a late-term animal fetus."
- **Mishnah Chullin 7:6:** it "applies to a kosher animal and does not apply to a non-kosher animal." R. Yehuda: it applies even to a non-kosher animal. He argues the sinew was forbidden to the sons of Jacob when unclean animals were still permitted to them. The Sages reply: "**The prohibition was stated in Sinai, but it was written in its place**" (in the Jacob story).
- **Practice (Chabad, "What Is Gid Hanasheh?"):** _nikkur_, removing the sciatic nerve and forbidden fats, "is labor-intensive and requires extensive training." In many diaspora communities the hindquarters are sold to non-kosher markets instead. Wikipedia ("Nikkur," secondary) adds that avoiding hindquarters is a practical custom, not a halakhic ban, and that _nikkur_ continued in many Ashkenazic communities into the 20th century.

**"Is this the only dietary rule in the Torah that comes directly out of a patriarch's story?"**

- **Yes, with one qualification.** _Sefer HaChinukh_ counts only three commandments in Genesis: #1 procreation (Gen 1:28), #2 circumcision (Gen 17:10), #3 the sciatic nerve (Gen 32:33). After #3 it says, "From here until the end of Genesis there are no commandments" (Sefaria, trans. Nataf). By that standard count, it is the **only food commandment in Genesis** and the only commandment of any kind grounded in a patriarch's narrative.
- **Qualification 1:** Genesis also contains a food prohibition given to **Noah** (not a patriarch of Israel): Gen 9:4 אַךְ־בָּשָׂר בְּנַפְשׁוֹ דָמוֹ לֹא תֹאכֵלוּ "only flesh with its life, its blood, you shall not eat" (VERIFIED).
- **Qualification 2:** Gen 32:32 is phrased as a custom ("the sons of Israel do not eat ... to this day"), not as a command. The Sages say it was commanded at Sinai and recorded here.
- **Wording to use:** "the only food law in the Torah whose reason is a story about one of the patriarchs."

### D12. Genesis 33:4 (LXX) and Luke 15:20: same Greek phrase? **VERIFIED: similar, not identical** (DOWNGRADE)

**Greek texts.**

- **LXX Gen 33:4** (Swete): καὶ προσέδραμεν Ἠσαὺ εἰς συνάντησιν αὐτῷ, καὶ περιλαβὼν αὐτὸν **ἐφίλησεν** καὶ **προσέπεσεν** ἐπὶ τὸν τράχηλον αὐτοῦ· καὶ ἔκλαυσαν ἀμφότεροι. "Esau ran to meet him, and embracing him he kissed him and fell upon his neck; and both wept."
- **Hebrew Gen 33:4:** וַיָּרָץ עֵשָׂו לִקְרָאתוֹ וַיְחַבְּקֵהוּ וַיִּפֹּל עַל־צַוָּארָו וַיִּשָּׁקֵהוּ וַיִּבְכּוּ "Esau ran (רוּץ) to meet him, embraced (חָבַק Piel) him, fell (נָפַל) on his neck, kissed (נָשַׁק) him, and they wept." The LXX puts the kiss _before_ the fall on the neck.
- **Luke 15:20:** καὶ **δραμὼν ἐπέπεσεν** ἐπὶ τὸν τράχηλον αὐτοῦ καὶ **κατεφίλησεν** αὐτόν "and running, he fell upon his neck and kissed him." (δραμών aorist active participle of τρέχω; ἐπέπεσεν aorist of ἐπιπίπτω; κατεφίλησεν aorist of καταφιλέω.)

**Comparison.**

- Shared: running, falling "upon his neck" (ἐπὶ τὸν τράχηλον αὐτοῦ), kissing.
- **Different verb forms:** προσέδραμεν/δραμών; προσέπεσεν/ἐπέπεσεν; ἐφίλησεν/κατεφίλησεν. Different order.
- The closest exact LXX wording to Luke's ἐπέπεσεν ἐπὶ τὸν τράχηλον αὐτοῦ is **Gen 46:29** (Joseph falls on Jacob's neck, same form). Gen 45:14 (Joseph and Benjamin) has ἐπιπεσὼν ἐπὶ τὸν τράχηλον. Acts 20:37 has ἐπιπεσόντες ἐπὶ τὸν τράχηλον τοῦ Παύλου κατεφίλουν αὐτόν (both verbs match Luke). All VERIFIED.

**DOWNGRADE — "Luke 15:20 uses the exact phrase of LXX Gen 33:4."** It uses the same _scene_ and the phrase "upon his neck," with different verb forms.

**Commentators who draw the link.**

- **Kenneth E. Bailey,** _Jacob and the Prodigal: How Jesus Retold Israel's Story_ (IVP, 2003). Reviewed by John J. Bombaro, _Modern Reformation_ (May/June 2004): Bailey identifies "fifty-one points of contact," "stimulated by N. T. Wright, who links the Jacob and Esau saga with Jesus' parable." A secondary summary says the triad of running, falling on the neck, and kissing appears only in Gen 33:4 and Luke 15:20. Given Gen 45:14, 46:29 and Acts 20:37, that claim needs care: the exact triad including _running_ is the distinctive feature.
- **Cambridge Bible for Schools** (F. W. Farrar, Luke 15:20): "kissed him ... Literally, 'kissed him warmly or closely,' Genesis 33:4."
- **Gill** (Luke 15:20) cites Gen 45:14 for "fell on his neck."
- _Thinking Faith_ (Jesuits in Britain), "The Transformation of Esau and the Parable of the Prodigal Son": Esau's embrace is described "in words which evoke the prodigal son."

### D13. Daniel 1:12 and Romans 14:2: same Greek word? **VERIFIED: NO** (DOWNGRADE)

| Text                                                              | Word                                                                                                                                             | Data                                                                                                                        |
| ----------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Hebrew Dan 1:12                                                   | מִן־הַזֵּרֹעִים "some of the _zeroim_"                                                                                                           | H2235, BDB "vegetable"; Strong's "something sown ... pulse"                                                                 |
| Hebrew Dan 1:16                                                   | זֵרְעֹנִים                                                                                                                                       | same root, BDB "id."                                                                                                        |
| **LXX Old Greek** Dan 1:12 (Swete)                                | δοθήτω ἡμῖν ἀπὸ τῶν **ὀσπρίων** τῆς γῆς "let us be given some **pulse** of the earth"; 1:16 ἀπὸ τῶν ὀσπρίων                                      | NETS (McLay): "some pulse from the earth"                                                                                   |
| **Theodotion** Dan 1:12 (ellopos; standard Greek Orthodox Daniel) | δότωσαν ἡμῖν ἀπὸ τῶν **σπερμάτων**, καὶ φαγώμεθα καὶ ὕδωρ πιώμεθα "let them give us some **seeds** (seed-food), and we will eat and drink water" | NETS: "let them give us some seed"                                                                                          |
| **Rom 14:2**                                                      | ὁ δὲ ἀσθενῶν **λάχανα** ἐσθίει                                                                                                                   | λάχανον G3001: Thayer "garden-herbs ... any potherb, vegetables" (Matt 13:32; Mark 4:32; Luke 11:42; Rom 14:2; LXX Gen 9:3) |

**Answer.** The Greek words differ: ὄσπρια (OG) and σπέρματα (Theodotion) in Daniel; λάχανα in Romans. There is **no verbal link** in Greek.

**Do scholars connect them?** Yes, as a **Jewish precedent for abstaining from meat to avoid defilement**, not as a word link.

- Barnes (Rom 14:2): Josephus' priests who "supported themselves with figs and nuts" (_Life_ 3), and Daniel who "lived on pulse and water, that he might not 'defile himself with the portion of the king's meat'; Daniel 1:8-16."
- Benson: "See Leviticus 17:10-14; Daniel 1:8."
- Matthew Poole: "see Daniel 1:8."
- A modern Romans commentary summary (biblicalscholarship.wordpress.com; author not named on the page) cites Dan 1:8, 10, 12, 16; Josephus _Life_ 3 §§13-14; Judith 12:1-2; Additions to Esther.
- **Josephus _Life_ 3 VERIFIED** (Whiston): priests sent to Rome under Felix "supported themselves with figs and nuts."

**Verified side note.** Thayer lists λάχανον at LXX Gen 9:3 (God gives Noah animals for food "as the green plants," λάχανα χόρτου).

**Guidance.** Say "like Daniel, some Jewish believers may have avoided meat they could not trust." Do not say "Paul uses Daniel's word."

### D14. John 19:36 ("not one of his bones will be broken") and "broken for you" **VERIFIED range**

**Lexical point (VERIFIED).**

- The 1 Cor 11:24 variant uses **κλώμενον** (from κλάω G2806, "to break (specially, of bread)"; 14 occurrences). It is the same verb as ἔκλασεν "he broke [the bread]" in that verse.
- John 19:36 uses **συντριβήσεται** (συντρίβω G4937, "crush, break, shatter"; future passive 3s): Ὀστοῦν οὐ συντριβήσεται αὐτοῦ.
- The sources are the Passover rule, LXX Exod 12:46 καὶ ὀστοῦν οὐ συντρίψετε ἀπ᾽ αὐτοῦ (also Num 9:12), and LXX Ps 34:20 Eng ἓν ἐξ αὐτῶν οὐ συντριβήσεται.
- John 19:33 uses a third verb for the soldiers breaking legs: οὐ κατέαξαν αὐτοῦ τὰ σκέλη (κατάγνυμι).
- So the two texts do not use the same Greek verb.

**Range of reconciliations (named).**

1. **Textual: no conflict, because "broken" is secondary.** Meyer ("κλώμενον is spurious"), Ellicott ("not supported by MS. evidence"), Expositor's Greek Testament ("an early gloss"), and all modern critical editions (see C2).
2. **The breaking belongs to the bread, as a symbol of violent death.** Meyer: "Christ's body was not, indeed, literally broken (John 19:33), but in His violent death our Lord sees that accomplished in His body which He had just done with the bread." The word "was not spoken by Jesus, only the thought was expressed in the action of breaking the bread."
3. **"Broken" = wounded and put to death, not bones broken.** Gill: "though a bone of him was not broken, but inasmuch as his skin and flesh were torn and broken by blows ... by thorns, nails, and spear; and body and soul were torn asunder ... by death; and death in Scripture is expressed by 'breaking'; see Jeremiah 19:11."
4. **The Eucharistic offering.** John Chrysostom, _Homily 24 on 1 Corinthians_ (on 10:16): "why adds he also, which we break? For although in the Eucharist one may see this done, yet on the cross not so, but the very contrary. For, A bone of Him, says one, shall not be broken. But that which He suffered not on the cross, this He suffers in the oblation for your sake, and submits to be broken, that he may fill all men."
5. **A scribal-motive theory (reported and rejected).** Meyer reports that Hofmann explained the _omission_ of "broken" in some manuscripts from John 19:36, as if scribes removed it to avoid a clash. Meyer rejects this ("nor will it do to explain the omission of the word by John 19:36 f.").
6. **Bengel (on the TR reading):** "broken" relates to the body "fitting ... for being eaten," "given" to the passion itself. Bengel's editors report he later judged κλώμενον the weaker reading.

**Devotional guidance.** Use "His body, given for you" (Luke 22:19 διδόμενον) or "which is for you" (1 Cor 11:24). If the imagery of breaking is wanted, attach it to the **bread He broke** (ἔκλασεν). Do not say "His body was broken" as a quotation, since John insists no bone was broken.

## PART E — MASTER LIST: DISPUTED AND DOWNGRADED ITEMS

**DOWNGRADE (do not print as fact):**

1. "Unclean = unhygienic" (A1): ritual category; hygiene is one theory only.
2. "Holy literally means 'cut off / set apart' by root etymology" (A3): usage yes, etymology unverified.
3. "sheqets = tamei" (A4): different categories in Lev 11 (Hawley, reported).
4. KJV "abomination" merges שֶׁקֶץ and תּוֹעֵבָה (A4).
5. "Jacob means deceiver" (A5): the text puns on "heel"; "supplanter" is Esau's barb; the name was likely theophoric originally (BDB).
6. "Hosea 12:3 uses aqev (noun)" (A6): it is the verb עָקַב.
7. "Barak means kneel, so blessing = kneeling" (A9): relation unproven.
8. "Struck his hip" (A11): Hebrew נָגַע "touched."
9. "Thigh = procreative power in Gen 32" (A11): BDB classes Gen 32 as the ordinary thigh sense.
10. "Israel means prince with God" (A12): KJV/Strong's etymology; the verb is "strive/persist."
11. HELPS "koinos always negative except Jude 3" (B1): false.
12. eleutheros "from 'go where one pleases'" (B4): uncertain etymology.
13. 1 Cor 8:9 "liberty" = eleutheria (B5): it is exousia, "right."
14. "The weak are spiritually immature" (B6): scruple about permitted things.
15. "Stumbling block = offending someone" (B7): occasion of sin against conscience.
16. "Co-heir = equal share" (B8): lexically "fellow-heir"; conditioned on suffering.
17. "Schoolmaster/teacher" (B12): paidagōgos was a supervising slave.
18. "Simon bastazō-ed the cross" (B13): Mark/Matt αἴρω, Luke φέρω; John's βαστάζω is of Jesus.
19. "Broken for you" as Paul's text (C2): absent from 𝔓46 א* A B C*; later reading.
20. "Tradition links Gen 3:15 heel to Jacob" (C3): only modern popular sources found.
21. "Ambrose said we hide under Christ our firstborn brother's robe" (C4): Calvin's paraphrase; Ambrose's robe = the Old Testament.
22. "100,000 Jews settled Cyrene" (C9): not in Josephus as fetched.
23. Simon as Black African / Jewish / later disciple / = Simeon Niger (C9): not in the text.
24. "Luke 15:20 uses the exact Greek of LXX Gen 33:4" (D12): same scene, different verb forms.
25. "Romans 14:2 uses Daniel's word" (D13): λάχανα vs ὄσπρια / σπέρματα.

**DISPUTED (present both sides):**

- Name meaning of Ya'akov (A5) and Yisrael (A12).
- Jer 9:4 pun on Jacob (A7): Ellicott/Barnes/Cambridge yes; Pulpit no.
- koinos vs akathartos technical distinction (B1): House vs 1 Macc 1:47 and Thayer.
- εἰς Χριστόν in Gal 3:24 (B12): Thayer vs Young.
- Col 1:15 "firstborn" (B14).
- Which temple curtain tore in Mark 15:38 (B10).
- Mark 7:19 construal (C1).
- Leviticus date (C5).
- Rationale of the Acts 15 decree (C6).
- What was eaten at Antioch (C7).
- Heb 9:16-17 will vs covenant (C8).
- Rufus = Rom 16:13; ossuary identification (C9).

**UNVERIFIED (do not cite without further checking):** TWOT/TDOT on barak and qadosh; Luther on Gen 27; Numbers Rabbah 4:8's explicit tie to Gen 27:15; Metzger's full note on the Western text of Acts 15:20; the specific arguments of Dunn (1990) and Sanders (1990); Young (1987), Hawley (2015), and House (1983) beyond their titles and summaries.

# 3. Dossier 02 — All Sides: Leviticus 11 (Clean and Unclean Foods) and Romans 14

Research agent: "all sides" dossier for a 7-day devotional series.
Compiled: 2026-09-12.
Brief: set out each position fairly and in its own terms. Focus on where traditions AGREE. Say clearly where they differ. Argue for none of them.

---

## 0. How to read this dossier

**Verification key**

- **[V]** Verified. The quote was fetched from the URL given and checked word for word against the fetched text.
- **[V-2nd]** Verified, but through a secondary source that quotes the author. The secondary source is named.
- **[S]** Summary only. No verbatim quote was checked. Treat as a description, not a quote.
- **[UNVERIFIED]** Could not be checked. Do not print as a quote without checking the book.

**Bible text.** Unless noted, Bible quotations are from the World English Bible (WEB, public domain), fetched from `https://bible-api.com/<ref>?translation=web`. The series may use a different translation. Check the wording against the translation used in print.

**Copyright.** Commentaries by Milgrom, Wenham, Douglas, Moo, Cranfield, Dunn, Kinzer, Rudolph, Nanos, Wright, Schreiner, the Chavel Ramban, and the Touger Mishneh Torah are under copyright. Quotes from them are kept short.

**Old PDFs.** Some quotes come from text pulled out of PDFs. Spacing and ligatures were normalized ("classiﬁed" to "classified"). Footnote numbers that fell inside a sentence were removed. Words were not changed.

### 0.1 Flags: misattributions, weak citations, and open items (read first)

1. **"Only the ethical one fits best with the facts" is NOT Milgrom.** Jiří Moskala's survey quotes this line inside his Milgrom section. His footnote gives it to Herold S. Stern, "The Ethics of the Clean and the Unclean," _Judaism_ 6 (1957): 320–321. Do not credit it to Milgrom.
2. **Milgrom, "curb their hunger for power."** Theology of Work cites this as _Leviticus 1–16_ (Yale, 1998), p. 105. Page 105 of that volume falls in the sacrifice chapters, not Leviticus 11. The line may come from Milgrom's shorter _Leviticus: A Book of Ritual and Ethics_ (Fortress, 2004). **[UNVERIFIED]** against either book.
3. **Mary Douglas, _Purity and Danger_ (1966).** The often-quoted sentence "Holiness is exemplified by completeness..." was not checked against the book. Secondary sources confirm she tied holiness to "wholeness" and "completeness" (pp. 51–57). Her later revision and her 2005 self-correction ARE verified (see A3).
4. **Wenham, NICOT _Leviticus_ (1979).** The quote on Mark 7 ("Jesus was drawing out the meaning of the symbolism...", p. 184) comes only through a student paper **[V-2nd, weak]**. A parallel statement in Wenham's own 1981 article _is_ verified (see A5).
5. **_Five Views on Law and Gospel_.** This book first appeared in 1993 as _The Law, the Gospel, and the Modern Christian: Five Views_. Zondervan reissued it in the Counterpoints series in 1996. Moo's chapter is labelled "A Modified Lutheran View," not New Covenant Theology. Linking Moo with NCT is common, but the chapter title does not use that label.
6. **The UMJC "One Law Movements" statement** was read in a reprint on Ron Cantor's site ("Reprinted from UMJC.org"). The original UMJC URL was not found. The statement names "writers of First Fruits of Zion, including Tim Hegg." That is now out of date. FFOZ later taught a "Divine Invitation" view, and Hegg (TorahResource) has written against it.
7. **Dunn page reference.** Snoeberger (DBSJ 2007) cites Dunn's view of "the weak" as "_Romans 1–8_... p. 798." Page 798 is in _Romans 9–16_ (WBC 38B). Nanos cites it that way. Treat "1–8" as a typo.
8. **Luther, _Freedom of a Christian_.** Project Gutenberg eBook #1911 does not name its translator. The wording ("A Christian man is the most free lord of all...") matches the Wace and Buchheim translation (1883). The modern _Luther's Works_ 31 wording ("perfectly free lord of all, subject to none") is widely quoted but was **not fetched** here.
9. **Luther, "How Christians Should Regard Moses."** The text used is E. Theodore Bachmann's translation (_LW_ 35:161–174), posted by wordofhisgrace.org. That site says it is in the public domain. This was not confirmed. Keep quotes short.
10. **Charles Ryrie, "The End of the Law"** (_Bibliotheca Sacra_ 124, July 1967). Only a paywalled preview was read.
11. **Cranfield, Dunn, and Moo on "the weak."** Their positions are given from surveys (Snoeberger, Nanos, Schreiner). No verbatim quotes from their commentaries were checked **[S]**.
12. **The Catholic historical record is sharp.** Aquinas (ST I-II q.103 a.4) and the Council of Florence (1442) said that keeping the legal ceremonies after the gospel was mortal sin. This must be stated honestly. Modern Catholic teaching also says "the Old Covenant has never been revoked" (CCC 121). No modern magisterial text was found that applies the Florence language to Messianic Jews today. That question is open.
13. **Mark 7:19 is translated two ways.** NRSV, ESV, NIV, and the RSV text quoted in CCC 582 read "(Thus he declared all foods clean)" as Mark's own comment. KJV ("purging all meats") and WEB ("thus purifying all foods") join the words to the digestion image. Adventist, Hebrew Roots, and some Messianic readers depend on the second reading.
14. **Ethiopian Orthodox food practice.** One Torah-observant writer (David Wilber) reports that the Ethiopian Orthodox Church keeps biblical food laws. No Ethiopian Orthodox source was checked. It is not used below.
15. **Sifra numbering varies by edition.** The Akiva saying on Leviticus 19:18 is cited here as Sifra, Kedoshim, Chapter 4:12 (Sefaria). The Rashi saying on Leviticus 20:26 cites a different Sifra section in the Rosenbaum-Silbermann translation. Check the edition before printing a reference.
16. **Two agreements from the brief could NOT be sourced on the Jewish side.** "Jesus ate with people others called unclean" and "the Jerusalem Council did not require Gentiles to become Jews" are Christian texts. Jewish sources have a close parallel only for the second one: the Noahide laws. See Part D for the exact limits.

---

# PART A — Why did God give the food laws? Readings of Leviticus 11

The text states its own purpose: "Sanctify yourselves therefore, and be holy; for I am holy... You shall therefore be holy, for I am holy" (Lev 11:44–45, WEB). It also ties the food laws to Israel's calling: "I, Yahweh, am holy, and have set you apart from the peoples, that you should be mine" (Lev 20:26, WEB). **Every reading below accepts this stated purpose.** They differ on _how_ the food laws serve holiness.

A caution that scholars from all sides share: Joe Sprinkle (evangelical) notes that "The rationale for these laws is never clearly spelled out, but several explanations probably have some validity." **[V]** Sprinkle, "The Rationale of the Laws of Clean and Unclean in the Old Testament," _JETS_ 43.4 (2000): 637–657, abstract. https://biblicalstudies.org.uk/pdf/jets/clean_sprinkle.pdf . Moskala (Adventist) reports Milgrom's remark that there are about as many theories as there are interpreters. **[V-2nd]** (Moskala, _Biblical Research_ 46 [2001], p. 5, citing Milgrom 1990, p. 159.)

---

## A1. Hygiene and health

**Who holds it.** Maimonides (Moses ben Maimon, 1138–1204), _Guide for the Perplexed_ 3.48. Popular modern health readings, for example S. I. McMillen, _None of These Diseases_ (1963), cited by Sprinkle. Aquinas gives a bodily-health reason as one of several (ST I-II q.102 a.6 ad 1). Seventh-day Adventists stress health, together with other reasons (see B8).

**Core texts.** Lev 11; Deut 14; Exod 15:26 ("none of the diseases").

**The argument in its own terms.** God cares for the body. Forbidden foods are harmful, and the list protects the people. Maimonides also names reasons beyond health, such as keeping away from idolatrous rites (meat with milk) and filthiness (the pig).

**Quotes**

> "I maintain that the food which is forbidden by the Law is unwholesome. There is nothing among the forbidden kinds of food whose injurious character is doubted, except pork (Lev. 11:7), and fat (ibid. 7:23). But also in these cases the doubt is not justified."
> — Maimonides, _Guide for the Perplexed_ 3.48, trans. M. Friedländer (1903). **[V]** https://www.sefaria.org/Guide_for_the_Perplexed%2C_Part_3.48

> "The principal reason why the Law forbids swine's flesh is to be found in the circumstance that its habits and its food are very dirty and loathsome."
> — Ibid. **[V]**

> "Meat boiled in milk is undoubtedly gross food, and makes overfull; but I think that most probably it is also prohibited because it is somehow connected with idolatry..."
> — Ibid. **[V]**

> "...the flesh of certain animals is unclean, either because like the pig they feed on unclean things... or because their flesh, through being too moist or too dry, engenders corrupt humors in the human body."
> — Aquinas, _Summa Theologiae_ I-II q.102 a.6 ad 1 (Fathers of the English Dominican Province trans.). **[V]** https://www.newadvent.org/summa/2102.htm

**Strongest criticisms**

- _Evangelical scholarship:_ "Hygiene, however, is at most a secondary explanation. Some animals which are excluded have no association with disease: the camel, for example, is a delicacy for Arabs to this day..." Sprinkle adds that poisonous plants are not listed, and that "some of the clean animals present health hazards." **[V]** Sprinkle, _JETS_ 43.4, 644–645.
- _Jewish tradition (Chabad):_ "if keeping kosher was purely for physical health benefits, G‑d would certainly have included them in his instructions for humanity at large, and not just the Jewish nation." **[V]** Pinchas Taylor, "Reason for Keeping Kosher," Chabad.org. https://www.chabad.org/library/article_cdo/aid/2837510/jewish/Reason-for-Keeping-Kosher.htm
- _Conservative Judaism (My Jewish Learning):_ Rabbi Bradley Shavit Artson sets aside the health theory. He grounds kashrut in holiness (see A7). **[S]** https://www.myjewishlearning.com/article/kashrut-after-refrigerators/
- _Christian critique:_ If health were the main purpose, it is hard to explain why the New Testament church stopped keeping the laws. Sprinkle raises this point, citing Mark 7:19. **[S]**

---

## A2. Symbolic and ethical (moral lessons pictured in the animals)

**Who holds it.** The Letter of Aristeas §§128–171 (Jewish, Alexandria, 2nd century BC). Philo of Alexandria, _Special Laws_ 4.100–118 (1st century AD). The Epistle of Barnabas 10 (early Christian, c. 70–135 AD). Aquinas's "figurative reason" (ST I-II q.102 a.6 ad 1). In a milder form, Ramban (Nachmanides).

**Core texts.** Lev 11:3 (split hoof, chewing the cud); Lev 11:9 (fins and scales); Lev 11:13–19 (birds of prey); Ps 1:1 (for Barnabas).

**The argument in its own terms.** The animals stand for kinds of character. Clean animals chew the cud, which pictures meditating on God's word and remembering Him. Their divided hoof pictures moral discernment. Birds of prey picture violence and oppression. By eating according to God's list, the people practice justice and memory.

**Key distinction inside this view.** Aristeas and Philo _keep_ the literal law and add the symbol. Barnabas treats the symbol as the _real_ meaning and says Israel misread it literally. Philo himself warns against allegorists who drop the practice.

**Quotes**

> "For you must not fall into the degrading idea that it was out of regard to mice and weasels and other such things that Moses drew up his laws with such exceeding care. All these ordinances were made for the sake of righteousness to aid the quest for virtue and the perfecting of character."
> — Letter of Aristeas §144, trans. R. H. Charles (Oxford, 1913). **[V]** http://www.pseudepigrapha.com/pseudepigrapha/aristeas.htm

> "...by naming them unclean, he gave a sign by means of them that those, for whom the legislation was ordained, must practice righteousness in their hearts and not tyrannize over any one in reliance upon their own strength..."
> — Aristeas §147. **[V]**

> "For the division of the hoof and the separation of the claws are intended to teach us that we must discriminate between our individual actions with a view to the practice of virtue."
> — Aristeas §150. **[V]**

> "...he has forbidden with all his might all animals... which are most fleshy and fat, and calculated to excite treacherous pleasure..."
> — Philo, _Special Laws_ 4.100, trans. C. D. Yonge. **[V]** http://www.earlyjewishwritings.com/text/philo/book30.html

> "And these signs are both of them symbols of instruction and of the most scientific learning, by which the better is separated from the worse..."
> — Philo, _Special Laws_ 4.106. **[V]**

> "Is there then not a command of God they should not eat [these things]? There is, but Moses spoke with a spiritual reference. For this reason he named the swine, as much as to say, You shall not join yourself to men who resemble swine."
> — Epistle of Barnabas 10, trans. Roberts and Donaldson (ANF 1). **[V]** https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/0124.htm

> "Moses then issued three doctrines concerning meats with a spiritual significance; but they received them according to fleshly desire, as if he had merely spoken of [literal] meats."
> — Barnabas 10. **[V]**

> "...a sordid person within the permissible realm of the Torah!"
> — Ramban on Lev 19:2, trans. C. B. Chavel. Ramban warns that one can obey every food rule and still be a glutton, so "be holy" calls for "moderation even in matters which are permitted." **[V]** (short quote, copyrighted translation) https://www.sefaria.org/Ramban_on_Leviticus.19.2

**Strongest criticisms**

- _From within Hellenistic Judaism (Philo against allegorists who stop observing):_
  > "...they ought to attend to both classes of things, applying themselves both to an accurate investigation of invisible things, and also to an irreproachable observance of those laws which are notorious."
  > — Philo, _Migration of Abraham_ 89. **[V]** http://www.earlyjewishwritings.com/text/philo/book16.html
- _Modern scholarship:_ Symbolic meanings are chosen by the reader and do not arise from the text. Moskala classes allegorical and symbolic readings as subjective. He notes a "striking difference" between Jewish allegory (Aristeas, Philo), which _defends_ observance, and Christian allegory, which does not. **[V-2nd]** Moskala, _BR_ 46 (2001).
- _Jewish critique of Barnabas's line:_ The symbolic reading was used to deny that Israel should keep the law literally. Rashi's reading (A6) and Chabad (A7) insist the command is binding as given. **[S]**

---

## A3. Categories and wholeness (Mary Douglas)

**Who holds it.** Mary Douglas (anthropologist, Catholic), _Purity and Danger_ (1966), ch. 3, "The Abominations of Leviticus." She changed her view in _Leviticus as Literature_ (Oxford, 1999) and again in a 2005 preface to the Hebrew translation of _Purity and Danger_.

**Core texts.** Gen 1 (the kinds of creatures); Lev 11 (land, water, air, swarming things); Lev 21–22 (blemishes).

**Her 1966 argument, in summary [S].** Holiness means wholeness and completeness, keeping God's categories distinct. Animals that do not fit their class (a land animal without both marks, a water creature without fins and scales) are "out of place." Dirt is "matter out of place." Keeping the food laws is a daily act of honoring God's ordered creation. (Secondary confirmation: Douglas "defines the holy as 'wholeness' and 'completeness,' which distinguishes 'the categories of creation,'" citing _Purity and Danger_ p. 51. **[V-2nd]** Trocino, SBL student paper; Sprinkle, _JETS_ 43.4, n. 53, cites pp. 51–57.)

**Her revision (1999).** The unclean animals are not abominable. Leviticus protects them. They are not to be eaten or sacrificed, but they are still God's good creatures.

> "But in Leviticus the unclean animals are not abominable."
> — Douglas, _Leviticus as Literature_ (Oxford, 1999), ch. 7. **[V]** (checked against a PDF of the 2000 paperback; copyrighted, short quote)

> "To be classified unclean ought to be an advantage for the survival of the species."
> — Ibid., p. 142 (page per Baumgarten 2020). **[V]**

> "...an overall survey of land animals classed all except the flocks and herds of the people as unclean, not to be eaten or offered for sacrifice... And the animals classified as unclean turn out to be not abominable at all."
> — Ibid. **[V]**

**Her self-correction (2005 preface, published 2020).**

> "But I now realize, after many decades, that the argument was not nearly good enough for the Jewish dietary laws. It was worse than rash to make use of that sacred book as if it were any other text."
> — Mary Douglas, 2005 preface to the Hebrew translation of _Purity and Danger_, printed in Albert I. Baumgarten, "The Preface to the Hebrew Translation of _Purity and Danger_," _Religion and Society_ 11 (2020). DOI 10.3167/arrs.2020.110103. **[V]** https://www.berghahnjournals.com/view/journals/religion-and-society/11/1/arrs110103.xml

**Strongest criticisms**

- _Biblical scholars on the revised view:_ James Watts: "[Douglas's] analysis of Leviticus provides a rather extreme example of mixing textual meaning and ritual significance and folding both into a theological superstructure provided by the interpreter." Thomas Kazen calls her parallels "an arbitrary use of parallels, a random selection of elements and an idiosyncratic structuring of Leviticus." **[V-2nd]** Both are quoted in Baumgarten 2020.
- _Evangelical critique of the "anomaly" idea:_ "God created both clean and unclean animals. It is difficult therefore to posit that He created them with 'anomalies.'" **[V-2nd]** Trocino, citing Rooker, NAC _Leviticus_ 173.
- _Adventist survey:_ Douglas's later view mixes allegorical, anthropological, and ethical approaches. **[V-2nd]** Moskala, _BR_ 46.
- _Douglas herself_ (above) came to regard her first argument as inadequate for Leviticus.

---

## A4. Life and death, reverence for life (Jacob Milgrom)

**Who holds it.** Jacob Milgrom (Conservative rabbi; leading Leviticus scholar), _Leviticus 1–16_ (Anchor Bible 3, 1991), pp. 704–742. Earlier: "The Biblical Diet Laws as an Ethical System," _Interpretation_ 17 (1963): 288–301. Also "Ethics and Ritual: The Foundations of the Biblical Dietary Laws," in _Religion and Law_ (Eisenbrauns, 1990). Similar Jewish ethical readings: Samuel Dresner (1959), Herold Stern (1957).

**Core texts.** Gen 9:3–6 (meat allowed, blood forbidden); Lev 17:10–14 ("the life of the flesh is in the blood"); Lev 11 (short list of permitted animals); Deut 12:20–24.

**The argument, in summary [S].** Impurity in Leviticus points toward death (corpses, carcasses, losses of blood or semen, wasting skin disease). Holiness points toward life. The food system limits how Israel may take animal life: only a few species, only by proper slaughter, and never the blood. This teaches reverence for life and restrains the human urge to kill.

**Quotes**

- The phrase "reverence for life" as Milgrom's thesis: "The well-known Torah scholar Jacob Milgrom has insisted for more than forty years that there is an ethical system of 'reverence for life' behind these laws." **[V-2nd]** Esias E. Meyer, "Respect for animal life in the book of Leviticus," _Old Testament Essays_ 24/1 (2011), abstract. https://scielo.org.za/scielo.php?pid=S1010-99192011000100008&script=sci_arttext
- Moskala's one-line summary of Milgrom (not Milgrom's words): "restricted access to animal life as food teaches reverence for life." **[V-2nd]** Moskala, _BR_ 46, citing Milgrom, _Leviticus 1–16_, p. 735.
- "Though they may satisfy their appetite for food, they must curb their hunger for power." — attributed to Milgrom by Theology of Work. **[UNVERIFIED page; see Flag 2]** https://www.theologyofwork.org/old-testament/leviticus-and-work/the-unclean-and-the-clean-leviticus-1116/the-permissibility-of-eating-particular-animals-leviticus-11

**Strongest criticisms**

- _David P. Wright:_ "The meat prohibitions do limit the choice of animal food, but do not limit the amount of meat consumed or the number of animals killed." **[V-2nd]** Quoted in Moskala, _BR_ 46, from Wright, "Observations on the Ethical Foundations of the Biblical Dietary Laws," in _Religion and Law_ (1990), 193–198.
- _Walter Houston (via Sprinkle):_ "designating certain species as 'unclean, abhorrent, or abominable' rather than 'holy' seems an odd way of inculcating 'reverence for life.'" **[V-2nd]** Sprinkle, _JETS_ 43.4, 649.
- _Moskala (Adventist):_ The ethical motive "does not really explain why the animals were divided exactly in the fashion as we find it in the Bible. Why, for instance, was the camel unclean from an ethical viewpoint?" **[V]** Moskala, _BR_ 46. https://wp.ces.org.tw/wp-content/uploads/2022/09/Jiri-Moskala-BR-46-2001-5-41-Categorization-and-evaluation-of-different-kinds-of-interpretation-of-the-laws-of-clean-and-unclean-animals-in-Lev-11.pdf
- _Friendly reception:_ Sprinkle, an evangelical, writes that "Milgrom has provided the most satisfactory reconstruction of the symbolism to date." **[V]** Sprinkle, n. 24.

---

## A5. Separation and identity (Lev 20:24–26; Gordon Wenham)

**Who holds it.** Gordon J. Wenham (evangelical Anglican), _The Book of Leviticus_ (NICOT, 1979), and "The Theology of Unclean Food," _Evangelical Quarterly_ 53 (1981). Also Jeffrey Tigay (JPS _Deuteronomy_), Sprinkle (as one of several reasons), and Walter Houston. In part, Milgrom's "concentric circles" (all nations / Israel / priests matched by all animals / clean animals / sacrificial animals).

**Core texts.** Lev 20:24–26 ("I... have set you apart from the peoples"); Exod 19:6 ("a holy nation"); Acts 10:13–15 and 10:28 (Peter's vision tied to Gentiles); Eph 2:14.

**The argument in its own terms.** Clean and unclean animals mirror Israel and the nations. Each meal reminds Israel that God chose them, and it limits shared tables with idolaters. The laws are a daily sign of covenant identity. For Christian holders of this view, this explains why the laws were dropped once Gentiles came into the one people of God.

**Quotes**

> "Observance of the food laws was one of the outward marks of a practising Jew, and this in turn enhanced Jewish attachment to them as a reminder of their special status."
> — Wenham, "The Theology of Unclean Food," _EvQ_ 53 (1981). **[V]** https://biblicalstudies.org.uk/pdf/eq/1981-1_006.pdf

> "With the incorporation of Gentiles on an equal footing with Jews in the Christian church, the food laws and circumcision lost their symbolic significance and were therefore dropped within the church."
> — Ibid. **[V]**

> "Acts 10 explicitly connects the incorporation of the Gentiles with the abolition of the food laws."
> — Ibid. **[V]**

> "In keeping the food laws, the Israelite was thus acknowledging that God had chosen and saved them from the nations. Moreover, the food laws discouraged table fellowship with the Canaanites..."
> — Sprinkle, _JETS_ 43.4, 651. **[V]**

> "The food laws not only reminded Israel of her distinctiveness, but they also served to enforce it."
> — Wenham, "Clean and Unclean," _New Bible Dictionary_, 3rd ed. (1996), 211. **[V-2nd]** quoted in Moskala, _BR_ 46.

**Strongest criticisms**

- _Adventist (Moskala):_ "The heavy objection to this sociological interpretation comes from the fact that God first chose and separated Israel in order to create one nation, and then He gave them the gift of the law... The idea that dietary laws have the aim to separate Israel physically from other nations is foreign to the biblical teaching." **[V]** Moskala, _BR_ 46. Adventists add that Noah already knew "clean" animals (Gen 7:2) before Israel existed.
- _Messianic Jewish and post-supersessionist critique:_ If the food laws marked Israel's calling, and Israel's calling is irrevocable (Rom 11:29), then the laws still have meaning for Jews. They were not simply "dropped." Kinzer and Rudolph make this case (B9). **[S]**
- _Jewish critique:_ Separation is a result, not the root reason. The root is God's decree (A6). **[S]**

---

## A6. A decree without a stated reason (chok / chukkim; Rashi)

**Who holds it.** Rabbinic tradition: Sifra; Babylonian Talmud Yoma 67b; Rashi (1040–1105). Chabad today. Maimonides accepts the category but encourages seeking reasons (see criticisms).

**Core texts.** Lev 18:4–5 ("my ordinances [mishpatim]... my statutes [chukkim]"); Lev 20:26; Num 19:2 (the red heifer, the classic chok).

**The argument in its own terms.** Some commands have reasons people can see (mishpatim), such as bans on theft and murder. Others are royal decrees (chukkim). Their reasons are not given, and the obedient heart keeps them out of love for the King. Food laws, especially the ban on pork, are the standard example. A Jew should not claim to find pork disgusting. He should say he would eat it, but his Father in heaven has forbidden it.

**Quotes**

> "And you shall keep my statutes, is a reference to matters that Satan and the nations of the world challenge because the reason for these mitzvot are not known. They are: The prohibitions against eating pork; wearing garments that are made from diverse kinds of material..."
> — Babylonian Talmud, Yoma 67b, William Davidson Edition (Steinsaltz). **[V]** https://www.sefaria.org/Yoma.67b

> "AND KEEP MINE ORDINANCES — matters which are decrees of the King (promulgated without any reason being stated) against which the evil inclination raises objections: 'Why should we observe them' and against which also the nations of the world raise objections, as e.g., the prohibition of eating swine's flesh..."
> — Rashi on Lev 18:4, trans. Rosenbaum and Silbermann (1929–34). **[V]** https://www.sefaria.org/Rashi_on_Leviticus.18.4

> "...one should not say, 'My soul loathes swine's flesh'... but one should say, 'I would, indeed, like them, but what can I do since my Father in heaven has imposed these decrees upon me'?"
> — Rashi on Lev 20:26, citing Rabbi Eleazar ben Azariah in the Sifra. **[V]** https://www.sefaria.org/Rashi_on_Leviticus.20.26

> "...because Israel cleave to the Omnipresent and therefore deserve to remain in life... He therefore separated them from what is unclean, and imposed commandments upon them, whilst to the other nations who do not cleave to him He prohibited nought."
> — Rashi on Lev 11:2. Rashi compares this to a physician who gives a diet only to the patient who can recover. **[V]** https://www.sefaria.org/Rashi_on_Leviticus.11.2

**Strongest criticisms**

- _From within Judaism (Maimonides the rationalist):_ Commands have purposes, and one should look for them. Maimonides also warns against dismissing a law whose reason one cannot find:
  > "It is appropriate for a person to meditate on the judgments of the holy Torah and know their ultimate purpose according to his capacity. If he cannot find a reason or a motivating rationale for a practice, he should not regard it lightly."
  > — Maimonides, _Mishneh Torah_, Me'ilah 8:8, trans. Eliyahu Touger. **[V]** (copyrighted translation; short quote) https://www.sefaria.org/Mishneh_Torah%2C_Trespass.8.8
- _Hellenistic Jewish critique:_ Aristeas §144 (above) rejects the idea that the laws are arbitrary rules "out of regard to mice and weasels."
- _Christian critique:_ Christians who hold A2 or A5 argue that the New Testament shows the reason: Christ and the joining of Gentiles. On that view, "no stated reason" does not mean "no reason." **[S]**

---

## A7. How observant Jews understand kashrut today

**Who.** Orthodox (Chabad), Conservative (Rabbi Bradley Shavit Artson), and rabbinic sources above.

**Summary.** Jews keep kosher first because God commanded it. Kashrut makes eating holy, three times a day. It is a discipline of the covenant and a mark of Jewish identity. Health benefits may exist, but they are not the reason. The laws bind Jews. Gentiles are not required to keep them (see D8).

**Quotes**

> "We eat kosher because G‑d commanded us to, and by fulfilling the Divine will we connect to G‑d."
> — "What Is Kosher?", Chabad.org. **[V]** https://www.chabad.org/library/article_cdo/aid/113425/jewish/What-Is-Kosher.htm

> "None of the above, however, is the reason we keep kosher. Rather, we keep kosher because it is the will of the Creator."
> — Ibid. **[V]**

> "Life in its totality is a sacred endeavor. Even the seemingly mundane activity of eating is a G‑dly act and a uniquely Jewish experience."
> — Ibid. **[V]**

> "In Jewish tradition, the kosher laws are classified as super-rational."
> — Pinchas Taylor, "Reason for Keeping Kosher," Chabad.org. **[V]**

> "Before I deal with your questions specifically, it is important to understand that we didn't make up the kashrut laws."
> — Tzvi Freeman, "Why Do We Keep Kosher?", Chabad.org. **[V]** https://www.chabad.org/library/article_cdo/aid/60319/jewish/Why-Do-We-Keep-Kosher.htm

> "Kashrut is a way of welcoming the holiness of Judaism into our daily lives."
> — Rabbi Bradley Shavit Artson, "Why Keep Kosher?", My Jewish Learning. **[V]** (fetched and checked through a page-reading tool, not a raw text dump) https://www.myjewishlearning.com/article/kashrut-after-refrigerators/

**Criticisms.** None are needed for this descriptive section. Christian traditions that differ are covered in Part B.

---

# PART B — Do the food laws bind Christians today?

## Map in one table

| Position                                 | Lev 11 food laws for Gentile Christians?                                   | For Jewish believers in Jesus?                                                         | Main reason given                               |
| ---------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------- |
| Catholic                                 | No. Ceremonial, fulfilled. Church has its own fasting and abstinence.      | Historically no (Aquinas, Florence). Modern position not settled in a text found here. | Christ fulfills the ceremonial law; Mark 7:19   |
| Eastern Orthodox                         | No Levitical list. Strong ascetic fasting. Blood banned in canon law.      | Not addressed in sources fetched                                                       | Fasting as discipline, not purity               |
| Reformed                                 | No. Ceremonial laws "abrogated."                                           | No                                                                                     | Moral / ceremonial / judicial division (WCF 19) |
| Lutheran                                 | No. Moses is not our lawgiver except where he agrees with natural law.     | No                                                                                     | Law and gospel; Christian freedom               |
| Anabaptist / Mennonite                   | No (practice)                                                              | No                                                                                     | Read Scripture "in harmony with Jesus Christ"   |
| Wesleyan / Methodist                     | No. Ceremonies do not bind; moral law does.                                | No                                                                                     | Anglican Article VII / Methodist Article VI     |
| NCT / progressive covenantal             | No. The Mosaic covenant as a whole has ended.                              | No                                                                                     | New covenant replaces old; law of Christ        |
| Dispensational                           | No                                                                         | No                                                                                     | Mosaic dispensation distinct and ended          |
| Seventh-day Adventist                    | **Yes, keep the clean/unclean distinction** (health, holiness, pre-Mosaic) | Yes                                                                                    | Gen 7:2; body as temple; Mark 7 about tradition |
