| Messianic Jewish (UMJC, Kinzer, Rudolph) | No, not obligatory                                                         | **Yes, as covenant calling**                                                           | Israel's calling is irrevocable                 |
| Hebrew Roots / One Law                   | **Yes**                                                                    | Yes                                                                                    | One law for native and stranger; grafted in     |

---

## B1. Catholic

**Who holds it.** The Catholic Church: _Catechism of the Catholic Church_ (1992/1997), Code of Canon Law (1983), Council of Florence (1442). Thomas Aquinas, ST I-II qq. 98–108. Earlier, Augustine.

**Core texts.** Mark 7:18–19; Matt 5:17; Rom 7:12; Gal 3:24; Acts 10; Acts 15; 1 Tim 4:4; Col 2:16–17; Heb 8:13.

**The argument in its own terms.** The Old Law is holy, good, and from God. It is the first stage of revealed law and a teacher leading to Christ. Its moral precepts (the Decalogue, natural law) remain. Its ceremonial precepts, including the food laws, were figures of Christ and ended when He came. Jesus "perfects the dietary law" by showing its teaching purpose: defilement comes from the heart. The Church has its own penitential discipline of fasting and abstinence. That discipline rests on the Church's authority and the call to penance, not on Levitical purity.

**Official quotes**

> "Going even further, Jesus perfects the dietary law, so important in Jewish daily life, by revealing its pedagogical meaning through a divine interpretation: 'Whatever goes into a man from outside cannot defile him. . . (Thus he declared all foods clean.). . . What comes out of a man is what defiles a man...'"
> — CCC 582. **[V]** https://www.vatican.va/archive/ENG0015/__P1N.HTM

> "According to Christian tradition, the Law is holy, spiritual, and good, yet still imperfect. Like a tutor it shows what must be done, but does not of itself give the strength, the grace of the Spirit, to fulfill it."
> — CCC 1963. **[V]** https://www.vatican.va/archive/ENG0015/__P6V.HTM

> "The Old Law is a preparation for the Gospel."
> — CCC 1964. **[V]** (same URL)

> "...a law of freedom, because it sets us free from the ritual and juridical observances of the Old Law..."
> — CCC 1972. **[V]** https://www.vatican.va/archive/ENG0015/__P6W.HTM

> "The Old Testament is an indispensable part of Sacred Scripture. Its books are divinely inspired and retain a permanent value, for the Old Covenant has never been revoked."
> — CCC 121. **[V]** https://www.vatican.va/archive/ENG0015/__PR.HTM

> "We must therefore distinguish three kinds of precept in the Old Law; viz. 'moral' precepts, which are dictated by the natural law; 'ceremonial' precepts, which are determinations of the Divine worship; and 'judicial' precepts, which are determinations of the justice to be maintained among men."
> — Aquinas, ST I-II q.99 a.4. **[V]** https://www.newadvent.org/summa/2099.htm

> "Speaking then of the first-mentioned uncleanness, no kind of food is unclean, or can defile a man, by reason of its nature..."
> — Aquinas, ST I-II q.102 a.6 ad 1. Aquinas gives several reasons for the food laws: bodily health, avoiding idolatry, "to prevent excessive care about food," and a figurative meaning. **[V]** https://www.newadvent.org/summa/2102.htm

> "It firmly believes, professes and teaches that every creature of God is good and nothing is to be rejected if it is received with thanksgiving, because according to the word of the Lord not what goes into the mouth defiles a person, and because the difference in the Mosaic law between clean and unclean foods belongs to ceremonial practices, which have passed away and lost their efficacy with the coming of the gospel."
> — Council of Florence, Bull of Union with the Copts (_Cantate Domino_), 1442. **[V]** https://www.papalencyclicals.net/councils/ecum17.htm

Florence says the apostolic decree (Acts 15:20, 29) "was suited to that time when a single church was rising from Jews and gentiles." It also teaches that "for the health of the body, for the practice of virtue or for the sake of regular and ecclesiastical discipline many things that are not proscribed can and should be omitted." **[V]** (same URL)

**Catholic fasting and abstinence**

> "The penitential days and times in the universal Church are every Friday of the whole year and the season of Lent." (Can. 1250) "Abstinence from meat, or from some other food as determined by the Episcopal Conference, is to be observed on all Fridays, unless a solemnity should fall on a Friday. Abstinence and fasting are to be observed on Ash Wednesday and Good Friday." (Can. 1251)
> — _Code of Canon Law_ (1983). **[V]** https://www.vatican.va/archive/cod-iuris-canonici/eng/documents/cic_lib4-cann1244-1253_en.html

> "The fifth precept ('You shall observe the prescribed days of fasting and abstinence.') ensures the times of ascesis and penance which prepare us for the liturgical feasts; they help us acquire mastery over our instincts and freedom of heart."
> — CCC 2043. **[V]** https://www.vatican.va/archive/ENG0015/__P75.HTM

**The hard historical edge (state it honestly).** Aquinas follows Augustine's three periods. Before the Passion, the ceremonies were "neither deadly nor dead." Between the Passion and the spread of the gospel, "it was lawful for the Jewish converts to Christianity to observe them, provided they did not put their trust in them so as to hold them to be necessary unto salvation." After the gospel was proclaimed, "it would be a mortal sin now to observe those ceremonies which the fathers of old fulfilled with devotion and fidelity." **[V]** ST I-II q.103 a.4. https://www.newadvent.org/summa/2103.htm . Florence (1442) "denounces all who after that time observe circumcision, the sabbath and other legal prescriptions." **[V]** See Flag 12 for modern nuance.

**Strongest criticisms from others**

- _Lutheran (Augsburg Confession XXVI):_ Church rules that "prescribe certain days and certain meats" should not bind consciences as necessary service to God. **[V]** (see B4)
- _Messianic Jewish:_ Rejects the idea that Jewish believers sin by keeping Torah. Rudolph and Kinzer argue the New Testament shows Jewish believers (including Paul, Acts 21:20–24) keeping the law (B9). **[S]**
- _Adventist:_ Mark 7 concerns ritual hand-washing and the "traditions of the elders," not the Levitical list (B8). **[V]**
- _Orthodox Jewish:_ Calling kashrut "passed away" treats a living covenant as obsolete. CCC 121's "never been revoked" is welcomed in Jewish–Catholic dialogue. **[S]**

---

## B2. Eastern Orthodox

**Who holds it.** Orthodox Church in America (OCA) teaching materials; the Apostolic Canons and the Quinisext Council (in Trullo, 692) as received in Orthodox canon law; _The Lenten Triodion_ (trans. Mother Mary and Kallistos Ware), as quoted by the OCA.

**Core texts.** Matt 6:16–17 ("when you fast"); Mark 2:20; Acts 15:20, 29 (blood); Rom 14; Didache 8.

**The argument in its own terms.** Orthodox fasting is not the Levitical clean/unclean system. It is an ascetic discipline received from the apostolic Church. It is kept on Wednesdays and Fridays and in four fasting seasons. Meat, dairy, eggs, fish with backbones, wine, and oil are given up in stages. The goal is freedom from the passions and readiness for communion, not ritual purity. Shellfish and octopus, which Leviticus forbids, are _allowed_ on strict fast days. The canons also forbid rejecting food as evil. The ban on eating blood is still found in Orthodox canon law (Trullo canon 67).

**Quotes**

> "Blessed fasting is done in secret, without ostentation or accusation of others (Mt 6.16; Rom 14)."
> "These rules exist not as a Pharisaic 'burden too hard to bear' (Lk 11.46), but as an ideal to be striven for; not as an end in themselves, but as a means to spiritual perfection crowned in love."
> — OCA, _The Orthodox Faith_, vol. 2, "Lenten Fasting." **[V]** https://www.oca.org/orthodoxy/the-orthodox-faith/worship/the-church-year/lenten-fasting (Attributed to Fr. Thomas Hopko's series. The author name was not shown on the fetched page.)

> "In practice, octopus and shell-fish are also allowed on days of xerophagy..."
> — OCA liturgics page, quoting _The Lenten Triodion_. **[V]** https://www.oca.org/liturgics/outlines/fasting-fast-free-seasons-of-the-church

> "The purpose of fasting is not to 'give up' things, nor to do something 'sacrificial.' The purpose of fasting is to learn discipline..."
> — OCA Questions and Answers, "Orthodox Fasting." **[V]** https://www.oca.org/questions/dailylife/orthodox-fasting

> "If any bishop, presbyter, or deacon... abstains from marriage, or flesh, or wine, not by way of religious restraint, but as abhorring them, forgetting that God made all things very good... let him be corrected, or else be deposed..."
> — Apostolic Canon 51 (NPNF² 14, trans. Percival). **[V]** https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/3820.htm

> "The divine Scripture commands us to abstain from blood, from things strangled, and from fornication... If anyone henceforth venture to eat in any way the blood of an animal, if he be a clergyman, let him be deposed; if a layman, let him be cut off."
> — Council in Trullo (692), Canon 67. **[V]** https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/3814.htm

> "But let not your fasts be with the hypocrites; for they fast on the second and fifth day of the week; but fast on the fourth day and the Preparation (Friday)."
> — Didache 8 (ANF 7, trans. Riddle). **[V]** https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/0714.htm

**Strongest criticisms from others**

- _Lutheran:_ Set fast days risk binding the conscience (AC XXVI). The OCA pages themselves warn against legalism. **[S]**
- _Jewish:_ The Didache sets Christian fast days against Jewish ones ("with the hypocrites"). Jewish readers may find this polemical. **[S]**
- _Catholic (Florence):_ Treats the blood ban as fitted to the early mixed church and now ceased. Orthodox canon law still states it. **[V]**

---

## B3. Reformed (the threefold division)

**Who holds it.** Westminster Confession of Faith (1646), chs. 19–20. John Calvin, _Institutes_ 2.7–2.8, 3.19. Reformed and Presbyterian churches. (Theonomists such as Greg Bahnsen also treat the ceremonial laws as fulfilled. **[S]**)

**Core texts.** Rom 3:31; Rom 7:12; Col 2:16–17; Heb 8–10; Gal 4:1–7; Matt 5:17.

**The argument in its own terms.** God's law has three parts. The moral law, summed up in the Ten Commandments, binds everyone forever. The ceremonial laws, including food laws, were given to Israel "as a church under age." They pictured Christ and are now abrogated. The judicial laws expired with Israel's state, except for their "general equity." Christian liberty frees believers from the ceremonial yoke. It also frees the conscience in things indifferent (food, days). But love limits how that liberty is used around the weak.

**Official and primary quotes**

> "Beside this law, commonly called moral, God was pleased to give to the people of Israel, as a church under age, ceremonial laws, containing several typical ordinances, partly of worship, prefiguring Christ, his graces, actions, sufferings, and benefits; and partly, holding forth divers instructions of moral duties. All which ceremonial laws are now abrogated, under the new testament."
> — WCF 19.3 (OPC text). **[V]** https://www.opc.org/wcf.html

> "The moral law doth forever bind all, as well justified persons as others, to the obedience thereof..."
> — WCF 19.5. **[V]**

> "...under the new testament, the liberty of Christians is further enlarged, in their freedom from the yoke of the ceremonial law, to which the Jewish church was subjected..."
> — WCF 20.1. **[V]**

> "God alone is Lord of the conscience, and hath left it free from the doctrines and commandments of men, which are, in anything, contrary to his Word; or beside it, if matters of faith, or worship."
> — WCF 20.2. **[V]**

> "The case of ceremonies, which have been abrogated, not as to their effect, but only as to their use, is very different. Their having been abolished by the advent of Christ, is so far from derogating from their sanctity, that it rather recommends and renders it more illustrious."
> — Calvin, _Institutes_ 2.7.16, trans. John Allen. **[V]** https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/45001

> "The third use of the law, which is the principal one, and which is more nearly connected with the proper end of it, relates to the faithful, in whose hearts the Spirit of God already lives and reigns."
> — Calvin, _Institutes_ 2.7.12 (Allen). **[V]**

> "The third part of Christian liberty teaches us, that we are bound by no obligation before God respecting external things, which in themselves are indifferent... Many in the present age think it a folly to raise any dispute concerning the free use of meats, of days, and of habits... but they are of greater importance than is generally believed."
> — Calvin, _Institutes_ 3.19.7 (Allen). **[V]** https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/64392

> "...we shall so temper the use of our liberty, that it ought to submit to the ignorance of weak brethren, but not at all to the austerity of Pharisees."
> — Calvin, _Institutes_ 3.19.11 (Allen). Calvin distinguishes "offence given" from "offence taken." **[V]**

**Strongest criticisms from others**

- _Messianic Jewish (UMJC statement):_ "If Israel has not been replaced, but is still the covenant people of God, then the division of the Torah into an easy moral/ceremonial dyad cannot be sustained." **[V]** https://www.roncantor.com/post/one-law-movements
- _Jewish:_ The Torah does not divide itself into moral and ceremonial parts. The rabbis weigh all 613 commandments together. **[S]**
- _NCT / progressive covenantal:_ The New Testament treats the Mosaic covenant as one unit that has ended as a covenant. The Decalogue binds only as taken up into the law of Christ (B6). **[S]**
- _Lutheran (Luther):_ Even the Ten Commandments bind Gentiles only "so far as they are implanted in everyone by nature." **[V]** (B4)

---

## B4. Lutheran (law and gospel)

**Who holds it.** Martin Luther; the Augsburg Confession (1530); the Formula of Concord (1577); confessional Lutheran churches.

**Core texts.** Rom 10:4; Gal 3–5; Rom 14; Col 2:16; Acts 15:10; 1 Tim 4:1–5; 1 Cor 9:19.

**The argument in its own terms.** Law and gospel must be kept distinct. Moses is Israel's lawgiver, not the Gentiles'. His commands bind Christians only where they agree with natural law and the New Testament. Christians still read Moses for three things: natural law, the promises of Christ, and examples of faith. Food rules, whether Mosaic or churchly, cannot earn grace and must not trap consciences. Fasting as self-discipline is still good. The Formula of Concord affirms a "third use" of the law as a rule of life for believers.

**Quotes**

> "Moses is dead. His rule ended when Christ came. He is of no further service."
> — Luther, "How Christians Should Regard Moses" (1525), trans. Bachmann, _LW_ 35. **[V]** https://sermons.martinluther.us/Luther_How_Christians_Moses.pdf

> "We will regard Moses as a teacher, but we will not regard him as our lawgiver - unless he agrees with both the New Testament and the natural law."
> — Ibid. **[V]**

> "I want to keep Moses and not sweep him under the rug, because I find three things in Moses."
> — Ibid. (1) commands, which bind only where they agree with natural law; (2) "the promises and pledges of God about Christ"; (3) "the beautiful examples of faith, of love, and of the cross." **[V]**

> "Therefore, we do not condemn fasting in itself, but the traditions which prescribe certain days and certain meats, with peril of conscience, as though such works were a necessary service."
> — Augsburg Confession XXVI.39, "Of the Distinction of Meats" (Triglot translation). **[V]** https://bookofconcord.org/augsburg-confession/article-xxvi/

> "...every Christian ought to train and subdue himself with bodily restraints, or bodily exercises and labors that neither satiety nor slothfulness tempt him to sin, but not that we may merit grace or make satisfaction for sins by such exercises."
> — AC XXVI.33. **[V]**

> "We believe, teach, and confess that, although men truly believing [in Christ] and truly converted to God have been freed and exempted from the curse and coercion of the Law, they nevertheless are not on this account without Law..."
> — Formula of Concord, Epitome VI.2 (Third Use of the Law). **[V]** https://bookofconcord.org/formula-of-concord-epitome/article-vi/

> "Moreover, how we can yield and give way with a good conscience to the weak in faith in such external adiaphora, Paul teaches Rom. 14, and proves it by his example, Acts 16:3; 21:26; 1 Cor. 9:19."
> — Formula of Concord, Solid Declaration X.9 (Church Rites / Adiaphora). **[V]** https://bookofconcord.org/formula-of-concord-solid-declaration/article-x/

> "This commandment, therefore, according to its gross sense, does not concern us Christians; for it is altogether an external matter, like other ordinances of the Old Testament..."
> — Luther, Large Catechism, Third Commandment §82 (on days). **[V]** https://bookofconcord.org/large-catechism/

**Strongest criticisms from others**

- _Reformed:_ Luther's "Moses is dead" undervalues the moral law. Calvin calls the third use "the principal one" (2.7.12). **[V]**
- _Catholic:_ Church fasting is not a "tradition of men" that obscures grace. It is a precept that helps "mastery over our instincts and freedom of heart" (CCC 2043). **[V]**
- _Messianic Jewish and Jewish:_ "Moses is the lawgiver of the Jews and not of the Gentiles." Many Jewish and Messianic readers accept this half of Luther's claim. They reject the idea that Moses is "dead" for Jews. **[S]**
- _Internal Lutheran note:_ Luther's 1525 sermon and the Formula's "third use" are held together in confessional Lutheranism. Scholars debate how well they fit. **[S]**

---

## B5. Anabaptist / Mennonite and Wesleyan / Methodist (brief)

### Anabaptist / Mennonite

**Summary [S].** Scripture is read with Jesus Christ at the center. Old Testament law is read through Jesus' life and teaching, especially the Sermon on the Mount. In practice, Mennonites do not keep Levitical food laws. Their emphasis falls on discipleship, community discernment, and nonviolence rather than a formal division of the law.

> "We seek to understand and interpret Scripture in harmony with Jesus Christ as we are led by the Holy Spirit in the church."
> "Because Jesus Christ is the Word become flesh, Scripture as a whole has its center and fulfillment in him."
> — _Confession of Faith in a Mennonite Perspective_ (1995), Article 4, "Scripture." **[V]** https://www.mennoniteusa.org/who-are-mennonites/what-we-believe/confession-of-faith/scripture/

**Criticism.** Reformed and Lutheran critics say a Christ-centered reading without a clear law framework can leave the Old Testament's role vague. Torah-observant critics say "harmony with Jesus" should include Jesus' own Torah observance (Matt 5:17). **[S]**

### Wesleyan / Methodist

> "Although the law given from God by Moses as touching ceremonies and rites doth not bind Christians, nor ought the civil precepts thereof of necessity be received in any commonwealth; yet notwithstanding, no Christian whatsoever is free from the obedience of the commandments which are called moral."
> — United Methodist Articles of Religion, Article VI, "Of the Old Testament" (from Anglican Article VII). **[V]** https://www.umc.org/en/content/articles-of-religion

> "The ritual or ceremonial law, delivered by Moses to the children of Israel, containing all the injunctions and ordinances which related to the old sacrifices and service of the temple, our Lord indeed did come to destroy, to dissolve, and utterly abolish... But the moral law, contained in the Ten Commandments, and enforced by the prophets, He did not take away."
> — John Wesley, Sermon 25, "Upon Our Lord's Sermon on the Mount, Discourse 5." **[V]** http://wesley.nnu.edu/john-wesley/the-sermons-of-john-wesley-1872-edition/sermon-25-upon-our-lords-sermon-on-the-mount-discourse-five/

**Criticism.** The same as for B3: the moral/ceremonial division is questioned by NCT, Messianic Jewish, and Jewish readers. **[S]**

---

## B6. New Covenant Theology and progressive covenantalism

**Who holds it.** Thomas R. Schreiner (_The Law and Its Fulfillment_, 1993; _40 Questions about Christians and Biblical Law_, 2010). Peter Gentry and Stephen Wellum (_Kingdom through Covenant_, 2012). Jason DeRouchie. Brian Rosner (_Paul and the Law_, 2013). Douglas Moo's "modified Lutheran" chapter in _Five Views_ is often grouped nearby (see Flag 5).

**Core texts.** Heb 7:12; 8:13; Rom 10:4; Gal 3:15–25; 1 Cor 9:20–21 ("under law toward Christ"); Matt 5:17 ("fulfill"); Mark 7:19.

**The argument in its own terms.** The Mosaic law is one covenant package. When the new covenant came, the whole package ended _as covenant law_. Christians are under "the law of Christ." Moral commands repeated or fulfilled in Christ still guide life. Food laws, circumcision, and sacrifices do not bind, because they belonged to the old covenant. The Old Testament law remains Scripture. It reveals God, points to Christ, and gives wisdom.

**Quotes**

> "To sum up: the Mosaic covenant has passed away because it has been fulfilled in Christ. This means that circumcision, food laws, and animal sacrifices should not be observed any longer. But the moral norms of the law are fulfilled by keeping them, and we keep them not in our on [sic: own] strength but by depending upon the Holy Spirit."
> — Thomas R. Schreiner, "The Fulfillment of the Law" (paper, SBTS). **[V]** https://cdn.sbts.edu/documents/tschreiner/Disp_Law.pdf

> "Christians repudiate the Mosaic law-covenant."
> — Jason S. DeRouchie, "Relating Moses's Law to Christians," 9Marks (2023). DeRouchie adopts Brian Rosner's three verbs: "repudiate, replace, and reappropriate." **[V]** https://www.9marks.org/article/relating-mosess-law-to-christians/

> "The prediction and arrival of the Melchizedekian priesthood means the Aaronic priesthood and the Mosaic law are no longer in force. Christians no longer live under the old covenant."
> — Schreiner, _Hebrews_ (2020), 217. **[V-2nd]** quoted by Wyatt Graham, TGC Canada. https://ca.thegospelcoalition.org/columns/detrinitate/does-the-mosaic-law-still-apply-to-christians/

**Moo in _Five Views_.** Moo's chapter is titled "The Law of Christ as the Fulfillment of the Law of Moses: A Modified Lutheran View." **[V]** (title per Monergism listing and Amazon/Zondervan catalogs.) Its argument is summarized, not quoted **[S]**. The Mosaic law belonged to the era before Christ. Christians are bound by the law of Christ. Old Testament commands bind only as taken up in New Testament teaching.

**Strongest criticisms from others**

- _Reformed:_ Losing the moral law's lasting authority weakens the Decalogue, including the Sabbath principle. WCF 19.5 says the moral law "doth forever bind all." **[V]**
- _Messianic Jewish:_ "Repudiating" the Mosaic covenant sounds like rejecting Israel's covenant, when Rom 11:29 calls it irrevocable. **[S]**
- _Torah-observant (Wilber):_ Most of church history held a middle position between antinomian and "pronomian" views, not a full repudiation. **[V]** https://davidwilber.com/articles/what-is-the-hebrew-roots-movement-and-why-i-dont-identify-with-it

---

## B7. Dispensational

**Who holds it.** Dallas Theological Seminary; Lewis Sperry Chafer; Charles Ryrie; Wayne G. Strickland ("A Dispensational View," in _Five Views_).

**Core texts.** Rom 6:14 ("not under law but under grace"); Rom 10:4; Gal 3:19–25; 2 Cor 3; John 1:17.

**The argument in its own terms.** God has run history through distinct stewardships. The Mosaic Law, the present age of grace, and the coming kingdom are "distinct and are not to be intermingled." The Mosaic law, including food laws, ended with Christ. Commands repeated in the New Testament bind because they are part of the law of Christ, not because Moses gave them. Salvation was always by grace through faith.

**Quotes**

> "We believe that three of these dispensations or rules of life are the subject of extended revelation in the Scriptures—namely, the dispensation of the Mosaic Law, the present dispensation of grace, and the future dispensation of the millennial kingdom. We believe that these are distinct and are not to be intermingled or confused, as they are chronologically successive."
> — Dallas Theological Seminary, Doctrinal Statement, Article V. **[V]** https://www.dts.edu/about/doctrinal-statement/

> "We believe that the dispensations are not ways of salvation nor different methods of administering the 'Covenant of Grace.'... salvation in the divine reckoning is always by grace through faith..."
> — Ibid. **[V]**

- Ryrie, "The End of the Law," _BSac_ 124 (1967): 239ff. Preview text describes the thesis that "the death of Christ brought an end to the Mosaic law (Rom 10:4) while at the same time recognizing that some of the commandments of that law are restated clearly and without change in the epistles." **[V-2nd, preview only]** https://www.galaxie.com/article/bsac124-495-05

**Strongest criticisms from others**

- _Messianic Jewish (UMJC statement):_ Classic dispensationalism "taught that the Mosaic Law had no claim at all on the believer," though "Many of today's Dispensationalists have abandoned this severe anti-law position." **[V]** (Cantor reprint.) Note: dispensationalists would dispute this description of their view as "anti-law."
- _Reformed:_ Separating the dispensations too sharply undercuts the unity of Scripture and the moral law. **[S]**

---

## B8. Seventh-day Adventist

**Who holds it.** The General Conference of Seventh-day Adventists, Fundamental Beliefs (FB 22 "Christian Behavior"; FB 19 "The Law of God"). Andrews University scholars: Jiří Moskala and David Merling.

**Core texts.** Gen 7:2 (clean animals before Sinai); Lev 11; Deut 14; 1 Cor 6:19–20; Rom 12:1–2 (listed with FB 22); Mark 7; Acts 10; Rom 14 (as reinterpreted).

**The argument in its own terms.** The body is the temple of the Holy Spirit, so believers care for it. The clean/unclean distinction existed before Israel (Noah knew it). It is therefore not a Jewish ceremony that ended with the temple. It is guidance for health and holiness, rooted in creation. Mark 7 is about ritual hand-washing and the "traditions of the elders." Acts 10 is about calling _people_ "common" (see Acts 10:28) **[S; the Biblical Research Institute article making this case could not be fetched (HTTP 429)]**. Romans 14 concerns food linked to idols and rabbinic "common" defilement, not the Levitical list. Salvation is by grace. Obedience is its fruit.

**Official quotes**

> "It also means that because our bodies are the temples of the Holy Spirit, we are to care for them intelligently. Along with adequate exercise and rest, we are to adopt the most healthful diet possible and abstain from the unclean foods identified in the Scriptures."
> — Seventh-day Adventist Fundamental Belief 22, "Christian Behavior." **[V]** https://www.nadadventist.org/beliefs/christian-behavior/

> "Salvation is all of grace and not of works, but its fruitage is obedience to the Commandments."
> — Fundamental Belief 19, "The Law of God." **[V]** https://www.nadadventist.org/beliefs/the-law-of-god/

> "At issue in Mark 7 are the 'traditions of the elders' (verses 3,5, 8, 9,13)."
> "Paul acknowledged that koinos, as a command, was not binding on Christians, but some in the church had not become free from the 'tradition of the elders.'"
> — David Merling, "Clean and unclean meat," _Ministry_ (June 1999). **[V]** https://www.ministrymagazine.org/archive/1999/06/clean-and-unclean-meat

> "I propose that this unifying criterion is a Creation theology which is linked to life and the primary rationale of the Mosaic dietary laws is respect for the Creator who is the Giver of life."
> — Jiří Moskala, _Biblical Research_ 46 (2001). **[V]**

**Strongest criticisms from others**

- _Catholic, Reformed, Lutheran, NCT:_ Mark 7:19 ("Thus he declared all foods clean," in most modern versions), Rom 14:14, 20 ("All things indeed are clean"), and 1 Tim 4:3–5 treat food distinctions as ended. Schreiner: Matthew "was well aware that the dissolution of the food laws was the implication of Jesus' statement." **[V]** (Schreiner SBTS paper.)
- _On Gen 7:2:_ Sprinkle notes that the distinction "first made in the account of Noah's flood" is "elaborated in detail in Leviticus 11." Most traditions read Noah's "clean" as about sacrifice (Gen 8:20), not diet. Genesis 9:3 gives Noah "every moving thing that lives" as food. **[V/S]**
- _Mainstream evangelical (AiG):_ The modern Hebrew Roots Movement "has been influenced in some ways by Seventh-Day Adventism." Adventists would stress their differences from that movement (they do not require the feasts, for example). **[V]** https://answersingenesis.org/presuppositions/dangers-hebrew-roots-movement/
- _Internal Adventist discussion:_ Some Adventist scholars frame the practice as "unhealthful" rather than "unclean." Merling cites John Brunt, "Unclean or Unhealthful? An Adventist Perspective," _Spectrum_ 11:3. **[V-2nd]**

---

## B9. Messianic Jewish (Torah observance as Jewish covenant identity)

**Who holds it.** Union of Messianic Jewish Congregations (UMJC). Mark S. Kinzer, _Postmissionary Messianic Judaism_ (Brazos, 2005). David J. Rudolph, _A Jew to the Jews: Jewish Contours of Pauline Flexibility in 1 Corinthians 9:19–23_ (Mohr Siebeck, 2011; 2nd ed. Pickwick, 2016). Messianic Jewish Theological Institute.

**Core texts.** Rom 11:29 ("the gifts and the calling of God are irrevocable"); Acts 21:20–24 (Paul shows he "walk[s] keeping the law"); 1 Cor 7:17–20 ("Was anyone called having been circumcised? Let him not become uncircumcised"); Acts 15 (a different rule for Gentiles); Matt 5:17–18; 1 Cor 9:19–23.

**The argument in its own terms.** God's covenant with Israel stands. Jews who follow Yeshua remain Jews and should live out Israel's covenant responsibilities, including kashrut, Shabbat, and the feasts. They do this as calling and identity, not to be saved. Gentile believers are joined to Israel's Messiah without becoming Jews, and they are not bound to the whole Torah. The church is "bilateral": one body with a Jewish expression and a Gentile expression. Rudolph argues that 1 Cor 9:19–23 does not show Paul dropping Torah. It shows a Torah-observant Jew accepting hospitality at many kinds of tables.

**Quotes**

> "The Union of Messianic Jewish Congregations (UMJC) envisions Messianic Judaism as a movement of Jewish congregations and groups committed to Yeshua the Messiah that embrace the covenantal responsibility of Jewish life and identity rooted in Torah, expressed in tradition, and renewed and applied in the context of the New Covenant."
> — UMJC, "Defining Messianic Judaism," affirmed by delegate vote, July 20, 2005. **[V]** https://www.umjc.org/defining-messianic-judaism

> "In short, we have argued for a bilateral ecclesiology in solidarity with Israel that affirms Israel's covenant, Torah, and religious tradition."
> — Mark Kinzer, _Postmissionary Messianic Judaism_. **[V-2nd]** as quoted in Jonathan Kaplan, "A Synopsis of Mark Kinzer's Post-Missionary Messianic Judaism," _Kesher_ 20 (2006). https://www.kesherjournal.com/article/a-synopsis-of-mark-kinzers-post-missionary-messianic-judaism/

> "we have good grounds for upholding the view that the New Testament as a whole treats Jewish practice as obligatory for Jews."
> — Kinzer, quoted ibid. **[V-2nd]**

> Rudolph's aim is to show "that scholars overstate their case when they maintain that 1 Cor 9:19-23 is incompatible with a Torah-observant Paul."
> — Rudolph, _A Jew to the Jews_, quoted by Lionel Windsor, _Themelios_ 36.3. **[V-2nd]** https://www.thegospelcoalition.org/themelios/review/a-jew-to-the-jews-jewish-contours-of-pauline-flexibility-in-1-corinthians-9/

> Windsor's summary: "The Mosaic law, therefore, applies to Jewish Christians and Gentile Christians in different ways. Paul understood this difference; hence he lived consistently as a Jew, but never insisted that Gentile converts do the same." **[V]** (reviewer's words)

**Strongest criticisms from others**

- _Evangelical (Windsor, Themelios):_ "Rudolph does not adequately deal with the soteriological implications of Paul's use of the word 'law'. He tends to skim past Paul's frequent (often negative) utterances concerning the relationship of the law to eschatological blessing and salvation." **[V]**
- _Catholic historical position:_ Aquinas q.103 a.4 and Florence (B1). **[V]**
- _NCT / Reformed:_ Keeping Jewish practice as obligatory risks rebuilding the "dividing wall" that Christ removed (Eph 2:14–15). It also conflicts with Gal 2:14, where Peter, "being a Jew," lived "as the Gentiles do." **[S]**
- _From within the Messianic movement:_ _Kesher_ 20 printed responses to Kinzer by David H. Stern, Daniel C. Juster, Mitch Glaser, R. Kendall Soulen, Douglas Harink, and Peter Hocken, plus Kinzer's rejoinder. Their content was not reviewed for this dossier. **[S]**
- _Jewish community:_ Many Jewish bodies do not regard Messianic Judaism as a form of Judaism. This is outside this dossier's scope and is noted only for context. **[S]**

---

## B10. Hebrew Roots / "One Law" / Torah-observant Gentile movements

**Who holds it.** TorahResource (Tim Hegg), _Fellow Heirs_ and _There Shall Be One Law_. Various independent teachers and ministries. David Wilber holds a related "pronomian" view but rejects the Hebrew Roots label. (Two-House / Ephraimite groups are a separate subgroup. Both Hegg and Wilber reject them.)

**Core texts (as their UMJC critics report them, verified).** Exod 12:49; Lev 24:22; Num 15:16 ("one law for the native-born and for the stranger"); the olive tree of Rom 11 (Gentiles grafted in); Matt 5:17–18; Acts 15:21 ("Moses... being read in the synagogues every Sabbath").

**The argument in its own terms.** There is one people of God and one Torah. Gentile believers are grafted into Israel. The Torah's rule of "one law" for native and stranger therefore applies to them. Salvation is by grace, not by Torah-keeping. But once saved, every believer should be discipled in the whole Torah, including the Sabbath, feasts, and food laws, as the way of blessing. Acts 15 set only the _entry_ requirements. Verse 21 expects Gentiles to keep learning Moses in the synagogue. Many historical Christians kept parts of Torah (for example Seventh Day Baptists, Quartodecimans, Nazarenes), so the practice is not new.

**Quotes**

> "At TorahResource, we believe the Scriptures (both Tanach and Apostolic Scriptures) are clear in stating that the people of God are one people, and that the Torah (the teaching of God) is applicable to all who are in Yeshua, regardless of their ethnicity, gender, or station in life. This position has become known as 'One Law,' which TorahResource affirms without reservation."
> — TorahResource, "One Law." **[V]** https://torahresource.com/about/one-law/

> "Whether Jew or Gentile, we are equally important and necessary in the family of God, for we have all been purchased with the same price..."
> — Tim Hegg, quoted on the same page. **[V]**

> "I am a follower of Yeshua (Jesus) the Messiah, and I believe the Torah (the Law of Moses) remains relevant for God's people today and should still be obeyed."
> — David Wilber, "What Is the Hebrew Roots Movement? (And Why I Don't Identify With It)." **[V]** https://davidwilber.com/articles/what-is-the-hebrew-roots-movement-and-why-i-dont-identify-with-it

**A fair statement from a critic (UMJC):** "One Law teachers agree that salvation is by grace, not based on observance of Torah... After acceptance into the body of Messiah through faith, however, everyone should be discipled to keep the whole Torah as the way of a blessed life." **[V]** (Cantor reprint.)

**Strongest criticism, Messianic Jewish leaders (UMJC):**

> "In so doing, these One Law movements not only misinterpret a great body of Scripture, but they also miss the unique calling of Jews and Gentiles within the Body of Messiah, robbing both groups of the biblical richness of their identity."
> — UMJC, "One Law Movements" (reprint). **[V]** https://www.roncantor.com/post/one-law-movements

The UMJC's exegetical points, in summary **[V]**:

- In Exod 12:43–48, the "one law" applied only to the _circumcised_ stranger. The uncircumcised stranger could not eat the Passover.
- Acts 15 laid on Gentiles "only four requirements."
- Gal 5:3 implies that without circumcision, Gentiles are not required to keep the whole Torah.
- "Paul never qualifies his argument" to say all believers must keep the whole Torah "for a discipled life of blessing."
- "unity of Jew and Gentile does not require that there be one set of commandments for both" (1 Cor 7:17–20).

**Strongest criticism, mainstream Christian (Answers in Genesis):**

> "The 'Jerusalem Council' rejected the notion that Gentile believers must follow the Law of Moses."
> — Tim Chaffey, "Dangers of the Hebrew Roots Movement," Answers in Genesis (2018; revised with an editor's note on fairness). **[V]** https://answersingenesis.org/presuppositions/dangers-hebrew-roots-movement/

Chaffey on Acts 15:21: "The reason for the restrictions was so that the Gentile Christians who were not under the Mosaic law would not unnecessarily offend the Jews." **[V]**

**Note on fairness.** AiG's own editor's note says some Hebrew Roots adherents felt "painted with a broad brush." The article was revised to clarify "that some adherents to the HRM may not agree with all of the positions outlined." **[V]** Wilber likewise separates Torah observance from other distinctives such as Sacred Name and Two-House teaching.

---

## B11. _Five Views on Law and Gospel_ as a map of the evangelical spread

**Bibliographic note.** Stanley N. Gundry, ed. (Zondervan). First published 1993 as _The Law, the Gospel, and the Modern Christian: Five Views_. Reissued 1996 in the Counterpoints series as _Five Views on Law and Gospel_. **[V]** (chapter titles from retailer and Monergism listings; contents summarized **[S]**)

| Contributor          | Chapter                                                                            | Food laws today (summary)                                                                |
| -------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Willem A. VanGemeren | The Non-Theonomic Reformed View                                                    | Ceremonial, fulfilled in Christ; moral law continues (as WCF)                            |
| Greg L. Bahnsen      | The Theonomic Reformed View                                                        | Ceremonial laws fulfilled; judicial laws still normative for society                     |
| Walter C. Kaiser Jr. | The Law as God's Gracious Guidance for the Promotion of Holiness                   | Moral law continues. Ceremonial and civil laws apply through their underlying principles |
| Wayne G. Strickland  | A Dispensational View                                                              | Mosaic law ended; law of Christ                                                          |
| Douglas J. Moo       | The Law of Christ as the Fulfillment of the Law of Moses: A Modified Lutheran View | Mosaic law belonged to the era before Christ; binds only as fulfilled in Christ          |

**What the book shows.** No contributor argues that Gentile Christians must keep Leviticus 11. The debate is about _how_ the Old Testament law carries over (moral core, principle, or Christ's law), not _whether_ the food list binds.

---

# PART C — Romans 14 itself

## C1. Who are "the weak"?

Paul describes the weak as one who "eats only vegetables" (14:2) and one who "esteems one day as more important" (14:5). He says "nothing is unclean of itself" (14:14) and "All things indeed are clean" (14:20). He places himself among "we who are strong" (15:1). (WEB)

A survey note: "Moo lists six options, Reasoner five, Cranfield six, etc.—and ironically, these lists do not correspond to one another." **[V]** Mark A. Snoeberger, "Weakness or Wisdom? Fundamentalists and Romans 14.1–15.13," _DBSJ_ 12 (2007): 29–49. https://dbts.edu/wp-content/uploads/2025/10/Weakness-of-Wisdom-Fundamentalists-and-Romans-14-1-15-13-Snoeberger.pdf

### View 1: Jewish Christians (and Gentile sympathizers) who still keep Torah food and day laws. This is the majority view.

**Held by.** C. E. B. Cranfield (ICC, 1975–79, 2:690–698). James D. G. Dunn (WBC 38B, 1988, esp. p. 798). Douglas Moo (NICNT, 1996, pp. 828–829). Thomas Schreiner (BECNT, 1998). John Chrysostom (Homily 25 on Romans). **[S for Cranfield, Dunn, Moo; V for Schreiner and Chrysostom]**

> "I argue in the exegesis of 14:1–15:13 that 'the weak' are primarily composed of Jewish believers."
> — Schreiner, _Romans_ (BECNT, 1998), Introduction, n. 24. **[V]** https://www.christianstudylibrary.org/files/pub/book_previews/Thomas-R-Schreiner_Romans_Introduction-to-Romans.pdf

> "There were many of the Jews which believed, who adhered of conscience to the Law, and after their believing, still kept to the observance of meats, as not having courage yet to quit the service of the Law entirely. Then that they might not be observed if they kept from swine's flesh only, they abstained in consequence from all flesh, and ate herbs only..."
> — John Chrysostom, Homily 25 on Romans (NPNF¹ 11). **[V]** https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/210225.htm

Nanos describes this consensus: "The 'weak' are also understood to be Christ-believers, but in contrast, they still observe Torah, and probably consist mostly of Jews, with some 'God-fearing' Gentiles perhaps included among them." **[V]** (Nanos 2008, below)

Nuance within the view: Snoeberger reports that "Dunn does not find 'weakness' as something truly 'defective,' and apparently allows it to be consistent with justifying faith." **[V-2nd]** (see Flag 7 on the page citation)

**Criticism.** Adventists (Merling) say the issue is idol-linked or "common" food, not Levitical law. Nanos says the reading assumes, in a circle, that Paul was "Torah-free." Karris says Paul is not describing a real Roman group at all (View 3).

### View 2: Gentile Christians anxious about idol-offered meat, or ascetics

**Held by.** Readers who see Rom 14 as parallel to 1 Cor 8–10. Some older scholars proposed a syncretistic or ascetic group (Käsemann, Barrett, Black, per Snoeberger). A. Andrew Das argues that both groups were Gentile **[S, via search summary only]**. The Adventist reading (Merling) also belongs here in part.

> Snoeberger: "Others have conceded that the 'weak' may be Jews, but must represent some syncretistic, ascetic, or otherwise heterodox sect thereof (see, e.g., Ernst Käsemann... Black... Barrett...)." **[V]**

**Criticism.** Rom 14:5 on days and 14:14 on "unclean" (_koinos_) sound Jewish, not pagan. Paul never mentions idols in Rom 14, although 1 Cor 8 does. **[S]**

### View 3: A general teaching, not a report on a specific group

**Held by.** Robert J. Karris, "Rom 14:1–15:13 and the Occasion of Romans," _CBQ_ 35 (1973): 155–178; Wayne Meeks.

> Snoeberger: "Robert J. Karris, for instance, opines that Paul is addressing in general terms a hypothetical scenario to which specificity is assigned in 1 Corinthians 8, 10." **[V]**

**Criticism.** Romans 16 greets many named people. Paul seems to know the Roman house churches, so the section fits a real situation (Schreiner, Wright). **[S]**

### View 4: Mark Nanos. The "weak in faith" are non-Christ-believing Jews in the Roman synagogues.

**Held by.** Mark D. Nanos (Jewish NT scholar), _The Mystery of Romans_ (Fortress, 1996), pp. 85–165; "The Jewish Context of the Gentile Audience Addressed in Paul's Letter to the Romans," _CBQ_ 61 (1999).

> "...the distinction between the groups is with respect to their present level of 'ability' or 'inability' to believe in Christ. 'Stumbling' or not over Christ, and the proclamation of the message of good in Christ to the nations, is at issue. They are in this way 'weak' or 'impaired' or 'stumbling' with respect to faith in Christ, but they are not non-believers in God."
> — Mark D. Nanos, "The Myth of the 'Law-Free' Paul Standing Between Christians and Jews" (web version, 2008). **[V]** https://pdfs.semanticscholar.org/7013/c2be570d5f296f62bf73ef78737db3256e5d.pdf

> "These characteristics appear to be typical norms for Jewish behavior, such as eating vegetables when properly koshered meat is not available, avoiding wine that may have been libated to the gods according to normal Greco-Roman practice, and Jewish calendrical observances, including Sabbath."
> — Ibid. **[V]**

> "I also propose that Paul's appeal to the inherent purity or even goodness of everything God created is not a rejection of halakhic behavior."
> — Ibid. **[V]**

**Criticism.**

- Schreiner: "It seems historically incredible that Gentile Christians were the dominant group (per Nanos's reconstruction) in a Jewish synagogue. Indeed, the presence of house churches in Rom. 16 indicates that the churches were not still part of the synagogue." **[V]**
- Robert A. J. Gagnon, "Why the 'Weak' at Rome Cannot Be Non-Christian Jews." The title and Nanos's 2000 rejoinder are confirmed in Nanos's footnote 42. The journal details (_CBQ_ 62, 2000) are from memory. **[UNVERIFIED]**
- Paul calls the weak a "brother... for whom Christ died" (14:15). Most readers take that to mean a believer. **[S]**

### View 5: N. T. Wright. Mixed groups, with the labels deliberately not ethnic.

**Held by.** N. T. Wright, "Romans and the Theology of Paul," in _Pauline Theology_ III (Fortress, 1995), 30–67; also _New Interpreter's Bible_ X (2002).

> "What Paul does, of course, is explicitly not to discuss these issues in terms of 'Jewish Christians' and 'Gentile Christians' but to line them up in terms borrowed from his (somewhat different) discussion in 1 Corinthians 8, where he had spoken of the 'strong' and the 'weak,' both of which categories almost certainly included Gentile Christians, and both of which likewise may well have contained Jewish Christians. Paul refuses to reinforce a potential split by addressing different groups within the church in terms of their ethnic origins..."
> — Wright, "Romans and the Theology of Paul." **[V]** https://ntwrightpage.com/wp-content/uploads/2016/05/Wright_Romans_Theology_Paul.pdf

> "The covenant that the creator made with Abraham has been fulfilled in Christ, and a multiethnic people is the result; one must not, for the sake of human regulations, destroy this unique and climactic work of the creator god."
> — Ibid. **[V]**

Wright also calls 15:7–13 "the real summing-up of the entire letter." **[V]**

**Criticism.** Nanos: the view still assumes a "Torah-free" Paul. Schreiner and others see a clearer Jewish-believer profile in the weak. **[S]**

### Mark Reasoner (for completeness)

Mark Reasoner, _The Strong and the Weak: Romans 14.1–15.13 in Context_ (SNTSMS 103, Cambridge, 1999). He reads the labels against Roman social status. His review of earlier views appears at pp. 1–22. **[V]** (existence and scope, via BMCR review) https://bmcr.brynmawr.edu/2000/2000.04.11/

## C2. What Paul says is NOT negotiable in the same letter

The key texts are Rom 3:31, 7:12, 13:8–10, and 14:17. In WEB: "Do we then nullify the law through faith? May it never be! No, we establish the law" (3:31). "Therefore the law indeed is holy, and the commandment holy, and righteous, and good" (7:12). "Owe no one anything, except to love one another; for he who loves his neighbor has fulfilled the law... 'You shall love your neighbor as yourself.' Love doesn't harm a neighbor. Love therefore is the fulfillment of the law" (13:8–10). "God's Kingdom is not eating and drinking, but righteousness, peace, and joy in the Holy Spirit" (14:17). Also: "whatever is not of faith is sin" (14:23); "Therefore accept one another, even as Christ also accepted you" (15:7).

**Non-negotiables inside Rom 14–15 itself (all traditions read these plainly):**

1. Do not despise and do not judge (14:3, 10, 13).
2. Each person answers to the Lord (14:4, 12).
3. Act from conviction and faith, not doubt (14:5, 22–23).
4. Do not destroy a brother "for whom Christ died" (14:15).
5. Pursue peace and building up (14:19).
6. The strong bear with the weak, as Christ did not please himself (15:1–3).
7. Welcome one another for God's glory (15:7).

**How traditions use Rom 3:31 and 13:8–10 [S, with verified anchors]:**

- _Catholic:_ The Golden Rule and charity's respect for conscience are rules that "apply in every case" (CCC 1789, quoting Rom 14:21). **[V]**
- _Reformed and Wesleyan:_ "We establish the law" means the moral law is upheld (WCF 19.5; Wesley Sermon 25). **[V]**
- _Lutheran:_ Luther builds the second thesis of _Freedom_ on Rom 13:8 ("Owe no man anything, but to love one another"). **[V]**
- _Messianic Jewish:_ The law is upheld in its covenant form for Jews. **[S]**
- _One Law:_ The Torah is upheld for all believers. **[S]**
- _Jewish:_ Rabbi Akiva calls Lev 19:18 "an all-embracing principle in the Torah" (Sifra). **[V]** (see D3)

## C3. How 1 Corinthians 8–10 and Galatians 2:11–14 relate

**1 Corinthians 8–10 (idol food).** Paul says "food will not commend us to God" (8:8). He warns: "be careful that by no means does this liberty of yours become a stumbling block to the weak" (8:9). He adds: "if food causes my brother to stumble, I will eat no meat forever more" (8:13). And: "whatever you do, do all to the glory of God. Give no occasion for stumbling, either to Jews, or to Greeks, or to the assembly of God" (10:31–32). (WEB)

- _Common view:_ Rom 14 reuses the pastoral pattern of 1 Cor 8–10 (Karris; Wright says the labels are "borrowed" from 1 Cor 8). **[V]**
- _Rudolph (Messianic Jewish):_ "Paul's whole approach to idol-food fits well within the bounds of Torah-observant Judaism... Paul's instructions can, in fact, be read as a contextualised application of the apostolic decree (Acts 15)." **[V]** (Windsor's summary of Rudolph)
- _Adventist (Merling):_ 1 Cor 8 "specifically cautions those who think idols are nothing." He reads Rom 14 in the same idol and "common" defilement context. **[V]**
- _Calvin:_ Offence given versus offence taken; yield to the weak, not to Pharisees (3.19.11). **[V]**

**Galatians 2:11–14 (the Antioch incident).** Peter ate with Gentiles, then "drew back and separated himself" when men came from James. Paul asked: "If you, being a Jew, live as the Gentiles do, and not as the Jews do, why do you compel the Gentiles to live as the Jews do?" (WEB)

- _Wright:_ "The so-called Antioch incident of Galatians 2 reflects Paul's opposition to any sense that Jewish Christians are superior to Gentile Christians." Romans faced "the mirror image": Gentile believers who might sideline Jews. **[V]**
- _Nanos:_ Paul's objection was not that Peter kept Jewish food norms. It was that Peter's withdrawal denied Gentiles' full standing in Christ _as Gentiles_. (See Nanos, "What Was at Stake in Peter's 'Eating with Gentiles' at Antioch?", in _The Galatians Debate_.) **[S]**
- _Aquinas:_ Reports the old Jerome–Augustine dispute over Gal 2 and sides with Augustine's "middle period." **[V]** (q.103 a.4 ad 1)
- _Luther (Freedom):_ Paul had Timothy circumcised for the sake of the weak (Acts 16:3). He refused circumcision for Titus when "hardened self-justifiers" demanded it (Gal 2:3). Paul was "sparing the weak for the time, and always resisting the hardened." **[V]**

**Shared point across readings.** Whatever each tradition thinks about Torah, all read Gal 2 as teaching one thing. The table must not become a way to rank believers or to force Gentiles to "live as the Jews do" in order to belong. **[S]**

## C4. Luther's two theses from _The Freedom of a Christian_ (1520), and how traditions use them

> "A Christian man is the most free lord of all, and subject to none; a Christian man is the most dutiful servant of all, and subject to every one."
> — Luther, _Concerning Christian Liberty_ (1520). Gutenberg #1911; translator unnamed, wording matches Wace and Buchheim. **[V]** https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/1911
> Luther grounds these theses in 1 Cor 9:19 and Rom 13:8. **[V]**
> Modern _LW_ 31:344 wording: "A Christian is a perfectly free lord of all, subject to none. A Christian is a perfectly dutiful servant of all, subject to all." **[UNVERIFIED here; standard citation]**

Luther applies the theses directly to Rom 14:

> "...we may meet with simple-minded and ignorant persons, weak in the faith, as the Apostle calls them, who are as yet unable to apprehend that liberty of faith, even if willing to do so. These we must spare, lest they should be offended... we must keep fasts and do other things which they consider necessary. This is required of us by charity, which injures no one, but serves all men."
> — Ibid. **[V]**

> "If you wish to use your liberty, do it secretly, as Paul says, 'Hast thou faith? have it to thyself before God' (Rom. xiv. 22). But take care not to use it in the presence of the weak."
> — Ibid. **[V]**

**How each tradition uses the same pair: freedom from rules, and service to others.**

- _Lutheran:_ This is the confessional pattern. The Formula of Concord SD X.9 yields to "the weak in faith in such external adiaphora" (Rom 14; 1 Cor 9:19). It refuses to yield when compulsion threatens the gospel (SD X.10). **[V]**
- _Reformed:_ Calvin's 3.19 follows the same logic. Liberty in "meats, days, and habits," limited by love for the weak but not by the "austerity of Pharisees." The WCF adds that "God alone is Lord of the conscience" (20.2). **[V]**
- _Catholic:_ The Catechism frames the New Law as "a law of freedom" (CCC 1972). It states that "charity always proceeds by way of respect for one's neighbor and his conscience" (CCC 1789, quoting Rom 14:21). The Catholic Church does not adopt Luther's theses as such. It reaches a similar pairing of freedom and charity through Aquinas and the New Law. **[V anchors / S framing]**
- _Orthodox:_ Freedom is received as freedom _from the passions_ through discipline. Fasting is done "without ostentation or accusation of others (Mt 6.16; Rom 14)." **[V]**
- _Messianic Jewish (Rudolph):_ Reads 1 Cor 9:19–23 ("free from all... servant to all") as a Torah-observant Jew's flexibility in hospitality, not as freedom from Torah. **[V]**
- _Adventist and One Law:_ Accept "servant of all" and "do not despise." They deny that Levitical food law is among the "things indifferent" that freedom covers. **[S]**
- _Anabaptist:_ Stresses the second thesis, costly service and discipleship in community. No direct Anabaptist commentary on the _Freedom_ treatise was fetched. **[S]**

---

# PART D — THE AGREEMENTS

Each agreement below has sources from at least three traditions. "Essentially all" means every tradition surveyed in Part B, plus Jewish sources where they are cited. Exceptions are stated plainly.

### D1. The food laws came from God, and God's law is holy and good.

- _Jewish:_ "we didn't make up the kashrut laws" (Freeman, Chabad). "...how much more so with regard to the mitzvot which God ordained for us should a person not treat them derisively" (Maimonides, MT Me'ilah 8:8). **[V]**
- _Catholic:_ "the Law is holy, spiritual, and good" (CCC 1963). Old Testament books "retain a permanent value" (CCC 121). **[V]**
- _Reformed:_ "God was pleased to give to the people of Israel... ceremonial laws" (WCF 19.3). Their abolition is "so far from derogating from their sanctity" (Calvin 2.7.16). **[V]**
- _Lutheran:_ "I want to keep Moses and not sweep him under the rug" (Luther 1525). **[V]**
- _Methodist:_ "The Old Testament is not contrary to the New" (Art. VI). **[V]**
- _Early church (Barnabas 10):_ "Is there then not a command of God they should not eat [these things]? There is..." **[V]**
- _Adventist, Messianic Jewish, One Law:_ hold the laws as God's instruction (FB 22; UMJC; TorahResource). **[V]**
- _Scripture all Christians share:_ "the law indeed is holy, and the commandment holy, and righteous, and good" (Rom 7:12, WEB).

**Where they differ:** whether the laws still bind, and whom.

### D2. The stated purpose of Leviticus 11 is holiness: "be holy, for I am holy" (Lev 11:44–45; 20:26).

- _Jewish:_ Rashi on 20:26 (separation "should be for My sake"). Artson: "Kashrut is a way of welcoming the holiness of Judaism into our daily lives." **[V]**
- _New Testament:_ 1 Pet 1:15–16 quotes Leviticus directly to Christians: "because it is written, 'You shall be holy; for I am holy'" (WEB). All Christian traditions accept this text.
- _Catholic:_ Aquinas, commenting on Rom 7:12, links "holy" to the ceremonial precepts (q.99 a.4). **[V]**
- _Evangelical scholars:_ "the most important message conveyed by these laws is that God is holy" (Sprinkle). **[V]**
- _Adventist:_ the laws express "respect for the Creator who is the Giver of life" (Moskala). **[V]**
- _Scholars of every kind:_ Milgrom (life), Douglas (wholeness), Wenham (separation). All explain holiness; none replaces it. **[V/S]**

**Where they differ:** whether Christian holiness still includes the food list (Adventist, One Law, and Messianic Jews for Jews) or is now expressed in "evil thoughts" coming "out of the heart" (Mark 7; CCC 582).

### D3. "Love your neighbor as yourself" (Lev 19:18) still stands, and all agree it sums up the law.

- _Jewish:_ "R. Akiva says: This is an all-embracing principle in the Torah" (Sifra, Kedoshim 4:12, trans. Silverstein). **[V]** Hillel: "That which is hateful to you do not do to another; that is the entire Torah, and the rest is its interpretation. Go study" (Shabbat 31a, Davidson ed.). **[V]** https://www.sefaria.org/Shabbat.31a
- _New Testament:_ Rom 13:8–10; Mark 12:31; Gal 5:14.
- _Catholic:_ the Golden Rule is among rules that "apply in every case" (CCC 1789). The Law of the Gospel "is summed up in the Golden Rule" (CCC 1970). **[V]**
- _Reformed:_ "The moral law doth forever bind all" (WCF 19.5). **[V]**
- _Lutheran:_ Luther: "love one another" [John 15:12] "pertains to me, for it pertains to all who belong to the gospel" (1525). **[V]**
- _Methodist:_ "no Christian whatsoever is free from the obedience of the commandments which are called moral" (Art. VI). **[V]**
- _Adventist:_ God's law is "binding upon all people in every age" (FB 19). **[V]**
- _Messianic Jewish (UMJC):_ Acts 15 "assumes a universal morality." **[V]**

**Where they differ:** almost nowhere on this point. It is the strongest agreement in the dossier.

### D4. Food is God's good gift, and eating is done before God with thanksgiving.

- _Scripture:_ "He who eats, eats to the Lord, for he gives God thanks" (Rom 14:6). "For every creature of God is good, and nothing is to be rejected, if it is received with thanksgiving" (1 Tim 4:4, WEB).
- _Jewish:_ "Even the seemingly mundane activity of eating is a G‑dly act" (Chabad, "What Is Kosher?"). **[V]**
- _Catholic:_ "every creature of God is good and nothing is to be rejected if it is received with thanksgiving" (Florence, 1442). **[V]**
- _Orthodox canon law:_ one who abstains from flesh or wine "as abhorring them, forgetting that God made all things very good" is to be corrected (Apostolic Canon 51). "There is nothing essentially wrong with meat and dairy products, in and of themselves" (OCA Q&A). **[V]**
- _A Catholic anthropologist on Leviticus itself:_ "in Leviticus the unclean animals are not abominable" (Douglas 1999). **[V]**
- _Lutheran:_ AC XXVI rejects forbidding foods as a way to merit grace (1 Tim 4:1–3). **[V]**

**Where they differ:** whether "good creature" means "permitted to eat." Jewish, Adventist, and One Law readers say an animal can be good and still not food for God's people (compare Douglas). Others say all foods are now clean.

### D5. Self-restraint in eating is a spiritual good.

- _Jewish:_ Philo: the laws curb foods "calculated to excite treacherous pleasure" (_Spec. Laws_ 4.100). Ramban: holiness means "moderation even in matters which are permitted" (on Lev 19:2). **[V]**
- _Catholic:_ Aquinas gives as one reason for the food laws "to prevent excessive care about food" (q.102 a.6 ad 1). Fasting and abstinence "help us acquire mastery over our instincts and freedom of heart" (CCC 2043). **[V]**
- _Orthodox:_ "The purpose of fasting is... to learn discipline" (OCA). **[V]**
- _Lutheran:_ "every Christian ought to train and subdue himself with bodily restraints" (AC XXVI.33). **[V]**
- _Adventist:_ "whatever brings our thoughts and bodies into the discipline of Christ" (FB 22). **[V]**

**Where they differ:** the form (a Levitical list, set fast days, or personal discipline) and whether it may be required.

### D6. Conscience must not be trampled. Do not put a stumbling block before another.

- _Jewish:_ "And you shall not put a stumbling block before the blind" (Lev 19:14). The Talmud applies this to handing "a cup of wine to a nazirite" or forbidden meat to a Noahide, that is, helping another person break their own obligation (Avodah Zarah 6b, Davidson ed.). **[V, short]** https://www.sefaria.org/Avodah_Zarah.6b
- _Catholic:_ "charity always proceeds by way of respect for one's neighbor and his conscience... Therefore 'it is right not to . . . do anything that makes your brother stumble'" (CCC 1789, citing 1 Cor 8:12 and Rom 14:21). **[V]**
- _Orthodox:_ fasting "without ostentation or accusation of others (Mt 6.16; Rom 14)" (OCA). **[V]**
- _Reformed:_ "God alone is Lord of the conscience" (WCF 20.2). Liberty "ought to submit to the ignorance of weak brethren" (Calvin 3.19.11). **[V]**
- _Lutheran:_ "we must bear with their infirmity" (Luther, _Freedom_). One may "yield and give way with a good conscience to the weak in faith" (FC SD X.9). **[V]**
- _Patristic:_ Chrysostom on Rom 14: "For sinning is not so ruinous as leading others also into the same." **[V]** (Homily 25)
- _Adventist:_ "Paul wrote the Roman Christians not to do anything that would weaken the faith of their fellow Christians" (Merling). **[V]**
- _Jewish scholar reading Paul (Nanos):_ the strong must "respect the 'weak.'" **[V]**

**Where they differ:** who counts as "weak," and whether the weak or the strong holds the correct view of food. Every tradition still names the same duty.

### D7. Among Christian traditions: food and days do not earn salvation. "God's Kingdom is not eating and drinking" (Rom 14:17).

- _Catholic:_ whoever "submits himself to them as necessary for salvation and as if faith in Christ without them could not save, sins mortally" (Florence). The error condemned is making them _necessary for salvation_. **[V]**
- _Lutheran:_ "by the observance of human traditions we cannot merit grace or be justified" (AC XXVI.21). **[V]**
- _Reformed:_ believers are "not under the law, as a covenant of works, to be thereby justified" (WCF 19.6). **[V]**
- _Dispensational:_ "salvation... is always by grace through faith" (DTS Art. V). **[V]**
- _Adventist:_ "Salvation is all of grace and not of works" (FB 19). **[V]**
- _Orthodox:_ the rules are "not as an end in themselves, but as a means to spiritual perfection crowned in love" (OCA). **[V]**
- _Messianic Jewish:_ Rudolph holds that Paul "did not view Jewish Torah-observance as a means of eschatological salvation" (Windsor's summary). **[V]**
- _One Law:_ "One Law teachers agree that salvation is by grace, not based on observance of Torah" (as reported by their UMJC critics). **[V]**

**Limit:** This is a Christian agreement. Judaism does not frame kashrut as "earning salvation," so the question is posed differently. It is not listed as a Jewish–Christian agreement.

### D8. Gentiles do not have to become Jews to belong to God.

- _Jewish:_ "Moses only gave the Torah and mitzvot as an inheritance to Israel... and to all those who desire to convert... However, someone who does not desire to accept Torah and mitzvot, should not be forced to." A Gentile who keeps the seven Noahide commandments "will merit a share in the world to come" (Maimonides, MT Kings and Wars 8:10–11, trans. Touger). **[V, short]** https://www.sefaria.org/Mishneh_Torah%2C_Kings_and_Wars.8.10 . "Non-Jews fulfill their Divine mission—and earn their portion in the World to Come—by observing the Seven Noahide Laws" (Yehuda Shurpin, Chabad). **[V]** https://www.chabad.org/library/article_cdo/aid/7171140/jewish/Non-Jews-and-Shabbat.htm
- _Scripture:_ "my judgment is that we don't trouble those from among the Gentiles who turn to God" (Acts 15:19). "to lay no greater burden on you than these necessary things" (Acts 15:28, WEB).
- _Catholic:_ the Acts 15 decree was "suited to that time when a single church was rising from Jews and gentiles" (Florence). **[V]**
- _Lutheran:_ "Here Peter forbids to burden the consciences with many rites, either of Moses or of others" (AC XXVI.28). **[V]**
- _Methodist:_ Wesley: the apostles declared "that to command them to keep this law, was to 'subvert their souls'" (Sermon 25). **[V]**
- _Messianic Jewish:_ Acts 15 laid on Gentiles "only four requirements" (UMJC). Paul "never insisted that Gentile converts do the same" (Rudolph, per Windsor). **[V]**
- _Evangelical:_ "The 'Jerusalem Council' rejected the notion that Gentile believers must follow the Law of Moses" (Chaffey, AiG). **[V]**

**Exceptions, stated plainly:**

- One Law / Hebrew Roots teachers agree Gentiles need not be circumcised to be saved. But they hold the Torah, food laws included, "applicable to all who are in Yeshua" (TorahResource). They read Acts 15:21 as expecting Gentiles to keep learning Moses.
- Adventists agree Gentiles need not become Jews. They hold the clean/unclean distinction as older than Israel and binding on all.
- The Jewish parallel is real but not identical. Judaism's reason is the Noahide covenant, not Acts 15.

### D9. Jesus shared tables with people others considered unclean or sinners. Table fellowship across boundaries matters.

- _Scripture:_ "many tax collectors and sinners sat down with Jesus and his disciples... 'Why is it that he eats and drinks with tax collectors and sinners?'" (Mark 2:15–16, WEB).
- _Catholic:_ "Jesus scandalized the Pharisees by eating with tax collectors and sinners as familiarly as with themselves" (CCC 588). **[V]** https://www.vatican.va/archive/ENG0015/__P1N.HTM
- _Evangelical Anglican:_ Wenham on the Samaritan woman (John 4:9): "By his request Jesus demonstrated his indifference to the uncleanness rules that were accepted by stricter Jews." **[V]** (EvQ 1981)
- _Messianic Jewish:_ Rudolph's Paul "imitates the gospel-tradition concerning Christ's accommodation towards others and open table-fellowship" (Windsor's summary). **[V]**
- _Wright:_ Paul opposed "the continuing separation of Jews and Gentiles within the Christian fellowship" at Antioch. **[V]**

**Limit:** Christian and Messianic Jewish sources only. No Jewish communal source was fetched for this point. Do not present it as a Jewish–Christian agreement. Traditions also differ on its meaning. Wenham reads "indifference" to purity rules. Rudolph and Nanos read accommodation _within_ Torah observance.

### D10. The Old Testament remains Scripture for Christians, to be read, preached, and loved.

- _Catholic:_ CCC 121 (above). **[V]**
- _Lutheran:_ Moses read for promises of Christ and "beautiful examples of faith, of love, and of the cross" (Luther 1525). **[V]**
- _Reformed:_ the third use of the law is "the principal one" (Calvin 2.7.12). **[V]**
- _Methodist:_ Art. VI. **[V]**
- _Mennonite:_ Old and New Testaments inspired; "Scripture as a whole has its center and fulfillment in him" (Art. 4). **[V]**
- _NCT:_ the Mosaic law "carries the authority of Scripture; it prophecies of Christ; it shows us how to live" (Graham, TGC Canada). **[V]**
- _Adventist, Messianic Jewish, One Law:_ hold it as fully authoritative. **[V]**

### D11 (near-agreement, with real exceptions). Setting apart holy time is good, and the Sabbath commandment still speaks.

- _Jewish:_ Shabbat. _Catholic:_ "In Christ's Passover, Sunday fulfills the spiritual truth of the Jewish sabbath" (CCC 2175). _Lutheran:_ the commandment's "gross sense" does not bind, yet holy days serve rest and the Word (Large Catechism §§82–83). _Reformed:_ Sabbath as "a positive, moral, and perpetual commandment" (WCF 21.7, as quoted by Wilber). _Orthodox:_ Wednesday and Friday fasts (Didache 8; Apostolic Canon 69). _Adventist and One Law:_ the seventh-day Sabbath. Rom 14:6: "He who observes the day, observes it to the Lord." **[V except WCF 21.7 V-2nd]**
- **Exception:** Traditions disagree on which day, whether it binds, and whether Col 2:16–17 ("a shadow of the things to come") ends the Sabbath as a law. Present this as a shared practice with different theologies, not a doctrinal agreement.

---

# PART E — Where they differ (short reference)

1. **Do Lev 11 food laws bind Christians?** No: Catholic, Orthodox, Reformed, Lutheran, Anabaptist, Methodist, NCT, Dispensational. Yes, for all: Adventist, One Law. Yes for Jewish believers, no for Gentiles: Messianic Jewish (UMJC, Kinzer, Rudolph).
2. **What does Mark 7:19 mean?** "Declared all foods clean" (CCC 582; Schreiner) versus a comment on digestion and hand-washing tradition (Merling; many One Law and Messianic readers). This is partly a question of Greek syntax and translation (Flag 13).
3. **Is the law divided into moral, ceremonial, and judicial parts?** Yes: Aquinas, WCF, Methodist Art. VI, Wesley. Not as a key to what continues: Lutheran (natural law instead), NCT (covenant as a unit), Messianic Jewish (UMJC calls the "dyad" unsustainable if Israel is not replaced), Jewish.
4. **Who are "the weak"?** Torah-keeping Jewish believers (majority); idol-food scruples (Adventist, some others); a general case (Karris); non-believing Jews (Nanos); a deliberately non-ethnic mix (Wright).
5. **Is Torah observance by Jewish believers today faithful or mistaken?** Faithful calling: Messianic Jewish. Historically condemned: Aquinas, Florence (modern Catholic position open). Allowed but not required, a matter of liberty: many evangelicals **[S]**. Required for all: One Law.
6. **Blood (Acts 15:20).** Still in force in Orthodox canon law (Trullo 67). Ceased with its cause: Florence. Treated as a Noahide-type universal rule: Wenham, UMJC.
7. **What was the reason for Lev 11?** Health (Maimonides, partly Adventist), symbol (Aristeas, Philo, Barnabas), categories (Douglas 1966), protection of creatures (Douglas 1999), reverence for life (Milgrom), separation (Wenham), decree (Rashi, Chabad). All accept holiness as the stated goal.
