| 10  | Augustine                     | Church Father, 412                      | On the Spirit and the Letter 19.34                | Law and grace                  | VERIFIED                               |
| 11  | John Bunyan                   | Puritan, 1666                           | Grace Abounding §229                              | Law and grace                  | VERIFIED                               |
| 12  | Augustine                     | Church Father, c. 420                   | City of God 16.37                                 | Birthright, blessing, garments | VERIFIED                               |
| 13  | Ambrose (quoted by Calvin)    | Church Father c. 387 / Reformation 1559 | De Iacob 2.2.9; Institutes 3.11.23                | Jacob in Esau's clothes        | VERIFIED                               |
| 14  | C. H. Spurgeon                | Modern (Victorian Baptist)              | MTP No. 3219                                      | Peniel                         | VERIFIED                               |
| 15  | R. Shimon bar Yochai (Sifrei) | Jewish, rabbinic, 2nd c.                | Sifrei Bamidbar 69:2                              | Esau's embrace (Gen 33:4)      | VERIFIED                               |
| 16  | John Calvin                   | Reformation, 1554                       | Commentary on Genesis 33:10                       | Gen 33:10                      | VERIFIED                               |
| 17  | C. H. Spurgeon                | Modern, 1888                            | MTP No. 2015 "The Rent Veil"                      | Torn veil                      | VERIFIED                               |
| 18  | John Chrysostom               | Church Father, c. 403                   | Homily 16 on Hebrews §2                           | Heb 9:15–17                    | VERIFIED                               |
| 19  | George MacDonald              | Modern, 1889                            | Unspoken Sermons III, "The Inheritance"           | Co-heirs (Rom 8:17)            | VERIFIED                               |
| 20  | Dietrich Bonhoeffer           | Modern, 1939 / Eng. 1954                | Life Together, ch. 4, p. 100                      | Gal 6:2                        | VERIFIED (copyright: short quote only) |

Coverage: Church Fathers (Barnabas, Irenaeus, Augustine x2, Chrysostom x2, Ambrose). Medieval (Aquinas, Maimonides). Reformation (Luther, Calvin x2). Puritan (Bunyan). Modern (Spurgeon x2, MacDonald, Bonhoeffer). Jewish voices on the food laws: Aristeas, Sifra, Maimonides (three). A fourth Jewish voice (Sifrei) speaks to Esau's embrace.

---

## Part 1 — Leviticus 11: the clean and the unclean

### 1. Letter of Aristeas §144

> "For you must not fall into the degrading idea that it was out of regard to mice and weasels and other such things that Moses drew up his laws with such exceeding care. All these ordinances were made for the sake of righteousness to aid the quest for virtue and the perfecting of character."

- **Author / work / date:** Anonymous Alexandrian Jewish author writing as "Aristeas." The speaker is Eleazar the high priest. Usually dated 2nd century BCE (range debated, c. 250–100 BCE).
- **Locator:** §144 (the section numbers in the HTML are marginal and fall slightly off the sentence starts because of line wrapping).
- **Source fetched:** https://www.ccel.org/c/charles/otpseudepig/aristeas.htm
- **Translation:** H. T. Andrews, in R. H. Charles (ed.), _The Apocrypha and Pseudepigrapha of the Old Testament_, vol. 2 (Oxford: Clarendon, 1913). Public domain. Thackeray's 1904/1917 translation words it differently (not checked).
- **Also verified, §168–169 (closing summary, 25 words):** "And so concerning meats and things unclean, creeping things, and wild beasts, the whole system aims at righteousness and righteous relationships between man and man."
- **Also verified, §154 (on chewing the cud):** "For the act of chewing the cud is nothing else than the reminiscence of life and existence."
- **Why this voice:** The oldest surviving Jewish explanation of Leviticus 11, and it insists the food laws are about justice between people, not about the animals.
- **Caution:** The same passage (§163–166) repeats ancient false natural history (the weasel "conceives through the ears"). Quote §144 or §169, not the animal details.
- **Verdict:** VERIFIED.

### 2. Epistle of Barnabas 10.11

ANF translation:

> "[The ruminant animal denotes him] who, on receiving food, recognizes Him that nourishes him, and being satisfied by Him, is visibly made glad."

Lightfoot translation (same verse):

> "He that receiveth the food knoweth Him that giveth him the food, and being refreshed appeareth to rejoice in him."

- **Author / work / date:** Anonymous early Christian letter, c. 96–135.
- **Locator:** 10.11 (verse number confirmed in Lightfoot's numbered text; ANF prints no verse numbers).
- **Sources fetched:** https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/0124.htm (Roberts & Donaldson, ANF vol. 1, 1885) and https://www.earlychristianwritings.com/text/barnabas-lightfoot.html (J. B. Lightfoot, 1891). Both public domain.
- **Wording variance:** ANF brackets are the translators' supplement. Lightfoot is shorter and does not need the bracket. Lightfoot reads more plainly for a devotional.
- **Why this voice:** An early Christian who reads "chews the cud" as a picture of receiving food with gratitude to the Giver.
- **Caution (important):** Barnabas 10.9 says Moses gave the laws "with a spiritual significance; but they received them according to fleshly desire." The letter denies that the literal command was ever meant to be kept. It also repeats false animal lore (hare, hyena, weasel). Use only 10.11, and do not present Barnabas's view of Israel as the devotional's view.
- **Verdict:** VERIFIED.

### 3. Rabbi Elazar ben Azariah, Sifra Kedoshim 9:12 (on Lev 20:26)

> "R. Elazar b. Azaryah says: Whence is it derived that a man should not say: I do not desire to wear sha'atnez; I do not desire to eat the flesh of a pig; I do not desire to cohabit with ervah (illicit relations). I do desire it, but what can I do? My Father in heaven has decreed against it!"

- **Author / work / date:** Rabbi Elazar ben Azariah (fl. c. 100 CE), recorded in the Sifra, the halakhic midrash on Leviticus (redacted c. 3rd century).
- **Locator:** Sifra, Kedoshim, Chapter 9:12 (Sefaria numbering). Numbering differs by edition. Rashi on Lev 20:26 cites it as "Sifra, Kedoshim, Chapter 12 23" in the Rosenbaum–Silbermann text.
- **Sources fetched:** https://www.sefaria.org/Sifra%2C_Kedoshim%2C_Chapter_9.12 (via Sefaria API) and https://www.sefaria.org/Rashi_on_Leviticus.20.26
- **Translation:** Rabbi Shraga Silverstein (Sefaria, CC-BY).
- **Wording variance:** Rashi's version (Rosenbaum & Silbermann, 1929–34, public domain per Sefaria): "one should not say, 'My soul loathes swine's flesh', or, 'I have no desire to wear clothes which are a mixture of wool and linen', but one should say, 'I would, indeed, like them, but what can I do since my Father in heaven has imposed these decrees upon me'". Rashi's form omits the third example (illicit relations), which suits a general devotional audience.
- **Why this voice:** A Jewish teacher who grounds "be holy, for I am holy" in obedience to a Father, not in disgust at the food. It is a direct and generous counterpart to Romans 14.
- **Verdict:** VERIFIED.

### 4. Irenaeus, Against Heresies 4.16.5

> "But He has also increased the feeling of reverence; for sons should have more veneration than slaves, and greater love for their father."

- **Author / work / date:** Irenaeus of Lyons, _Against Heresies_, c. 180.
- **Locator:** Book 4, chapter 16, section 5.
- **Source fetched:** https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/0103416.htm
- **Translation:** Alexander Roberts & William Rambaut, ANF vol. 1 (1885). Public domain.
- **Also verified, same section:** "These things, therefore, which were given for bondage, and for a sign to them, He cancelled by the new covenant of liberty."
- **Why this voice:** Irenaeus says freedom does not lower the bar; it moves the believer from slave to son, which links the food laws to the Father theme of the final day.
- **Verdict:** VERIFIED.

### 4b. Irenaeus, Against Heresies 5.21.1 (Genesis 3:15 read of Christ)

> "He shall be on the watch for your head, and you on the watch for His heel."

> "For from that time, He who should be born of a woman, [namely] from the Virgin, after the likeness of Adam, was preached as keeping watch for the head of the serpent."

- **Author / work / date:** Irenaeus of Lyons, _Against Heresies_, c. 180.
- **Locator:** Book 5, chapter 21, section 1.
- **Source fetched:** https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/0103521.htm (re-fetched 2026-09-13)
- **Translation:** Alexander Roberts & William Rambaut, ANF vol. 1 (1885). Public domain.
- **Why this voice:** The earliest clear Christian reading of the struck heel in Gen 3:15 as Christ's. Supports Day 3 and the Day 6 recap line "Since Irenaeus...". He does not connect Gen 3:15 to Jacob's heel (Gen 25:26); the devotional labels that link as a reflection only.
- **Verdict:** VERIFIED.

### 5. Maimonides, Guide for the Perplexed 3.48

> "If the Law provides that such grief should not be caused to cattle or birds, how much more careful must we be that we should not cause grief to our fellowmen."

- **Author / work / date:** Moses Maimonides, _Guide for the Perplexed_, completed c. 1190.
- **Locator:** Part 3, chapter 48 (in the discussion of the mother bird and Lev 22:28).
- **Source fetched:** https://www.sefaria.org/Guide_for_the_Perplexed%2C_Part_3.48 (via Sefaria API). sacred-texts.com (the original transcription) blocked automated fetch.
- **Translation:** M. Friedländer, one-volume edition (1903). Public domain.
- **Also verified, same chapter (on Lev 11 signs):** "The characteristics given in the Law (Lev. xi., and Deut. xiv.) of the permitted animals, viz., chewing the cud and divided hoofs for cattle, and fins and scales for fish, are in themselves neither the cause of the permission when they are present, nor of the prohibition when they are absent; but merely signs by which the recommended species of animals can be discerned from those that are forbidden."
- **Note for the Peniel day:** the same chapter says "The reason why the sinew that shrank is prohibited is stated in the Law (Gen. 32:33)." That links the food laws to Jacob's limp.
- **Why this voice:** The great medieval Jewish philosopher reads the food laws as training in mercy, and his "how much more" lands close to Romans 14:15 ("if your brother is grieved").
- **Caution:** Maimonides also argues the forbidden foods are "unwholesome" and makes a remark about women's vows that reads badly today. Quote only the lines above.
- **Verdict:** VERIFIED.

### 6. Thomas Aquinas, Summa Theologiae I-II q.102 a.6 ad 1

> "Speaking then of the first-mentioned uncleanness, no kind of food is unclean, or can defile a man, by reason of its nature; wherefore we read (Matthew 15:11): 'Not that which goeth into the mouth defileth a man; but what cometh out of the mouth, this defileth a man.'"

- **Author / work / date:** Thomas Aquinas, _Summa Theologiae_, Prima Secundae, c. 1271.
- **Locator:** I-II, question 102 ("Of the causes of the ceremonial precepts"), article 6, reply to objection 1.
- **Source fetched:** https://www.newadvent.org/summa/2102.htm
- **Translation:** Fathers of the English Dominican Province, 2nd revised ed., 1920. Public domain.
- **Also verified, same reply (plainer, pairs with Maimonides):** "the Lord wished to withdraw them from cruelty even in regard to irrational animals, so as to be less inclined to be cruel to other men, through being used to be kind to beasts."
- **Note:** In the same reply Aquinas cites "Rabbi Moses (Doct. Perplex. iii)", that is, Maimonides. A Christian and a Jewish voice in dialogue, in the original text.
- **Why this voice:** Aquinas gives both a literal and a figurative reason for Leviticus 11 and holds them together without contempt for the law.
- **Verdict:** VERIFIED.

---

## Part 2 — Romans 14: the weak and the strong

### 7. John Chrysostom, Homily 26 on Romans (on Rom 14:15)

> "Or do you not value your brother enough even to purchase his salvation at the price of abstinence from meats? And yet Christ refused not to become a slave, nor yet to die for him; but thou dost not despise even food, that you may save him."

- **Author / work / date:** John Chrysostom, _Homilies on Romans_, preached at Antioch, c. 391–397.
- **Locator:** Homily 26, commenting on Romans 14:15 (New Advent prints no section number at this point).
- **Source fetched:** https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/210226.htm
- **Translation:** J. Walker, J. Sheppard and H. Browne, rev. George B. Stevens, NPNF 1st series vol. 11 (1889). Public domain.
- **Wording variance:** The revision mixes "you" and "thou" in one sentence. That mix is in the source. Keep it or modernise openly, but do not present a modernised form as verbatim.
- **Also verified, same homily (on "the kingdom of God is not meat and drink"):** "For your good is charity, love of the brotherhood, being united, being bound together, living at peace, living in gentleness."
- **Why this voice:** The great preacher of the early church ties Romans 14 straight to the cross: Christ gave his life, so we can give up a meal.
- **Verdict:** VERIFIED.

### 8. Martin Luther, The Freedom of a Christian (1520)

> "A Christian man is the most free lord of all, and subject to none; a Christian man is the most dutiful servant of all, and subject to every one."

- **Author / work / date:** Martin Luther, _Concerning Christian Liberty_ / _The Freedom of a Christian_, 1520.
- **Locator:** Opening of the treatise proper, the "two propositions, concerning spiritual liberty and servitude."
- **Source fetched:** https://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/1911/pg1911.txt (Project Gutenberg eBook #1911). Translator credit confirmed at https://www.ctsfw.edu/etext/luther/freedom/ and Project Wittenberg.
- **Translation:** R. S. Grignon, in Wace & Buchheim, _First Principles of the Reformation_ (1883), reprinted in Harvard Classics vol. 36 (1910). Public domain. Grignon translated Luther's Latin version.
- **Wording variance:** W. A. Lambert, revised by Harold J. Grimm (_Luther's Works_ vol. 31, Fortress 1957, copyrighted): "A Christian is a perfectly free lord of all, subject to none. A Christian is a perfectly dutiful servant of all, subject to all." (Lambert/Grimm wording taken from secondary quotation, not from a fetched copy of LW 31.) Use Grignon for a public-domain quotation.
- **Also verified, same treatise, for the Galatians 6:2 day:** "And as our heavenly Father has freely helped us in Christ, so ought we freely to help our neighbour by our body and works, and each should become to other a sort of Christ, so that we may be mutually Christs, and that the same Christ may be in all of us; that is, that we may be truly Christians."
- **Also verified, same treatise, on Romans 14:** "Fight vigorously against the wolves, but on behalf of the sheep, not against the sheep."
- **Why this voice:** Luther's two sentences are the plainest statement of the whole week: free in Christ, servant to the brother.
- **Verdict:** VERIFIED.

### 9. John Calvin, Institutes 3.19.11–12

> "we will so temper the use of our liberty as to make it yield to the ignorance of weak brethren, but not to the austerity of Pharisees." (3.19.11)

> "There is nothing plainer than this rule, that we are to use our liberty if it tends to the edification of our neighbor, but if inexpedient for our neighbor, we are to abstain from it." (3.19.12)

- **Author / work / date:** John Calvin, _Institutes of the Christian Religion_, final Latin edition 1559.
- **Locator:** Book 3, chapter 19 ("Of Christian Liberty"), sections 11 and 12.
- **Source fetched:** https://www.ccel.org/ccel/calvin/institutes.v.xx.html
- **Translation:** Henry Beveridge (1845). Public domain.
- **Wording variance:** John Allen (1813), Gutenberg eBook #64392, https://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/64392/pg64392.txt: "we shall so temper the use of our liberty, that it ought to submit to the ignorance of weak brethren, but not at all to the austerity of Pharisees" and "our liberty should be used, if it conduces to our neighbour's edification; but that if it be not beneficial to our neighbour, it should be abridged."
- **Also verified, 3.19.11:** "we shall call the one the offense of the weak, the other the offense of Pharisees" (the offence given / offence taken distinction).
- **Also verified, 3.19.13:** "For as our liberty is to be made subservient to charity, so charity must in its turn be subordinate to purity of faith."
- **Also verified, 3.19.7 (things indifferent):** "The third part of this liberty is that we are not bound before God to any observance of external things which are in themselves indifferent (ἀδιάφορα), but that we are now at full liberty either to use or omit them."
- **Why this voice:** Calvin gives the practical test the devotional needs: bend for the weak, do not bend for the Pharisee.
- **Verdict:** VERIFIED.

### 10. Augustine, On the Spirit and the Letter 19.34

> "The law was therefore given, in order that grace might be sought; grace was given, in order that the law might be fulfilled."

- **Author / work / date:** Augustine of Hippo, _De spiritu et littera_, 412.
- **Locator:** Chapter 34 in NPNF numbering (Benedictine chapter XIX), section 34.
- **Source fetched:** https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/1502.htm
- **Translation:** Peter Holmes & Robert Ernest Wallis, rev. B. B. Warfield, NPNF 1st series vol. 5 (1887). Public domain.
- **Also verified, ch. 29 [XVII]:** "There the law was given outwardly, so that the unrighteous might be terrified; here it was given inwardly, so that they might be justified."
- **Why this voice:** In 17 words Augustine states why Leviticus 11 and Romans 14 are not enemies.
- **Verdict:** VERIFIED.

### 11. John Bunyan, Grace Abounding §229

> "I also saw moreover, that it was not my good frame of heart that made my righteousness better, nor yet my bad frame that made my righteousness worse; for my righteousness was Jesus Christ Himself, The same yesterday, to-day, and for ever."

- **Author / work / date:** John Bunyan, _Grace Abounding to the Chief of Sinners_, 1666.
- **Locator:** Section 229 (the "Thy righteousness is in heaven" passage). Section 230 follows: "Now did my chains fall off my legs indeed."
- **Source fetched:** https://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/654/pg654.txt (Gutenberg eBook #654). Public domain.
- **Wording variance:** Gutenberg italicises the scripture phrase. Editions vary in punctuation only.
- **Also verified, The Pilgrim's Progress (1678), Interpreter's House:** "He that began to sweep at first, is the Law; but she that brought water, and did sprinkle it, is the Gospel." Source: https://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/131/pg131.txt
- **Why this voice:** A Puritan tinker who lived under the terror of the law says in plain words where his righteousness is.
- **Verdict:** VERIFIED.

## Part 3 — Jacob and Esau: heel, birthright, garments, Peniel, embrace

### 12. Augustine, City of God 16.37

> "We learn from this that a person is to be blamed, not for the kind of food he eats, but for immoderate greed."

- **Author / work / date:** Augustine, _De civitate Dei_, Book 16, c. 420.
- **Locator:** Book 16, chapter 37 ("Of the Things Mystically Prefigured in Esau and Jacob"). The line comments on Esau selling the birthright for lentils.
- **Source fetched:** https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/120116.htm
- **Translation:** Marcus Dods, NPNF 1st series vol. 2 (1887). Public domain.
- **Also verified, same chapter (on the garments):** Jacob "put himself under his father's hands, having covered himself with kid-skins, as if bearing the sins of others."
- **Also verified, 16.39 (Peniel):** "And yet he besought a blessing from the very angel he had overcome; and so the imposition of this name was the blessing. For Israel means seeing God, which will at last be the reward of all the saints."
- **Why this voice:** This single Augustine sentence joins the food theme (Lev 11 / Rom 14) to Esau's bowl of stew. It is the bridge between the two halves of the week.
- **Verdict:** VERIFIED.

### 13. Ambrose on Jacob in Esau's garments (quoted by Calvin, Institutes 3.11.23)

Ambrose, as translated inside Beveridge's Calvin:

> "Isaac's smelling the odour of his garments, perhaps means that we are justified not by works, but by faith, since carnal infirmity is an impediment to works, but errors of conduct are covered by the brightness of faith, which merits the pardon of faults."

Calvin's own application, same section:

> "so we conceal ourselves under the precious purity of Christ, our first-born brother, that we may obtain an attestation of righteousness from the presence of God."

- **Author / work / date:** Ambrose of Milan, _De Iacob et vita beata_ (On Jacob and the Happy Life), c. 386–388. Quoted by Calvin, _Institutes_ (1559).
- **Locators:** Ambrose, _De Iacob_ 2.2.9 (Book II, chapter 2; §9 in standard numbering). Calvin, _Institutes_ 3.11.23.
- **Sources fetched:**
  - Latin Ambrose: https://la.wikisource.org/wiki/De_Iacob_et_vita_beata — "Et odoratus est odorem vestimentorum ejus. Et fortasse illud est, quia non operibus justificamur, sed fide; quoniam carnalis infirmitas operibus impedimento est, sed fidei claritas factorum obumbrat errorem, quae meretur veniam delictorum."
  - Beveridge Calvin: https://www.ccel.org/ccel/calvin/institutes.v.xii.html
  - Allen Calvin: https://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/45001/pg45001.txt
- **Translation:** Henry Beveridge (1845); John Allen (1813). Both public domain. The modern Ambrose translation (M. P. McHugh, Fathers of the Church vol. 65, 1972) is copyrighted and was not checked.
- **Wording variance:** Allen renders Calvin's line "so we shelter ourselves under the precious purity of Christ our elder brother, that we may obtain the testimony of righteousness in the sight of God." Beveridge says "first-born brother"; Allen says "elder brother". Allen renders Ambrose: "That Isaac smelled the odour of the garments, perhaps indicates, that we are justified not by works, but by faith; since the infirmity of the flesh is an impediment to works, but the brightness of faith, which merits the pardon of sin, conceals the error of our actions." The Latin "obumbrat" means "overshadows."
- **Why this voice:** This is the requested "Father on Jacob's garments," and Calvin's gloss connects the stolen blessing directly to Christ the firstborn brother (Heb 2:11, Rom 8:29).
- **Caution:** Ambrose's full passage reads the robe as passing from "the Jewish people" to "the Christian people." Quote only the lines above.
- **Verdict:** VERIFIED (Latin original confirmed; English via Calvin's translators).

### 14. C. H. Spurgeon, "He Blessed Him There" (Genesis 32:29)

> "But once more, where Jacob got the blessing, it was a place of conscious weakness. The angel touched the sinew in the hollow of his thigh. While he got the blessing, he got lameness too, and he might be well content to carry that lameness to his grave."

- **Author / work / date:** Charles Haddon Spurgeon. Delivered at the Metropolitan Tabernacle (date not printed). Published posthumously 13 October 1910.
- **Locator:** Metropolitan Tabernacle Pulpit, vol. 56, sermon No. 3219.
- **Source fetched:** https://www.spurgeongems.org/sermon/chs3219.pdf (sermon located through the Spurgeon Gems Old Testament scripture index, https://www.spurgeongems.org/sindex_ot.pdf).
- **Edition note:** Spurgeon Gems states it made "minimal updating of a few archaic words." For No. 2015 I compared its text with Blue Letter Bible and BibleBB and found only punctuation differences. I did not make that comparison for No. 3219.
- **Also verified, same sermon (links Peniel to Esau):** "To think, 'I did that man a wrong, but it is gone and forgiven forever,' is a blessing worth praying all night to obtain."
- **Why this voice:** Spurgeon says plainly that the blessing and the limp came together, which suits the Peniel day ("I will not let you go unless you bless me").
- **Note:** I found no Spurgeon sermon with Genesis 32:26 as its main text. The index lists No. 3219 (Gen 32:29), No. 2978 "Power with God" (Gen 32:28), and No. 2739 "I Have Enough" (Gen 33:9, 11).
- **Verdict:** VERIFIED.

### 15. Rabbi Shimon bar Yochai on Esau's kiss, Sifrei Bamidbar 69:2 (on Gen 33:4)

> "R. Shimon b. Yochai says: It is a known halachah that Esav hates Jacob, but his mercy gained the ascendancy at that time and he kissed him with all his heart."

- **Author / work / date:** Rabbi Shimon bar Yochai (mid-2nd century CE), recorded in Sifrei on Numbers (redacted c. 3rd century).
- **Locator:** Sifrei Bamidbar 69:2 (on the dotted letters in Scripture, including "vayishakehu" in Gen 33:4).
- **Sources fetched:** https://www.sefaria.org/Sifrei_Bamidbar.69.2 ; https://www.sefaria.org/Rashi_on_Genesis.33.4 ; https://www.sefaria.org/Bereshit_Rabbah.78.9 (all via Sefaria API)
- **Translation:** Rabbi Shraga Silverstein (Sefaria, CC-BY).
- **Wording variance:** Rashi (Rosenbaum & Silbermann): "Is it not well-known that Esau hated Jacob? But at that moment his pity was really aroused and he kissed him with all his heart." Genesis Rabbah 78:9 gives the same reading to Rabbi Shimon ben Elazar, and then Rabbi Yannai's opposite view: Esau "did not come to kiss him, but rather to bite him."
- **Why this voice:** A Jewish reading that lets Esau's embrace be real mercy, which fits Genesis 33 and the week's theme of the elder brother who forgives.
- **Caution:** Be honest that the rabbis disagreed. The dots in the Hebrew text drew both readings.
- **Verdict:** VERIFIED.

### 16. John Calvin, Commentary on Genesis 33:10

> "And certainly Esau being thus changed, was no obscure figure of the favor of God: so that Jacob might properly say, that he had been exhilarated by that friendly and fraternal reception, as if he had seen God or an angel; that is, as if God had given some sign of his presence."

- **Author / work / date:** John Calvin, _Commentary on Genesis_ (Latin 1554).
- **Locator:** On Genesis 33:10.
- **Sources fetched:** https://biblehub.com/commentaries/calvin/genesis/33.htm ; confirmed in the printed scan at https://archive.org/details/commentariesonfi02calvuoft (vol. 2, p. 210).
- **Translation:** John King, Calvin Translation Society, Edinburgh (vol. 2; 1847–1850). Public domain.
- **Wording variance:** The printed King edition spells "favour"; Bible Hub modernises to "favor." Calvin's Latin text of the verse reads "acsi viderem faciem Angeli" ("as if I saw the face of an angel").
- **Also verified, same comment:** "for this was as a pledge of reconciliation." And: "provided that Esau, in return, would show himself a brother to him."
- **Why this voice:** A clear, restrained reading of Genesis 33:10: a brother's changed face is a sign of God's favour.
- **Note on the search for a Genesis 33:10 line:** Calvin is the strongest verified line I found. Rashi (on 33:10) reads the verse differently: Jacob means he saw Esau's guardian angel at Peniel, and says so partly to make Esau afraid. That is a useful contrast but it does not support the devotional's warm reading. Matthew Henry's Concise Commentary on Bible Hub has no comment on this phrase.
- **Verdict:** VERIFIED.

## Part 4 — The cross: veil, testament, heirs, brothers, burdens

### 17. C. H. Spurgeon, "The Rent Veil" (Matthew 27:50–51)

> "For believers the veil is not rolled up, but rent. The veil was not unhooked, and carefully folded up, and put away, so that it might be put in its place at some future time."

- **Author / work / date:** C. H. Spurgeon, delivered Lord's Day morning, 25 March 1888.
- **Locator:** Metropolitan Tabernacle Pulpit, vol. 34, sermon No. 2015.
- **Sources fetched:** https://www.spurgeongems.org/sermon/chs2015.pdf ; cross-checked at https://biblebb.com/files/spurgeon/2015.htm and https://www.blueletterbible.org/Comm/spurgeon_charles/sermons/2015.cfm
- **Wording variance:** The three texts agree in wording. Punctuation and capitals vary ("Believers", commas).
- **Also verified, same sermon:** "God has rent the veil, and the devil cannot mend it."
- **Also verified, same sermon (ties the veil to the law theme):** "In this double sense the veil is rent; the separating ordinance is abrogated, and the separating sin is forgiven."
- **Why this voice:** Spurgeon's image is plain and final. The veil is torn, not stored away, so the barrier of the ceremonial law does not come back.
- **Verdict:** VERIFIED.

### 18. John Chrysostom, Homily 16 on Hebrews §2 (Heb 9:15–17)

> "We had offended: we ought to have died: He died for us and made us worthy of the Testament."

- **Author / work / date:** John Chrysostom, _Homilies on Hebrews_, preached at Constantinople c. 403–404, published after his death.
- **Locator:** Homily 16, section 2.
- **Source fetched:** https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/240216.htm
- **Translation:** Frederic Gardiner, NPNF 1st series vol. 14 (1889). Public domain.
- **Also verified, same homily §2:** "And a testament is of this character: It makes some heirs, and some disinherited."
- **Why this voice:** Fifteen words that state Hebrews 9:15–17 exactly: the inheritance comes to us through the Testator's death. It also echoes the birthright story.
- **Verdict:** VERIFIED.

### 19. George MacDonald, "The Inheritance" (Colossians 1:12)

> "To have a share in any earthly inheritance, is to diminish the share of the other inheritors. In the inheritance of the saints, that which each has, goes to increase the possession of the rest."

- **Author / work / date:** George MacDonald, _Unspoken Sermons, Series III_ (dedication dated Bordighera, 3 May 1889).
- **Locator:** Series III, final sermon, "The Inheritance."
- **Source fetched:** https://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/9057/pg9057.txt (Gutenberg eBook #9057, Series I–III in one volume). Public domain.
- **Also verified, same sermon (Matthew 7:11 day):** "Whatever a father can do to make his children blessed, that will God do for his children."
- **Also verified, "The Hands of the Father" (Series I, 1867):** "And shall we dare to think that if we being evil know how to give good gifts to our children, God will not give us his own spirit when we come to ask him?"
- **Why this voice:** MacDonald names the difference between Jacob and Esau's fight over one birthright and the co-heirs of Romans 8:17, where no one's share is taken from another.
- **Theological caution:** MacDonald rejected penal substitution ("Justice," Series III) and, in "Abba, Father!" (Series II), rejected the idea of adoption in favour of innate sonship. Quote him on inheritance and fatherhood; do not use him as the voice for Hebrews 9 or Romans 8:15.
- **Verdict:** VERIFIED.

### 20. Dietrich Bonhoeffer, Life Together, ch. 4 "Ministry" ("The Ministry of Bearing")

> "The Christian, however, must bear the burden of a brother. He must suffer and endure the brother. It is only when he is a burden that another person is really a brother and not merely an object to be manipulated."

- **Author / work / date:** Dietrich Bonhoeffer, _Gemeinsames Leben_ (1939). English: _Life Together_, trans. John W. Doberstein (Harper & Row, 1954).
- **Locator:** Chapter IV, "Ministry," section "The Ministry of Bearing," p. 100 in the HarperOne paperback (ISBN 978-0-06-060852-1; chapter IV begins p. 90).
- **Source checked:** a scanned copy of the HarperOne printing posted online at [unlicensed scan; link removed] This is not a licensed source. It was used only to confirm wording and page. Confirm the page against the physical copy you cite, because pagination differs between printings (one online review cites p. 91 for a nearby sentence).
- **Copyright:** © 1954 Harper & Row, renewed; HarperCollins. Short quotation only, with full credit. The Fortress _Dietrich Bonhoeffer Works_ vol. 5 translation (1996) words these sentences differently; not checked.
- **Also verified, p. 101 ("To bear the burden of the other person means involvement with the created reality of the other, to accept and affirm it, and, in bearing with it, to break through to the point where we take joy in it.")**
- **Also verified, p. 102 (running head in the scan reads "101"; page sequence shows 102). Direct Romans 14 line:** "The weak must not judge the strong, the strong must not despise the weak. The weak must guard against pride, the strong against indifference."
- **Why this voice:** A 20th-century martyr who wrote Galatians 6:2 as the "law of Christ": the law of bearing, fulfilled at the cross.
- **Note:** _Life Together_ does not mention Simon of Cyrene (searched the full text). For Simon, use the Gospel text itself.
- **Verdict:** VERIFIED (short quote; copyrighted).

## REJECTED — popular quotes on law, grace, and freedom that fail verification

### R1. "In essentials unity, in non-essentials liberty, in all things charity." — attributed to Augustine

- **Verdict:** MISATTRIBUTED.
- **Evidence:** Not in Augustine. James J. O'Donnell (Augustine scholar, Georgetown) could not find it in Augustine's works: https://faculty.georgetown.edu/jod/augustine/quote.html. His page reproduces Philip Schaff, _History of the Christian Church_ vol. 7, pp. 650–653, which says the motto is "often falsely attributed to St. Augustin" and traces it to Rupertus Meldenius (Peter Meiderlin), _Paraenesis votiva pro pace ecclesiae_ (1626): "Si nos servaremus in necessariis Unitatem, in non-necessariis Libertatem, in utrisque Charitatem, optimo certe loco essent res nostrae." Henk Nellen (1999) found an earlier form in Marco Antonio de Dominis, _De republica ecclesiastica_ 4.8 (London, 1617): "unitatem in necessariis, in non necessariis libertatem, in omnibus caritatem."
- **Why it matters here:** It is the most-quoted "Romans 14" motto. If used, credit it to Meldenius (1626) or de Dominis (1617), never Augustine.

### R2. "Run, John, run, the law commands / But gives us neither feet nor hands; / Far better news the gospel brings: / It bids us fly and gives us wings." — attributed to John Bunyan

- **Verdict:** MISATTRIBUTED.
- **Evidence:** (1) Not found in the full Offor edition of _The Works of John Bunyan_ (Gutenberg eBook #6049, 12 MB text, searched for "nor hands," "neither feet," "gives me wings," "lends me wings," "Run, John"): https://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/6049/pg6049.txt. Also absent from _Pilgrim's Progress_ and _Grace Abounding_. (2) Spurgeon, _The Salt-Cellars_ (1889), p. 200, under the proverb "Gospel commandings are gracious enablings," credits John Berridge: "Run, John, and work, the law commands, / Yet finds me neither feet nor hands; / But sweeter news the gospel brings, / It bids me fly, and lends me wings." Source: archive.org scan `saltcellarsbein01spurgoog` (https://archive.org/details/saltcellarsbein01spurgoog). (3) Justin Taylor (TGC), citing Jason Meyer, _The End of the Law_ (2010), p. 2 n. 3, reports an earlier form in Ralph Erskine, _Sermons and Practical Works_ vol. 10 (1778), p. 283: https://www.thegospelcoalition.org/blogs/justin-taylor/run-john-run/ (Erskine text not checked by me).
- **Use instead:** Credit John Berridge (via Spurgeon, 1889) or Ralph Erskine. For a verified Bunyan line on law and gospel, use the Interpreter's House passage above.

### R3. "God doesn't need your good works, but your neighbor does." — attributed to Martin Luther

- **Verdict:** UNVERIFIABLE as Luther's own words.
- **Evidence:** The pithy form is cited to Gustaf Wingren, _Luther on Vocation_, p. 10, where Wingren summarises Luther (Modern Reformation cites "Wingren, 10": https://www.modernreformation.org/resources/articles/the-doctrine-of-vocation-2). I did not locate this wording in Luther. A search of Luther's _Epistle Sermons_ vol. 3 (Trinity to Advent; Gutenberg #30619) found no match. Search reports point to a Luther sermon for the 22nd Sunday after Trinity, but I could not fetch it.
- **Use instead (verified Luther, 1520, Grignon):** "And as our heavenly Father has freely helped us in Christ, so ought we freely to help our neighbour by our body and works, and each should become to other a sort of Christ."

### R4. "Pray as though everything depended on God; work as though everything depended on you." — attributed to Augustine (also to Ignatius, Luther, Wesley)

- **Verdict:** MISATTRIBUTED (to Augustine). Origin uncertain.
- **Evidence:** No Augustine source located. Trent Horn, Catholic Answers (https://www.catholic.com/magazine/online-edition/st-ignatius-said-what), finds no source in Ignatius's writings. The earliest match for this wording he found is an 1868 _American Phrenological Journal_ credit to Ignatius; an 1867 journal gave a similar line to Wesley. Secondary sources report a related (and reversed) Latin maxim in Gábor Hevenesi's _Scintillae Ignatianae_ (1705), about 150 years after Ignatius (Latin not checked by me). The Catechism of the Catholic Church §2834 cites it as Ignatian tradition.

### R5. "Love God and do what you please." — attributed to Augustine

- **Verdict:** GENUINE SOURCE, DISTORTED WORDING.
- **Evidence:** The real text is Augustine, _Homilies on 1 John_ 7.8: "Once for all, then, a short precept is given you: Love, and do what you will: whether you hold your peace, through love hold your peace; whether you cry out, through love cry out; whether you correct, through love correct; whether you spare, through love do you spare: let the root of love be within, of this root can nothing spring but what is good." (H. Browne, NPNF 1.7): https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/170207.htm. The context is a father who disciplines out of love. It is not a licence. "Please" and "God" are not in the text.
- **Guidance:** Do not use the short form on a Romans 14 day. It will be heard as "freedom means do as you like."

### R6. "Love God and sin boldly" / "Sin boldly." — attributed to Martin Luther

- **Verdict:** GENUINE SOURCE, TRUNCATED.
- **Evidence:** Luther to Melanchthon, 1 August 1521, from the Wartburg (WA Br 2:372; Latin "Esto peccator et pecca fortiter, sed fortius fide et gaude in Christo"). Project Wittenberg translation (Erika Bullmann Flores): "Be a sinner, and let your sins be strong, but let your trust in Christ be stronger, and rejoice in Christ who is the victor over sin, death, and the world." https://www.projectwittenberg.org/pub/resources/text/wittenberg/luther/letsinsbe.txt
- **Guidance:** The clause about stronger faith in Christ is the point. The short form "sin boldly" misrepresents Luther. "Love God and sin boldly" is not in the letter.

### R7. "Even if I knew that tomorrow the world would go to pieces, I would still plant my apple tree." — attributed to Martin Luther

- **Verdict:** MISATTRIBUTED (Luther apocrypha).
- **Evidence:** Not in Luther's works. Martin Schloemann, _Luthers Apfelbäumchen?_ (Vandenhoeck & Ruprecht), traces the earliest documented use to an October 1944 circular of the Confessing Church in Hesse (pastor Karl Lotz), which already treats it as familiar. Summary: https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luthers_Apfelb%C3%A4umchen
- **Why listed:** It is the most-shared Luther line online. It is a flag that "Luther said" needs a WA or LW locator every time.

### R8. "You don't have a soul. You are a soul. You have a body." — attributed to C. S. Lewis (and to George MacDonald)

- **Verdict:** MISATTRIBUTED (Lewis apocrypha).
- **Evidence:** The C. S. Lewis Foundation lists it first among "Quotes Misattributed to C.S. Lewis," as a paraphrase connected with George MacDonald: https://www.cslewis.org/aboutus/faq/quotes-misattributed/ . Mere Orthodoxy and First Things trace the earliest known form to an 1892 Quaker periodical, _The British Friend_, which credits MacDonald second-hand without a source (I did not read the 1892 page). Search of the full text of MacDonald's _Unspoken Sermons_ I–III (Gutenberg #9057) found no such sentence.
- **Also on the Foundation's list:** "Humility is not thinking less of yourself, it's thinking of yourself less." Not by Lewis (Rick Warren / others).
- **Guidance:** Any Lewis quote used in the series needs book and chapter. The verified Lewis line above (Mere Christianity II.5) is safe.

### R9. "Preach the Gospel at all times; when necessary, use words." — attributed to Francis of Assisi

- **Verdict:** MISATTRIBUTED (included because it is often used to set "deeds" against "words" and "law").
- **Evidence (secondary only; weaker than R1–R8):** Not found in Francis's writings or early lives, per The Gospel Coalition FactChecker (fetched): https://www.thegospelcoalition.org/article/factchecker-misquoting-francis-of-assisi/ . The nearest genuine text is the Earlier Rule (Regula non bullata, 1221), ch. 17: "Omnes tamen fratres operibus praedicent" ("All the brothers, however, should preach by their deeds"), after a rule that no brother may preach without his minister's permission. TGC labels the chapter XII; standard editions number it XVII. I did not fetch the Latin Rule directly; the Latin phrase and chapter number come from search results. National Catholic Register and Christian Today make the same point (search results, not fetched).

# 5. 04 — Stories: verified primary-source illustrations

Series: Law and Freedom (Leviticus 11 / Romans 14 / Jacob and Esau / the cross)
Compiled: 2026-09-12. Research agent: stories verification.

## How to read this file

- **VERIFIED** = I fetched and read the primary text (or a scholarly edition that prints it) and the element is stated there.
- **VERIFIED (self-report)** = the element rests on the subject's own testimony. True to the source, not independently confirmed.
- **DISPUTED** = sources conflict, or the element rests only on later or secondhand retellings.
- **UNVERIFIED** = I could not reach a primary text in this pass. Do not use it as fact.
- **REJECTED** = a popular embellishment the primary text contradicts or does not contain.
- Quotations inside retellings are copied verbatim from the cited text. No line is placed in anyone's mouth that the source does not give.
- Local copies of every text I read are in `research/stories-src/` (same folder as this file).

# DAY 1 — Holiness and the food laws

## Recommended: Zwingli and the Lenten sausages ("Affair of the Sausages"), Zurich, 1522

### Names and dates

- **Huldrych (Ulrich) Zwingli** (1484–1531), people's priest (_Leutpriester_) at the Great Minster, Zurich, from 1 January 1519.
- **Christoph Froschauer**, printer, Zurich.
- Meal at Froschauer's house: "an der alten Fastnacht" (the old Shrovetide, the first Sunday of Lent). In 1522 Easter was 20 April, so that Sunday was **9 March 1522** (calendar conversion, standard; the depositions give only the feast name).
- Zwingli's sermon on the choice of foods: **23 March 1522** (third Sunday of Lent).
- Printed as _Von Erkiesen und Freiheit der Speisen_: dated **16 April 1522**.
- Bishop of Constance's delegation before the Zurich councils: early April 1522. Council decision and mandate: **9 April 1522**.

### Retelling (verified details only, 232 words)

In Lent 1522, a group of men met in the Zurich house of the printer Christoph Froschauer. One of them, the baker Bartholomäus Pur, later gave evidence to the city council. He named those present, among them two priests and Ulrich Zwingli, the people's priest of the Great Minster. The printer brought out two dried sausages. They cut them up, and each man received a small piece. According to Pur, all of them ate, "except Master Ulrich Zwingli."

Froschauer wrote to the council to defend himself. His workers were printing the letters of Saint Paul day and night for the Frankfurt fair. Mush was not enough to feed them, he said, and he could not always afford fish.

Zwingli had not eaten, but he did not leave the others to face the charge alone. On 23 March he preached on the choice of foods. He wrote that he would be an unfaithful shepherd if he did not strengthen the weak and guard the strong. The printed sermon walks through Romans 14: the one who eats must not despise the one who does not.

Later that year Zwingli reported that when he told the meat-eaters to consider the offence they might cause, they stopped. On 9 April the council ordered that no one eat meat in Lent without good cause. It also ordered that no one quarrel with or insult another over it.

### Primary citations

1. **Egli, Emil, ed.** _Actensammlung zur Geschichte der Zürcher Reformation in den Jahren 1519–1533._ Zürich, 1879. **No. 233** (depositions, "Kundschaft, wer jetz in der fasten fleisch und eier esse"), pp. 72–74; **No. 234** (Froschauer's defence to the council), pp. 74–75; **No. 235** (opinion of the Provost and Chapter), pp. 75–76; **No. 236** (council decision, 9 April 1522), pp. 76–77; **No. 237** (mandate, 9 April 1522), p. 77.
   URL: https://archive.org/details/bub_gb_CGRKAAAAYAAJ
2. **Zwingli, Huldreich.** _Von Erkiesen und Freiheit der Speisen_ (16 April 1522). In _Huldreich Zwinglis sämtliche Werke_, vol. 1 (Corpus Reformatorum 88), ed. Egli and Finsler, Berlin 1905, pp. 74–136 (editor's introduction pp. 74–78; text from p. 88).
   URL: https://archive.org/details/smtlichewerkeunt0088zwin
3. **Zwingli.** _Archeteles_ (1522), section "The Episcopal Visitation." English in S. M. Jackson, ed., _Selected Works of Huldreich Zwingli_, Philadelphia 1901, pp. 11–19.
   URL: https://archive.org/details/huldreichzwingli00jackuoft

### Key source wording (verbatim)

- Egli no. 233, Pur's deposition: "do brächte der trucker harfür zwo (ge)digen würst; die zerschnittind si, und wurde ir jetlichem ein kleins stucki. Das essint si all, usgenommen M. Uolrich Zwingli, lütpriester zum Grossen Münster."
- Egli no. 234, Froschauer: "ich muoss tag und nacht, firtag und werktag mit umgan und arbeiten, domit und ich es ferggen mög uf die Frankfurter mess. Und das ist nämlich die epistel des heiligen Pauli. Do mag ich (es) mit minem husgesind mit muos, und sunst nüt, nit erzügen; und fisch vermag ich nit aber allwegen ze koufen."
- Zwingli, _Von Erkiesen_, SW I p. 91: "das ich als ein unfrütiger hirt ... umb liesse kummen die schaff, so miner trüw empfolht sind, das ich die blöden nit starckte unnd die starcken nit behüte."
- Zwingli, _Archeteles_, tr. Jackson p. 19: "presently when told by me that they ought to take into account the possible cause of offence they stopped."
- Egli no. 237, mandate: "dass hinfür in der fasten on merklich ursachen und erlouptnuss gar niemas mer fleisch essen solle bis uf witern bescheid ... dass sich niemas einichs zanks und haders oder widerwärtiger, ungeschickter worten gegen einandern nit anneme ... es syg des fleischessens, des predigens, oder derglich sachen."

### Element verdicts

| Element                                                                                                                               | Verdict                           | Evidence                                                                                         |
| ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |

| Zwingli did not eat                                                                                                                   | VERIFIED (two sources)            | Egli 233 §1c; Zwingli's own statement at the Second Disputation (1523), summarised in SW I p. 75 |
| Date 9 March 1522                                                                                                                     | VERIFIED as conversion            | "alte Fastnacht" = first Sunday of Lent; Easter 1522 = 20 April                                  |
| Froschauer's reason: printing Paul's epistles for Frankfurt fair; mush insufficient; fish unaffordable                                | VERIFIED                          | Egli 234                                                                                         |
| Sermon preached 23 March 1522                                                                                                         | VERIFIED                          | SW I p. 75 and editor's note p. 89                                                               |
| Printed 16 April 1522, dedicated to "all pious Christians at Zurich"                                                                  | VERIFIED                          | SW I p. 75                                                                                       |
| Sermon treats Romans 14–15 on offence                                                                                                 | VERIFIED                          | SW I, text section "Von ergernus"                                                                |
| Meat-eaters stopped when Zwingli urged regard for offence                                                                             | VERIFIED (self-report)            | _Archeteles_, Jackson p. 19                                                                      |
| Council order of 9 April: no Lenten meat without good cause; no quarrelling or insults over it; eaters to confess to their confessors | VERIFIED                          | Egli 236–237                                                                                     |
| Maid's separate testimony: meat eaten that evening by a company of 10 or 12                                                           | VERIFIED as a separate deposition | Egli 233 §II. Do not merge it with Pur's "two sausages" account.                                 |

### Embellishments checked and REJECTED

- **"Zwingli ate the sausages" / "Zwingli held a sausage party."** REJECTED. Pur's deposition and Zwingli's own 1523 statement both say he did not eat. The house and the sausages were Froschauer's.
- **"Froschauer was jailed."** REJECTED as unsupported. Egli 233–237 contain no imprisonment. The council required confession to a confessor and forbade further Lenten meat without cause.
- **"The name 'Affair of the Sausages' is from 1522."** REJECTED. It is a modern English label. The 1522 records say "fleischessen in der fasten" (meat-eating in Lent).
- **"Zwingli told people to eat whatever they liked in Lent."** REJECTED. _Archeteles_ (Jackson p. 18): "I had nowhere taught that Lent ought not to be kept, though I could wish that it were not prescribed so imperiously, but were left free to the individual."
- **Merging the Froschauer meal with the Ash Wednesday incident.** REJECTED. The roast on Heini Aberli's plate at the "Weggen" guild house was Ash Wednesday (Egli 233 §2), a different event.

### Why it fits the day

It gives both halves of Romans 14 in one documented case. There is freedom ("nach göttlichem gesatzt dhein spis cristenmenschen verbotten," Egli 235), and there is restraint for the sake of the weak (Zwingli's own report). It also shows why Leviticus 11 mattered: food rules were treated as boundaries of holiness worth a city dispute.

## Day 1 alternate A: Eleazar the scribe, 2 Maccabees 6:18–31

> **WITHDRAWN — NOT FOR USE (SA-141, founder 2026-09-13: "We are only using the 66 books.").** This section, the canonical-status table and the Hebrews 11:35 note below were used in the first Day 1 draft and removed before shipping. Kept only as a record of what was verified and why it was dropped. Day 1 now uses §7 (Daniel 1).

### Names and dates

- **Eleazar**, "one of the principal scribes," aged ninety (2 Macc 6:18, 24).
- Setting: the persecution under **Antiochus IV Epiphanes**. 1 Macc 1:54 dates the "abomination of desolation" to "the fifteenth day of the month Casleu, in the hundred forty and fifth year" of the Seleucid era. That converts to **December 167 BC** (standard conversion). 2 Macc 6:1 introduces the decrees as "Not long after this." The text gives no exact date for Eleazar's death. "About 167 BC" is a fair, labelled inference.
- The book says it abridges the five books of **Jason of Cyrene** (2 Macc 2:23).

### Text, KJV Apocrypha (public domain), exact verse locators

Source: Project Gutenberg eBook #124, _Deuterocanonical Books of the Bible_ (KJV Apocrypha text). https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/124

> **6:18** Eleazar, one of the principal scribes, an aged man, and of a well favoured countenance, was constrained to open his mouth, and to eat swine's flesh.
> **6:19** But he, choosing rather to die gloriously, than to live stained with such an abomination, spit it forth, and came of his own accord to the torment,
> **6:20** As it behoved them to come, that are resolute to stand out against such things, as are not lawful for love of life to be tasted.
> **6:21** But they that had the charge of that wicked feast, for the old acquaintance they had with the man, taking him aside, besought him to bring flesh of his own provision, such as was lawful for him to use, and make as if he did eat of the flesh taken from the sacrifice commanded by the king;
> **6:22** That in so doing he might be delivered from death, and for the old friendship with them find favour.
> **6:23** But he began to consider discreetly, and as became his age, and the excellency of his ancient years, and the honour of his gray head, whereunto he was come, and his most honest education from a child, or rather the holy law made and given by God: therefore he answered accordingly, and willed them straightways to send him to the grave.
> **6:24** For it becometh not our age, said he, in any wise to dissemble, whereby many young persons might think that Eleazar, being fourscore years old and ten, were now gone to a strange religion;
> **6:25** And so they through mine hypocrisy, and desire to live a little time and a moment longer, should be deceived by me, and I get a stain to mine old age, and make it abominable.
> **6:26** For though for the present time I should be delivered from the punishment of men: yet should I not escape the hand of the Almighty, neither alive, nor dead.
> **6:27** Wherefore now, manfully changing this life, I will shew myself such an one as mine age requireth,
> **6:28** And leave a notable example to such as be young to die willingly and courageously for the honourable and holy laws. And when he had said these words, immediately he went to the torment:
> **6:29** They that led him changing the good will they bare him a little before into hatred, because the foresaid speeches proceeded, as they thought, from a desperate mind.
> **6:30** But when he was ready to die with stripes, he groaned, and said, It is manifest unto the Lord, that hath the holy knowledge, that whereas I might have been delivered from death, I now endure sore pains in body by being beaten: but in soul am well content to suffer these things, because I fear him.
> **6:31** And thus this man died, leaving his death for an example of a noble courage, and a memorial of virtue, not only unto young men, but unto all his nation.

Greek check (Rahlfs text, Greek Wikisource, https://el.wikisource.org/wiki/Μακκαβαίων_Β'): "the torment" in 6:19 and 6:28 is **τὸ τύμπανον** (_to tympanon_). 6:30 has **μαστιγούμενος** ("being scourged"). The pork is **ὕειον κρέας** (6:18), the meat Leviticus 11:7 names unclean.

### Retelling (text-bound, 168 words)

2 Maccabees tells of Eleazar, a leading scribe aged ninety, in the years when the Seleucid king forced Jews to eat from pagan sacrifices. His captors forced his mouth open to make him eat pork. He spat it out and walked of his own accord to the instrument of punishment. The officials in charge had known him for years. They took him aside and offered a way out: bring meat he was permitted to eat, and only pretend to eat the king's sacrificial meat. Eleazar refused. He said that at his age pretending was unworthy. The young would think that Eleazar, at ninety, had gone over to a foreign religion, and his pretence would lead them astray. He chose instead to "leave a notable example to such as be young." As he died under the blows he said that he suffered in body but was content in soul "because I fear him." The book says his death became an example "unto all his nation."

### Element verdicts

| Element                                           | Verdict                 | Note                                                                                                                                                                                                               |
| ------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| What 2 Maccabees says (every element above)       | VERIFIED as text        | KJV and Greek checked                                                                                                                                                                                              |
| Eleazar was a historical person who died this way | DISPUTED / UNVERIFIABLE | Known only from Maccabean literature (2 Macc 6; expanded in 4 Macc 5–7). No independent attestation. 2 Macc is an epitome of Jason of Cyrene (2:23). Present it as "2 Maccabees tells," not as documented history. |
| Date about 167 BC                                 | VERIFIED as inference   | From 1 Macc 1:54 and 2 Macc 6:1 "Not long after this"                                                                                                                                                              |
| Offer to fake eating                              | VERIFIED as text        | 6:21–22                                                                                                                                                                                                            |
| Motive: not to mislead the young                  | VERIFIED as text        | 6:24–25, 28                                                                                                                                                                                                        |

### Hebrews 11:35 and the Maccabean martyrs

- KJV Heb 11:35: "and others were tortured, not accepting deliverance; that they might obtain a better resurrection."
- Greek verb: **ἐτυμπανίσθησαν** (_etympanisthēsan_), from the same root as **τύμπανον** in 2 Macc 6:19, 28.
- **B. F. Westcott**, _The Epistle to the Hebrews: the Greek text with notes and essays_ (London 1889; 2nd ed. 1892), note on 11:35 (pp. 379–380): "The reference is to the martyrdom of the seven brethren related in 2 Macc. vi 18 ff ... vii." On "not accepting deliverance" he cites "2 Macc. vi. 21; vii. 27." On "better resurrection" he cites "2 Macc. vii. 9, 14." In his essay on Old Testament quotations (c. p. 473): "There are no quotations from the Apocryphal Books of the Greek Bible, though the incidents described in 2 Macc. vi, vii. are referred to (Hebr. xi. 35)." URL: https://archive.org/details/epistletohebrew00westgoog
- VERDICT: The link is **VERIFIED as a mainstream scholarly reading** (Westcott read directly). Modern commentaries (Attridge, Lane, Koester, deSilva) are widely said to agree, but I did not open them in this pass. Hebrews does not name Eleazar; the link is an allusion, not a quotation.

### Embellishments REJECTED

- **"Eleazar was a priest / high priest."** REJECTED for 2 Maccabees, which calls him a principal scribe (6:18). The priestly identification belongs to 4 Maccabees; do not mix the books.
- **"He was racked" / "broken on the wheel."** REJECTED as stated fact. The Greek _tympanon_ is uncertain in meaning (Westcott notes the Greek fathers "were at a loss as to its exact meaning"). 6:30 says "stripes" and "being beaten."
- **"Hebrews 11:35 names Eleazar."** REJECTED. Allusion only.

## Day 1 alternate B (thin): the priests who lived on figs and nuts (Josephus)

- Josephus, _Life_ §3 (§§13–14 in Niese numbering), tr. William Whiston: "certain priests of my acquaintance ... whom on a small and trifling occasion he [Felix] had put into bonds, and sent to Rome ... I was informed that they were not unmindful of piety towards God, even under their afflictions, but supported themselves with figs and nuts." Josephus sailed to Rome, was shipwrecked in the Adriatic, and through Poppaea obtained their release. Date: when Josephus was 26, so c. AD 63–64.
- URL: https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/2846
- VERDICT: text VERIFIED (self-report by Josephus). **That they ate figs and nuts to avoid unclean food is an INFERENCE.** Josephus does not say why. Whiston's footnote links it to Daniel 1:8–16, but that is the translator's comment. Use only with that caveat.

# DAY 2 — Birthright and blessing, received rather than earned

## Recommended: John Bunyan and "that scripture about Esau"

### Names and dates

- **John Bunyan** (1628–1688), nonconformist preacher, Bedfordshire.
- _Grace Abounding to the Chief of Sinners_, first published **London: George Larkin, 1666**. The 1666 title page says it was "written by his own hand there" (in prison) "and now published for the support of the weak and tempted People of God."
- Arrested **November 1660**; the text states he had then "preached ... about five years" and had lain in prison "complete twelve years" (1672 and later editions).
- The Esau crisis is undated in the text; he says it held him "for two years together" (§142 later numbering).

### Retelling (verified details only, 236 words)

John Bunyan wrote _Grace Abounding_ in prison, where he was held for leading unlawful religious meetings. In it he describes a morning when he lay in bed and a thought ran through his mind: "Sell Him, sell Him." He resisted, until at last he felt the words pass through his heart: "Let Him go, if He will." He went out into a field "like a man bereft of life."

Then a Bible verse seized him. It was Hebrews 12:16–17, about Esau, "who for one morsel of meat, sold his birthright," and afterward, "when he would have inherited the blessing, he was rejected; for he found no place of repentance, though he sought it carefully with tears." Bunyan writes that this verse lay on him "all day long, all the week long, yea, all the year long." He believed he had become Esau.

Relief came slowly. He studied the story in Genesis. He noticed that Esau's sale was deliberate and public, and that Esau later sought the blessing, not the birthright. Then one day, as he walked in a field, "suddenly this sentence fell upon my soul, Thy righteousness is in heaven." He saw by faith Jesus Christ at God's right hand. He understood that his righteousness was "Jesus Christ Himself," not his good or bad frame of heart. "Now did my chains fall off my legs indeed." At home he could not find those exact words in the Bible. He found their meaning in 1 Corinthians 1:30.

### Primary citations

- **First edition:** _Grace Abounding to the Chief of Sinners_, London: George Larkin, 1666. Esau verse seizes him: **§113**; "Thy righteousness is in heaven": **§183**; "chains fall off": **§184** (pp. 63–64).
  URL: https://archive.org/details/bim_early-english-books-1641-1700_grace-abounding-to-the-c_bunyan-john_1666
- **Standard later text** (enlarged editions; paragraph numbers most modern editions use): Religious Tract Society, 1905, transcribed as Project Gutenberg #654. "Sell Him": **§139**; Esau verse: **§141**; "for two years together": **§142**; "all the year long": **§145**; Esau reconsidered: **§§225–227**; "Thy righteousness is in heaven": **§229**; "chains fall off" and 1 Cor 1:30: **§230**; imprisonment: **§§318–319**.
  URL: https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/654

### Element verdicts

| Element                                                           | Verdict                | Evidence                                                                                                               |
| ----------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| "Sell Him" temptation; "Let Him go, if He will"                   | VERIFIED (self-report) | 1666 and 1905 texts                                                                                                    |
| Heb 12:16–17 on Esau seized him                                   | VERIFIED (self-report) | 1666 §113; 1905 §141                                                                                                   |
| Tormented "for two years together"                                | VERIFIED (self-report) | 1905 §142. Note §198 speaks of "well nigh two years and a half" for a related fresh assault. Do not merge the figures. |
| Reasoning that Esau sought the blessing, not the birthright       | VERIFIED (self-report) | 1905 §226; 1666 §180                                                                                                   |
| "Thy righteousness is in heaven" in a field                       | VERIFIED (self-report) | 1666 §183; 1905 §229                                                                                                   |
| Could not find that exact sentence in Scripture; found 1 Cor 1:30 | VERIFIED (self-report) | 1666 p. 64; 1905 §230                                                                                                  |
| Written in prison                                                 | VERIFIED               | 1666 title page and preface ("from the lions' dens")                                                                   |
| First published 1666                                              | VERIFIED               | 1666 imprint                                                                                                           |

### Embellishments REJECTED

- **Merging the Esau crisis with the game of "cat" (tip-cat).** REJECTED. The voice during the game ("Wilt thou leave thy sins and go to heaven...", §22) is a separate, earlier episode.
- **"Thy righteousness is in heaven" cited as a Bible verse.** REJECTED. Bunyan himself says he "could not find such a saying."
- **"Bunyan wrote this in the Bedford jail at the same time as Pilgrim's Progress."** REJECTED as imprecise. _Grace Abounding_ was printed in 1666; _The Pilgrim's Progress_ appeared in 1678. Do not link the two in time.
- **Paragraph numbers quoted without edition.** Numbers differ between 1666 (§113, §183) and later editions (§141, §229). Always name the edition.

### Why it fits

Bunyan read his own soul through Esau's lost birthright and found peace only when the blessing was located outside himself, "at God's right hand." That is the Jacob/Esau theme turned into grace received, not earned.

All at once I felt that I had been born again and entered into paradise itself through open gates ... This phrase of Paul was for me the very gate of paradise."

# DAY 3 — The torn veil and the unclean made clean

## Recommended: Father Damien (Jozef De Veuster) of Molokai

### Names and dates

- **Jozef De Veuster, SS.CC.**, religious name Damien. Born **3 January 1840**, Tremelo, Belgium. Arrived Kalaupapa/Kalawao, Molokai, **10 May 1873**. Leprosy announced **1884**. Died **15 April 1889**, aged 49. Beatified 4 June 1995 (Brussels); canonized 11 October 2009. (Vatican biography.)
- **Edward Clifford**, English painter (Anglican), visited Damien at Kalawao in **December 1888** and wrote an eyewitness account (1889).
- **Robert Louis Stevenson** visited the settlement after Damien's death ("eight days and seven nights"). Open letter dated **Sydney, 25 February 1890**, answering the Rev. **C. M. Hyde's** letter of **2 August 1889** to the Rev. H. B. Gage (printed in the Sydney _Presbyterian_, 26 October 1889).

### Retelling (verified details only, 244 words)

In May 1873 a Belgian priest named Damien went to the leprosy settlement on the island of Molokai, where the Hawaiian government sent people with the disease. Years later he described what he found. Many patients lived in shelters of branches and leaves. He himself "was sheltered during several weeks under the single pandanus-tree" in what became the churchyard.

He stayed sixteen years. The English painter Edward Clifford, who visited near the end, found that Damien asked his guests to sit at a separate table "as a precaution against infection." Clifford wrote that Damien had been "dressing the sufferers' sores, washing their bodies, visiting their deathbeds, and even digging their graves." In time Damien caught the disease himself. Clifford recorded his words: "Whenever I preach to my people, I do not say 'my brethren,' as you do, but 'we lepers.'" Looking at Clifford's sketch of him, Damien said, "What an ugly face! I did not know the disease had made such progress."

After Damien died in April 1889, a Protestant minister in Honolulu called him "a coarse, dirty man." Robert Louis Stevenson, a Protestant, answered in print. He did not pretend Damien was flawless. His own notes, gathered from Protestants who had opposed the priest, called Damien stubborn, untidy and hard to work with. Yet Stevenson concluded: "There is not a clean cup or towel in the Bishop-Home, but dirty Damien washed it."

### Primary citations

1. **Clifford, Edward.** _Father Damien: A Journey from Cashmere to his Home in Hawaii._ London: Macmillan, 1889. Chapter III, "Father Damien and Molokai": palm/pandanus tree p. 61; separate table pp. 61–62; "What an ugly face!" p. 63; Damien's own report on 1873 conditions pp. 70–71 ("sheltered during several weeks under the single pandanus-tree"); sores and graves p. 69; scalded foot and doctor pp. 88–89; "we lepers" p. 90.
   URL: https://archive.org/details/fatherdamienjour00clif
2. **Stevenson, Robert Louis.** _Father Damien: An Open Letter to the Reverend Dr. Hyde of Honolulu._ Dated Sydney, 25 February 1890. (Chatto & Windus impression used by Project Gutenberg #281.)
   URL: https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/281
3. **Vatican.** "St Jozef Damien De Veuster (1840–1889)," canonization biography, 11 October 2009.
   URL: https://www.vatican.va/news_services/liturgy/saints/2009/ns_lit_doc_20091011_de-veuster_en.html

### Element verdicts

| Element                                                             | Verdict                               | Evidence / caveat                                                                                                                                                                                                                     |
| ------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |

Clifford (from Damien's retelling): he spoke up at a chapel dedication on Maui and left that day. Compatible but not identical. Say "he volunteered." |

Stevenson says "slept that first night under a tree" (secondhand).                                                                                                                               |

It describes his habit when preaching, told to Clifford in 1888.                                                                                                                                                      |

Clifford gives no date and no circumstances.                                                                                                                                                                          |

The physician is usually identified as Eduard Arning; I did not verify that from a primary source. Use "by 1884."                                                                                                                                          |

Useful for honesty.                                                                                        |
| "There is not a clean cup or towel ... but dirty Damien washed it." | VERIFIED                              | Stevenson's letter                                                                                                                                                                                                                    |

### Embellishments checked and REJECTED

- **"One Sunday he opened his sermon with 'We lepers' and that is how his people learned he had the disease."** REJECTED as a dramatic single event. The only primary witness I found (Clifford) records it as his ongoing habit, in Damien's words to Clifford.
- **"Boiling water spilled on his feet while he was shaving / soaking his feet."** REJECTED as detail. Clifford says only "he once scalded himself in his foot."
- **"He went alone, chosen by no one."** REJECTED. The Vatican biography says four volunteered under Bishop Maigret.
- **Flawless-saint portrait.** REJECTED. Stevenson, his defender, lists real faults. Use them.
- **Clifford's birth year 1841.** REJECTED (Vatican: 1840).

### Theological link

Leviticus 13:45–46 required the leper to live "without the camp" and cry "Unclean, unclean." Damien chose to live inside that boundary, and at last to say "we." The day's torn-veil theme (Matt 27:51; Heb 10:19–20) can use this as a picture of access, not as proof.
