# DAY 4 — Romans 14: conscience without contempt

## Recommended: Polycarp and Anicetus — different days, one table (Rome, c. AD 155)

### Names and dates

- **Polycarp**, bishop of Smyrna, who "had always observed with John the disciple of our Lord" the Asian practice of keeping the Pascha on the fourteenth day (the Quartodeciman custom).
- **Anicetus**, bishop of Rome (mid-2nd century; conventionally c. 155–166). Polycarp's visit is usually dated c. 154–155 (conventional; not stated in the text).
- **Irenaeus of Lyons**, who as a youth had seen Polycarp. He wrote to **Victor**, bishop of Rome, in the 190s, when Victor tried to excommunicate the Asian churches over the same question.
- **Eusebius of Caesarea** preserves the letter in _Church History_ (early 4th century).

### Retelling (verified details only, 206 words)

In the middle of the second century, the churches disagreed about when to keep the Easter festival. The churches of Asia kept it on the fourteenth day of the Jewish month, as Polycarp of Smyrna said he had always done with John, the Lord's disciple. Rome kept it on Sunday.

Polycarp travelled to Rome while Anicetus was bishop there. Irenaeus, who had known Polycarp in his youth, later described the meeting. Neither man could persuade the other. Anicetus could not persuade Polycarp to give up what he had kept with John and the other apostles. Polycarp could not persuade Anicetus to give up the custom of the elders before him. "But though matters were in this shape, they communed together, and Anicetus conceded the administration of the eucharist in the church to Polycarp, manifestly as a mark of respect. And they parted from each other in peace."

Decades later, Victor of Rome tried to cut off the Asian churches over the same question. Irenaeus wrote to stop him. He reminded Victor that earlier Roman bishops had sent the Eucharist to churches that kept the other day. His summary of the matter is still sharp: "the disagreement in regard to the fast confirms the agreement in the faith."

### Primary citations

- **Eusebius, _Church History_ 5.24.9–18**, quoting Irenaeus's letter to Victor. Tr. A. C. McGiffert, _NPNF_ 2nd series vol. 1 (1890).
  URL: https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/250105.htm
- **Eusebius, _Church History_ 4.14.1** (Polycarp's conference with Anicetus "on a question concerning the day of the paschal feast").
  URL: https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/250104.htm
- **Irenaeus, _Against Heresies_ 3.3.4** (Polycarp "was in Rome in the time of Anicetus").
  URL: https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/0103303.htm

### Element verdicts

| Element                                                                                                     | Verdict                         | Evidence                                                                                                                                      |
| ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Polycarp visited Rome under Anicetus                                                                        | VERIFIED                        | Irenaeus AH 3.3.4; Eusebius HE 4.14.1 and 5.24.16                                                                                             |
| Disagreement about the paschal day                                                                          | VERIFIED                        | HE 4.14.1; 5.24.16                                                                                                                            |
| Neither persuaded the other                                                                                 | VERIFIED                        | HE 5.24.16                                                                                                                                    |
| They "communed together"; Anicetus "conceded the administration of the eucharist in the church to Polycarp" | VERIFIED (McGiffert tr.)        | HE 5.24.17. The exact sense of "conceded the eucharist" (preside vs. share) is a translation question; the fact of shared communion is clear. |
| "they disagreed a little about certain other things"                                                        | VERIFIED                        | HE 5.24.16. Irenaeus says the day was not their only difference.                                                                              |
| Parted in peace                                                                                             | VERIFIED                        | HE 5.24.17                                                                                                                                    |
| Victor tried to excommunicate the Asian churches; Irenaeus rebuked him                                      | VERIFIED                        | HE 5.24.9–11                                                                                                                                  |
| "the disagreement in regard to the fast confirms the agreement in the faith"                                | VERIFIED                        | HE 5.24.13                                                                                                                                    |
| Date c. 155                                                                                                 | VERIFIED as conventional dating | Not in text                                                                                                                                   |
| Whether Victor's excommunication took effect                                                                | DISPUTED                        | Text: he "attempted to cut off" and "declared ... excommunicate." Outcome not stated here.                                                    |

### Embellishments REJECTED

- **"Polycarp and Anicetus 'agreed to disagree.'"** REJECTED as a quotation. The phrase is not in the text. (Wesley uses it in 1770; see Day 5.)
- **"Anicetus adopted Polycarp's date" or the reverse.** REJECTED. The text says neither persuaded the other.
- **"Polycarp was martyred in Rome."** REJECTED. He died in Smyrna (Irenaeus says only that he died "a glorious and most illustrious martyr's death"; the place is from the _Martyrdom of Polycarp_, not opened here).

### Why it fits

Romans 14:5–6: "One man esteemeth one day above another: another esteemeth every day alike ... He that regardeth the day, regardeth it unto the Lord." This is a documented case of exactly that division, handled without contempt at the Lord's Table.

## Day 4 alternate: Eric Liddell, Paris 1924

### Names and dates

- **Eric Henry Liddell**, born **16 January 1902**, Tientsin (Tianjin), China; died **21 February 1945** in Weihsien internment camp.
- 1924 Paris Olympics, Stade de Colombes: **100 m** round one and quarter-finals **Sunday 6 July** (14:30, 16:00); semi-finals and final **Monday 7 July** (Olympedia).
- **200 m**: 8–9 July 1924; Liddell **bronze** (21.9) behind Jackson Scholz and Charley Paddock.
- **400 m**: 10–11 July 1924; final **Friday 11 July**; Liddell **gold**, 47.6.

### Retelling (verified details only, 176 words)

Eric Liddell was Scotland's fastest sprinter and a committed Christian. When the programme for the 1924 Paris Olympics was announced, the preliminary rounds of the 100 metres fell on a Sunday. Liddell decided not to run that event. He knew this months before the Games, and he trained instead for the longer 200 and 400 metres.

On Sunday 6 July 1924, while the 100-metre heats were run at Colombes, the Scots Kirk in Paris records that Liddell preached there. That week he took the bronze medal in the 200 metres. On Friday 11 July, drawn in the outside lane, he led the 400-metre final from the start and won in 47.6 seconds, a new Olympic record.

Nineteen years later he was interned by the Japanese at Weihsien in China. A fellow internee, Norman Cliff, remembered that boys there already knew him as "the man who refused to run on a Sunday at the Olympics." Cliff also remembered that Liddell organised sport for the camp's young people and taught their science classes without a blackboard.

### Sources used

- Olympedia, "Eric Liddell": https://www.olympedia.org/athletes/69245 ("When the program was announced for the Paris Olympic Games, Liddell immediately forsook any idea of running the 100 metres as the preliminaries were scheduled to be held on a Sunday.")
- Olympedia, 100 m 1924: https://www.olympedia.org/results/57285 (dates and times; notes the boat scene "was fabricated" and that he "knew about the Olympic schedule several months in advance").
- Olympedia, 200 m 1924: https://www.olympedia.org/results/57315
- Olympedia, 400 m 1924: https://www.olympedia.org/results/57345 ("In real life, Liddell knew about the 1924 Olympic schedule for six months").
- Scots Kirk Paris, "Eric Liddell Commemorations": http://www.scotskirkparis.com/eric-liddell-commemorations.html (2024 plaque "commemorating the centenary of Eric Liddell's preaching in the Scots Kirk Paris on 6th July 1924").
- Norman Cliff, "Eric Liddell in Weihsien Camp 1943–1945": https://www.weihsien-paintings.org/NormanCliff/people/individuals/Eric01/txt_personal.htm
- NOTE: Olympedia is a scholarly database compiled from official reports; it is not the 1924 Official Report itself. I could not open 1924 newspapers, D. P. Thomson's 1945 biography, or Jarvie et al., "Eric Henry Liddell 100 Years on from 1924," _Asian Journal of Sport History & Culture_ 3:2 (2024) (Cloudflare blocked the PDF). Those are the next sources to check.

| -------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Knew the schedule months ahead; did not enter 100 m because heats were on Sunday | VERIFIED (scholarly database)    | Olympedia athlete and event pages. Exact month (e.g. "November 1923," World Athletics) UNVERIFIED.                                                                                                         |
| 100 m heats/quarters Sunday 6 July; final Monday 7 July                          | VERIFIED                         | Olympedia 57285                                                                                                                                                                                            |

| Outside lane in 400 m final                                                      | VERIFIED (database)              | Olympedia athlete bio                                                                                                                                                                                      |
| 47.6 was a world record                                                          | DISPUTED                         | Olympedia event page marks "WR"; Olympedia athlete bio says "Olympic, European and British record"; World Athletics notes the record was later set aside for an earlier faster mark. Say "Olympic record." |

| He relaxed his rule and refereed games on Sunday in Weihsien                     | DISPUTED                         | Cliff: "Some former Weihsieners have stated that ... Liddell organised sports on Sundays ... I have no recollection of this, and so cannot confirm or deny this statement."                                |

### Chariots of Fire and popular embellishments REJECTED

- **Learned of the Sunday heats while boarding the boat to France.** REJECTED. Olympedia: "this was fabricated."
- **The 100 m _final_ was on Sunday.** REJECTED. Final was Monday 7 July.
- **"I believe God made me for a purpose, but He also made me fast. And when I run, I feel His pleasure."** REJECTED as a Liddell quotation. No 1924-era source found; it is screenplay dialogue. Do not quote it as his words.
- **"...since I have been a young lad, I have had my eyes on a different prize..."** (quoted on the Scots Kirk page). REJECTED for use. No source given; no primary located.
- **"I run the first 200m as hard as I can. Then, for the second 200m, with God's help, I run harder."** UNVERIFIED. World Athletics prints it without a source. Do not use.
- **Jackson Scholz handed him the "Them that honour me I will honour" (1 Sam 2:30) note.** REJECTED as film version. The common correction (a British team masseur) is itself UNVERIFIED in any primary I could open. Omit the note entirely, or say "a note is said to have been given him."
- **Lord Lindsay gave up his 400 m place; the Prince of Wales pressed him to run.** UNVERIFIED and widely described as film invention. Not in Olympedia. Omit.
- **Liddell condemned those who ran on Sunday.** No evidence either way was found. Do not invent an attitude. Olympedia and Cliff describe him as friendly to all.

# DAY 5 — Brothers reconciled, burdens carried

## Recommended: John and Charles Wesley reconciled at Leeds, 5 October 1749, with George Whitefield as peacemaker

### Names and dates

- **John Wesley** (1703–1791) and his younger brother **Charles Wesley** (1707–1788), leaders of the Methodist revival.
- **George Whitefield** (1714–1770), evangelist, a Calvinist who split with the Wesleys over predestination.
- **John Nelson**, Methodist lay preacher. **John Bennet**, Methodist preacher. **Grace Murray**, widow and Methodist worker.
- **28 March 1741 (Sat.)**: Whitefield tells John Wesley they preach "two different gospels" and refuses him "the right hand of fellowship."
- **3 October 1749 (Tue.)**: Grace Murray marries John Bennet at Newcastle, after Charles Wesley's intervention.
- **4–5 October 1749 (Wed.–Thu.)**: Leeds. Whitefield mediates between John and Charles.
- **8 October 1749**: Charles writes that "G. W., and my brother, and I, are one."
- **22 March 1770**: Whitefield signs his will in Georgia. He dies **30 September 1770**.
- **18 November 1770 (Sun.)**: John Wesley preaches Whitefield's funeral sermon in London.

(All dates Old Style, as the sources give them. Britain changed calendar in 1752.)

### Retelling (verified details only, 247 words)

In 1741 George Whitefield told John Wesley to his face that they preached "two different gospels." He refused to give Wesley "the right hand of fellowship" and said he would preach against John and his brother.

Eight years later it was the Wesley brothers who were divided. John believed that Grace Murray, a widow in the Methodist work, was pledged to marry him. Charles believed she was promised to the preacher John Bennet, and he acted to see her married to Bennet. John rode to Leeds on 4 October 1749 at Whitefield's urging. He found Whitefield waiting, not Charles. Whitefield "wept and prayed over me," John wrote.

The next morning news came that Grace had married Bennet. Charles arrived an hour later. John "did not desire to see him. But Mr. Whitefield constrained me." Charles said: "I renounce all intercourse with you, but what I would have with an heathen man or a publican." Then, John wrote, "Mr. Whitefield and John Nelson burst into tears. They prayed, cried, and entreated, till the storm passed away. We could not speak, but only fell on each other's neck." When Bennet came in, "Neither of us could speak, but we kissed each other and wept."

Three days later Charles wrote to a friend that Whitefield "and my brother, and I, are one, a threefold cord." At Leeds, he added, John "gave honest George the right hand of fellowship." In 1770 Whitefield left the brothers mourning rings "in token of my indissoluble union with them."

### Primary citations

1. **John Wesley, _Journal_, Saturday 28 March 1741.** In _The Journal of the Rev. John Wesley_, Standard Edition, ed. Nehemiah Curnock, vol. 2 (London 1909), pp. 439–440.
   URL: https://archive.org/details/a613690402wesluoft
   Verbatim: "He told me he and I preached two different gospels, and therefore he not only would not join with, or give me the right hand of fellowship, but was resolved publicly to preach against me and my brother, wheresoever he preached at all."
2. **John Wesley's private manuscript narrative on Grace Murray** (the "Grace Murray MS", British Museum), printed by Curnock alongside the published Journal, vol. 3 (London 1909), pp. 436–439 (entries for 2–5 October 1749).
   URL: https://archive.org/details/a613690403wesluoft
   Verbatim (p. 439): "Thur. 5. — About eight one came in from Newcastle, and told us, 'They were married on Tuesday.' My brother came an hour after. I felt no anger, yet I did not desire to see him. But Mr. Whitefield constrained me. After a few words had passed, he accosted me with, 'I renounce all intercourse with you, but what I would have with an heathen man or a publican.' I felt little emotion. It was only adding a drop of water to a drowning man, yet I calmly accepted his renunciation, and acquiesced therein. Poor Mr. Whitefield and John Nelson burst into tears. They prayed, cried, and entreated, till the storm passed away. We could not speak, but only fell on each other's neck. J[ohn] B[ennet] then came in. Neither of us could speak, but we kissed each other and wept. Soon after I talked with my brother alone. He seemed utterly amazed. He clearly saw I was not what he thought, and now blamed her only..."
3. **Charles Wesley to Ebenezer Blackwell, Sheffield, Sunday 8 October 1749.** In _The Journal of the Rev. Charles Wesley_, ed. Thomas Jackson, vol. 2 (London 1849), "Selections from the Correspondence," letter X, p. 178.
   URL: https://archive.org/details/journaletctowhi01weslgoog
   Verbatim: "G. W., and my brother, and I, are one, a threefold cord which shall no more be broken ... At Leeds we met my brother, who gave honest George the right hand of fellowship, and attended him everywhere to our Societies."
4. **Whitefield's will**, signed at the Orphan-house Academy, Georgia, 22 March 1770, with N.B. In John Gillies, _Memoirs of the Life of the Reverend George Whitefield_ (London: Dilly, 1772), pp. 347–355.
   URL: https://archive.org/details/memoirsoflifeof00gill
   Verbatim (p. 355): "N. B. I also leave a mourning ring to my honoured and dear friends and disinterested fellow-labourers, the Rev. Messrs. John and Charles Wesley, in token of my indissoluble union with them, in heart and christian affection, notwithstanding our difference in judgment about some particular points of doctrine."
5. **John Wesley, _A Sermon on the Death of the Rev. Mr George Whitefield_, preached at the chapel in Tottenham-Court-Road and at the Tabernacle near Moorfields, on Sunday, November 18, 1770** (London 1770), §III.1, p. 23.
   URL: https://archive.org/details/sermonondeathofr00wesliala
   Verbatim: "There are many doctrines of a less essential nature, with regard to which, even the sincere children of GOD ... are and have been divided for many ages. In these we may think and let think; we may 'agree to disagree.' But mean time let us hold fast the essentials of the faith."
6. **John Wesley, _Journal_, 10 November 1770**, Curnock vol. 5, p. 396: "had the melancholy news of Mr. Whitefield's death confirmed by his executors, who desired me to preach his funeral sermon on Sunday the 18th."
   URL: https://archive.org/details/a613690405wesluoft

B. to marry him—since which, you engaged yourself to another." The detailed ride narrative in that note is Curnock's summary (SECONDARY). Keep the retelling brief and fair to Charles. |

Reported by John, not by Charles.                                                                                                                                                                                                        |

W., and my brother, and I, are one"                            | VERIFIED                                      | Letter, 8 Oct 1749                                                                                                                                                                                                                               |

Only Curnock's editorial note says Whitefield left "a request." Say "at the request of Whitefield's executors."                                                                            |
| Whitefield died 30 Sept 1770 at Newburyport                                 | VERIFIED (editorial note)                     | Curnock vol. 5 note; widely standard                                                                                                                                                                                                             |

### Embellishments checked and REJECTED

- **"Will we see John Wesley in heaven?" — "No; he will be so near the throne..."** (attributed to Whitefield). REJECTED. Not in Gillies 1772, not in Wesley's 1770 sermon, not in the Journal passages read. No primary source located.
- **"Whitefield requested in his will that Wesley preach his funeral."** REJECTED as stated. The will as printed by Gillies contains the ring bequest, not a funeral request. Wesley says the executors asked.
- **"John's published Journal records the Leeds quarrel."** REJECTED. The published entry for 5 October 1749 only says Whitefield preached. The quarrel is in John's private manuscript, printed by Curnock in 1909.
- **Casting Charles as a villain.** REJECTED. The sources show Charles acting on his own belief that Grace was promised to Bennet and that the marriage would harm the work. John's account is one-sided and should be labelled as his.

### Why it fits

Genesis 33:4: "Esau ran to meet him, and embraced him, and fell on his neck, and kissed him: and they wept." John Wesley's private words — "fell on each other's neck ... kissed each other and wept" — follow the same pattern, documented in his own hand. Whitefield, once estranged from John, carries the burden of the two brothers in tears and prayer (Gal 6:2). The 1770 will and sermon show the union outlasting a doctrinal difference that never went away (Rom 14).

## Day 5 alternate: Corrie ten Boom and the former Ravensbrück guard (self-report; tellings differ)

### Names and dates

- **Cornelia (Corrie) ten Boom** (15 April 1892 – 15 April 1983), Haarlem. Arrested 1944 with her sister **Betsie**, who died at Ravensbrück.
- Meeting: a church in **Munich**. _Guideposts_ gives **1947**.

### Retelling (from the 1972 telling, labelled as hers, 190 words)

Corrie ten Boom said that the meeting took place after she spoke in a church in Munich in 1947, where she had come with the message that God forgives. As people filed out, a heavy, balding man in a grey overcoat came forward. She recognised him as a guard from Ravensbrück, the camp where her sister Betsie had died. In her 1972 account he said, "I was a guard in there ... But since that time, I have become a Christian ... will you forgive me?" She stood unable to move. She wrote that she prayed silently: "Jesus, help me! I can lift my hand. I can do that much. You supply the feeling." She put out her hand. She described warmth that seemed to pass down her arm into their joined hands. "I forgive you, brother!" she said. "With all my heart!" In her earlier 1971 book the same scene is shorter. There the man only thanks her for the message, and she prays, "Jesus, I cannot forgive him. Give me Your forgiveness." In both tellings, the love she felt came as she took his hand.

### Primary citations

- **Ten Boom, Corrie, with John and Elizabeth Sherrill.** _The Hiding Place._ Chosen Books, 1971. Passage as excerpted from the 35th Anniversary Edition (Grand Rapids: Chosen, 2006), pp. 247–248.
  Excerpt URL: https://renovare.org/articles/give-me-your-forgiveness
  Full book (lending only, not readable without borrowing): https://archive.org/details/hidingplace0000tenb
- **Ten Boom, Corrie.** "I'm Still Learning to Forgive." _Guideposts_, November 1972. Reprinted as "Guideposts Classics: Corrie ten Boom on Forgiveness."
  URL: https://guideposts.org/positive-living/guideposts-classics-corrie-ten-boom-forgiveness/
- **_Tramp for the Lord_** (1974) also tells it. NOT READ in this pass (archive.org lending only).

### How the two tellings differ

| Detail                            | _The Hiding Place_ (1971)                                                                                 | _Guideposts_ (Nov 1972)                                                                                                                              |
| --------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Year                              | not given in the excerpt                                                                                  | 1947                                                                                                                                                 |
| Setting                           | "a church service in Munich"                                                                              | church in Munich; "the basement room where I had just spoken"                                                                                        |
| Who he was                        | "a former S.S. man who had stood guard at the shower room door in the processing center at Ravensbruck"   | "a guard at Ravensbrück"; she recalls "a blue uniform and a visored cap with its skull and crossbones" and "the leather crop swinging from his belt" |
| What he says                      | "How grateful I am for your message, Fraulein ... To think that, as you say, He has washed my sins away!" | "You mentioned Ravensbrück in your talk ... I was a guard in there ... I have become a Christian ... will you forgive me?"                           |
| Does he ask forgiveness?          | No                                                                                                        | Yes                                                                                                                                                  |
| Her first reaction                | "kept my hand at my side"                                                                                 | "fumbled in my pocketbook rather than take that hand"                                                                                                |
| Her prayer                        | "Jesus, I cannot forgive him. Give me Your forgiveness."                                                  | "Jesus, help me! I can lift my hand. I can do that much. You supply the feeling."                                                                    |
| Words of forgiveness spoken aloud | none given                                                                                                | "I forgive you, brother! With all my heart!"                                                                                                         |
| Common to both                    | the current from shoulder down arm through the hand; love or warmth flooding her                          | same                                                                                                                                                 |

### Verdicts

- The meeting, place (Munich), her struggle, and the hand-clasp: **VERIFIED (self-report)**, consistent across both tellings.
- Year 1947: **VERIFIED (self-report, Guideposts only)**.
- His words, his request for forgiveness, and her spoken reply: **DISPUTED** — present in 1972, absent in 1971. If used, quote one telling and name it.
- **REJECTED:** that the man was the guard who beat or killed Betsie (neither telling says so); that he was named (neither telling names him); any claim he "remembered her" (Guideposts: "No, he did not remember me").

# EXTRA — Genesis 3:15 candidate: the crucified man from Giv'at ha-Mivtar

### Names and dates

- **Yehohanan**, adult male, Jerusalem, Second Temple period.
- Found **late 1968** in **Tomb I, Chamber B, Ossuary no. 4**, Giv'at ha-Mivtar, north-east Jerusalem, by **Vassilios Tzaferis** (Israel Department of Antiquities and Museums).
- Original reports: **Tzaferis 1970**, IEJ 20: 18–32 (tombs); **J. Naveh 1970**, IEJ 20: 33–37 (inscriptions); **N. Haas 1970**, IEJ 20: 38–59 (skeletal remains).
- Popular report: **Tzaferis 1985**, "Crucifixion — The Archaeological Evidence," _Biblical Archaeology Review_ 11:1 (Jan/Feb 1985): 44–53. It follows Haas.
- Correction: **Joseph Zias and Eliezer Sekeles 1985**, "The Crucified Man from Giv'at ha-Mivtar: A Reappraisal," _Israel Exploration Journal_ 35: 22–27.

### What I read

- Zias and Sekeles 1985, full text (10-page scan): [scan; link removed — cite IEJ 35 (1985) 22-27]
- Tzaferis 1985 BAR, full text as reprinted by the Biblical Archaeology Society: https://www.biblicalarchaeology.org/daily/biblical-topics/crucifixion/a-tomb-in-jerusalem-reveals-the-history-of-crucifixion-and-roman-crucifixion-methods/
- **Not read directly:** Tzaferis 1970, Haas 1970, Naveh 1970, Yadin 1973 (IEJ, JSTOR-restricted). Their content below comes from how Tzaferis 1985 and Zias–Sekeles 1985 report and cite them (with their page numbers).

| **Olive-wood plaque:** wood fragments **beneath the nail head** identified as **olive** by scanning electron microscope (Dept. of Botany, Hebrew University; Dr Ella Werker) | VERIFIED                                | Z&S §IV                                                                                                                                                                           |
| Wood at the **tip** of the nail: "too minute to be further specified"                                                                                                        | VERIFIED                                | Z&S §IV                                                                                                                                                                           |
| Plaque's likely purpose: to stop the foot being pulled free over the nail head                                                                                               | VERIFIED as Z&S inference               | Z&S Conclusions                                                                                                                                                                   |
| Bent tip probably struck a knot; family could not remove the nail without destroying the heel, so it stayed                                                                  | VERIFIED as inference (Haas, Kuhn, Z&S) | Z&S Conclusions                                                                                                                                                                   |
| Ossuary inscription: **"Yehohanan son of HGQWL"** (_Yhwhnn bn hgqwl_); father's name unclear                                                                                 | VERIFIED as reported                    | Tzaferis 1985 (citing Naveh 1970)                                                                                                                                                 |
| Ossuary also held a child's bones and a right cuboid from a third individual                                                                                                 | VERIFIED                                | Tzaferis 1985 (child, 3–4 yrs); Z&S §II (third adult)                                                                                                                             |
| Tomb dated by pottery c. late 2nd century BC to AD 70; bulk after 37 BC                                                                                                      | VERIFIED as reported                    | Tzaferis 1985                                                                                                                                                                     |
| Remains were reburied soon after study                                                                                                                                       | VERIFIED                                | Tzaferis 1985 ("four weeks"); Z&S intro ("reburied in accordance with the request of the religious authorities")                                                                  |
| Age 24–28; height about 167 cm                                                                                                                                               | VERIFIED as Haas's figures only         | Tzaferis 1985. Z&S did not re-examine these. Label as original report.                                                                                                            |

### Haas 1970 / Tzaferis 1985 claims that Zias and Sekeles REJECTED

| Original claim                                                            | Correction (Z&S 1985)                                                                                                                                                                   |
| ------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| One nail pierced **both** heels together, plus a plaque                   | REJECTED. The "left heel" fragment was a piece of long bone; no left calcaneum was present; an 11.5 cm nail could not pass through two heels and a plaque into the upright (§I, §VIII). |
| Plaque of **acacia or pistacia**; upright of olive                        | REJECTED. Wood under the nail head is **olive**; wood at the tip unidentifiable (§IV).                                                                                                  |
| A **nail through the forearm**, shown by a scratch on the right radius    | REJECTED. Such scratches are common and non-traumatic; similar marks on the fibula (§III). Z&S infer the arms were probably **tied** (Conclusions).                                     |
| **Legs broken** by a _coup de grâce_                                      | REJECTED. Evidence inconclusive; bones mis-sided; breaks at different angles and post-mortem (§VI).                                                                                     |
| Feet **amputated** to remove the body (cut talus)                         | REJECTED. No cut marks; talus may belong to the third individual (§V).                                                                                                                  |
| Legs together and twisted, body on a _sedile_ supporting the left buttock | REJECTED. Haas's reconstructions "technically and anatomically impossible" (§VIII). Z&S propose he straddled the upright, each foot nailed to its side — itself a hypothesis.           |
| **Cleft palate** and missing canine (basis of Tzaferis's face portrait)   | REJECTED. Six dental specialists at Johns Hopkins found no cleft palate; the canine is visible (§IX).                                                                                   |

### What the find does and does not tell us about Jesus

**It does tell us:**

- Iron nails were driven through the heel in at least one Roman-period crucifixion at Jerusalem.
- A crucified man could receive family burial in a rock-cut tomb and later an ossuary. (The wider claim that this supports the Gospel burial accounts is a scholarly inference; I did not verify a specific source for it in this pass.)

**It does not tell us:**

- How Jesus's hands or feet were fixed, how many nails were used, or where they went. John 20:25 speaks of nail prints in his hands; Luke 24:39–40 shows his hands and feet. The Giv'at ha-Mivtar bone neither confirms nor contradicts either.
- Anything about leg-breaking at Jesus's crucifixion (John 19:31–33). The earlier "confirmation" of leg-breaking has been withdrawn.
- The position of the body, the shape of the cross, or its wood.
- Yehohanan's crime or exact date.
- Any link to Genesis 3:15 beyond the word "heel." That connection is homiletic. Say so plainly if it is used.

### Popular errors still in circulation — REJECTED

- The Biblical Archaeology Society's own introduction to the reprint (dated January 2026) still says "Both of his feet had been nailed together," "His arm bones revealed scratches where the nails had passed between," and "Both legs were badly fractured." All three are the corrected Haas errors. Do not cite that introduction.
- Tzaferis 1985: "the only victim of crucifixion ever discovered." Out of date. Later finds are reported from Gavello, Italy, and Fenstanton, England. UNVERIFIED here; say "the first."
- Tzaferis 1985 says "Alexander Jannaeus crucified 800 Jews on a single day during the revolt against the census of 7 A.D." This conflates Jannaeus (1st century BC) with AD 7. Do not repeat.
- "Nail 17–18 cm / 7 inches." REJECTED (11.5 cm).

# 6. 05 - Video verification: Law and Freedom devotional series

Run: 2026-09-13 02:45 UTC (2026-09-12 local). All checks were run live with curl.
Raw verification output: `verify_IJ-FekWUZzE.json` (candidates) and `verify_UnpvtNEoOFo.json` (rejected mirrors), in this folder.

## Method (as specified)

- **A. oEmbed:** `https://www.youtube.com/oembed?url=https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=<ID>&format=json`. Every candidate returned HTTP 200. Title and author_name below are copied from that response.
- **B. Embed:** `curl -s -H 'Referer: https://euangelion.app/' -A 'Mozilla/5.0' 'https://www.youtube.com/embed/<ID>'`. Each `ytcfg.set({...})` block was JSON-parsed. `PLAYER_VARS.embedded_player_response` (a JSON-escaped string) was unescaped, then `previewPlayabilityStatus` was read. PASS = `status: "OK"` and `playableInEmbed: true`.
  - **Control:** the same parser with no Referer on IJ-FekWUZzE returned `{"status": "ERROR", "reason": "Video player configuration error"}`. This confirms the known false error, and it shows the parser does report non-OK states.
- **C. Duration and content:** `lengthSeconds` and `shortDescription` come from `ytInitialPlayerResponse` on the watch page. Chapters were used where present. Where the description was thin, the official bibleproject.com or gospelinlife.com page was checked.

### Raw status objects seen

- **Type S (standard):** `{"status": "OK", "playableInEmbed": true, "contextParams": "Q0FFU0FnZ0E="}`
- **Type L:** the same object plus `"liveStreamability": {"liveStreamabilityRenderer": {"videoId": "<ID>", "pollDelayMs": "15000", "displayEndscreen": true}}`. This is the end-screen poll renderer. It does not mean the video is a live stream, and all Type L videos are normal VOD with fixed lengths.

> 44 For I am the LORD your God; consecrate yourselves, therefore, and be holy, because I am holy. You must not defile yourselves by any creature that crawls along the ground.
